1. The first check demanded "some styling". The recorded program printed plain text, so it failed
   on a page that was right. **A session may legitimately carry no colour.**
2. The right invariant is equality with the reader's own document, not a floor — but equality
   between 0 and 0 proves nothing.
3. So the recorded program now prints in colour, and the comparison is meaningful: **1,127 styled
   spans on both sides.**

The check also fails if a missing model is accepted quietly, so the flag cannot regress into
ignoring its argument the way `page --all` once did.
## The second axis is now checked every time

`fields` joined `check-all`, which now reports every dimension in one pass:

```
  apps              100.0%  over 14 frame(s)
  nongrok           100.0%  over 8 frame(s)
  structured        100.0%  over 6 frame(s)
  tui               100.0%  over 57 frame(s)
  unseen            100.0%  over 73 frame(s)
  workflow          100.0%  over 4 frame(s)
  assembly          label path given the reader's own answers: 100.0% over 181 frame(s)
  provenance        provenance: 683 component(s), 336 carried, 0 contradiction(s)
  fields            fields: 683 component(s) compared, 0 differing
  round trip        0 session(s) below 100%
every check passed
```

Four axes now, each with a number: **kind and text** (`score`), **role, tabs, shortcuts,
properties and spans** (`fields`), **provenance**, and **the round trip**. Until this round the
first was the only one anybody looked at between releases, which is how a model-built diff lost its
colour while the report said 100%.

A check nobody runs is a document. This one runs with the others, in six minutes, on every change.
## The loop a person uses had never been closed

`tui` — the reverse direction — had no `--model`, so page-to-terminal was only ever measured on
the **reader's** pages. The path a person actually takes, model judges the screen and the page
draws back, was untested from end to end.

| screen | reader page | model page |
|---|---|---|
| vim, vertical split | 89/89 | **89/89** |
| real `git diff` | 10/10 | **10/10** |
| tmux popup | 25/25 | **25/25** |
| failing test run | 563/563 | **563/563** |

Identical, on four screens of four different shapes. `tui --model` exists now, and `check-all`
measures both round trips — `round trip` and `round trip/model` — so neither can rot silently.

That closes the last loop the product claims and nobody had walked: screen → model → page → screen.
## v0.5.0, and the download does what the release notes imply

Cut and verified by downloading it:

```
checking release: v0.5.0
archive: 1,709 KB, capture: True, model shipped: True
downloaded build, on a real session: model 100.0% floor 100.0% over 1 frame(s)
the download works the way a stranger would use it
```

Everything this month added is reachable from the archive — the two newest commands included:

| command | result from the download |
|---|---|
| `fields` | 13 component(s) compared, 0 differing |
| `tui --model` | round trip: 63/63 line(s) of content came back (100.0%) |

Linux, macOS and Windows assets are all present, built in one pass.

**What a release verifies that a repository cannot:** that the thing someone downloads is the thing
that was tested. Three rounds this month were spent on defects visible only from outside the working
tree — a missing model in the archive, a flag one command did not have, a default that resolved to
nothing. `check-release` now walks that path on demand, against the tag that was actually published.
## Trying to earn ADR 0004 a screen, and not getting one

The ADR argues panes should hold components. To decide it needs a screen that *demands* it — a
split where a pane holds something structured, like a diff. Two attempts:

1. **Keystrokes into a live tmux session** (split, run `git diff` in one pane, `ls` in the other).
   Eight frames, none containing the diff: the keys reached the shell before the split existed.
2. **A detached session with two commands, then attach.** One frame, no content — the attach did
   not survive the recorder's pty.

So there is still no screen showing structure inside a pane, and the ADR stays a proposal.

**That is the correct state, and it is worth saying why.** The evidence for the ADR is currently
*one* screen — a popup over two panes, where the split vanishes because the pane rule only runs on
single-block screens. That proves panes are fragile, not that they need children. Building the
container now would be spending a day of schema work on a need I have argued rather than observed.

The capture work itself is recorded as a limitation: driving a multiplexer through keystrokes to set
up a *specific* pane layout is not something the recorder does reliably, and two rounds have now
spent effort on it. The next attempt should compose the screen some other way — a program that draws
two panes itself — rather than fight the input path.
## The live view's memory and latency, neither of which had been looked at

The viewer re-reads the whole recording on every poll, which is the kind of design that is fine
until it is not. Measured rather than assumed:

| session | frames | `/state` median | max | resident |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| live, 200 frames recorded underneath it | 200 | — | — | **5.7–6.9 MB**, no trend |
| a session repeated to order | 1,500 | **18 ms** | 95 ms | **10.1 MB** |
| the same recording small | 40 | 19 ms | 110 ms | 5.8 MB |

**Latency does not move between 40 and 1,500 frames, and memory does not grow while watching a
recording produce two hundred of them.** Re-reading the file each poll is affordable at the sizes
this project sees, and the measurement is recorded so nobody has to guess where that stops being
true.

That is the last performance question the live path had, and the answer is the boring one — which is
what a measured answer usually is.
## Panes carry their styling, after four attempts and the right order

A split screen's panes had no colour at all: a `vimdiff` window drew its changes in red and green
and the page held plain text. Four attempts, the first three reverted:

| attempt | result |
|---|---|
| slice each pane's spans (reader only) | worked — 25 of 26 lines per pane — but the graft gave one pane the other's styling |
| fold panes into the row-keyed copy | 12 differing, because two panes claim the same rows |
| add the grouping field first (round 87) | landed, inert, JSON byte-identical |
| **slice the spans, then copy panes by id** | **0 differing, 688 components** |

The order was the whole problem. Panes share ids with their reader counterparts — both pages derive
the id from the pane's own text — so they must be copied by id and **skipped** by the row-keyed
pass, which cannot tell two panes apart.

Verified across every axis afterwards: all evaluation sets 100%, assembly 185 frames, provenance 688
components with 0 contradictions, fields 688 with 0 differing, and both round trips at 100%.
## A field that was never used, and a release that carries the fix

The `panes` grouping added in round 87 was **dead schema**: the styling fix ended up copying panes by
id, so nothing ever populated it. It was removed rather than shipped as an unused claim — five
literals, all mechanical, `fields` unchanged at 0 differing.

**v0.6.0** carries both changes and was verified by downloading it:

```
checking release: v0.6.0
archive: 1,712 KB, capture: True, model shipped: True
the download works the way a stranger would use it
```

And the fix is in the archive rather than only in the working tree:

| check on the downloaded build | result |
|---|---|
| `vimdiff` pane styling | 25, 25 styled lines |
| `panes` key in JSON | absent, as intended |

**What a release keeps honest:** every claim in this report is verified twice — once where it was
written, and once from the thing a person downloads.
## The pane fix now has a unit test, and the test has teeth

`crates/pages/tests/panes.rs` builds two pages by hand: a reader's with two panes carrying different
styling on the same rows, and a judged page with the same panes and none. It calls `graft_spans` and
requires each pane to come back with its own colour — and requires the two not to be equal, because
"both panes have styling" is exactly what a wrong-by-row copy produces.

The test was mutation-checked rather than trusted: making the id match accept anything fails it, and
restoring the match passes. **A test that cannot fail is decoration**, and this one can.
## The diff fix has a test too

`crates/pages/tests/diff_spans.rs` does for diffs what the pane test does for panes: a reader page
whose removal and addition carry different colours, a judged page whose lines carry none, then
`graft_spans` — and the assertions require **each line to keep its own colour and the two not to be
the same**. Mutation-checked: emptying the diff's spans fails the test; restoring the graft passes.

Together with `panes.rs` the two defects found by the `fields` axis — a diff with no styling, and two
panes sharing one — are now pinned by tests that fail when the fix is removed. **The axis found them;
the tests keep them found.**
## The third binary: shipped in every release, never run outside its own measurement

`ui-lm-validate` compares a recording against a draw-buffer dump from the application's own
renderer. It has 26 unit tests and appears in every archive — and **nothing exercised the command**,
because the dump it reads came from a throwaway fork that is not in this repository.

The dump format is four fields and an array, so a dump can be written by hand. Done, against a
program that prints `abc` and waits:

```
matching dump:   K5: the emulator's screen and the draw buffer agree on all 400 cell(s)
one wrong cell:  K5: the emulator's screen and the draw buffer disagree on 1 of 400 cell(s)
```

`scripts/check-validate.ps1` now runs both: agreement must exit 0, and a single wrong cell must exit
1 with the cell named. **A validator that cannot fail is an expensive way of printing "OK"**, and the
second half of that check is the half that makes the first mean anything.
## The corpus grew by 60%, and the ceiling did not move

Two fresh batches — 85 and 79 pairs — merged with the existing 270: **434 pairs, 16,816 rows**.

| | 270 pairs | 434 pairs |
|---|---|---|
| held-out row accuracy | 98.6% | **98.7%** |
| held-out ToolCall recall | 100% | 100% |
| held-out Meter recall | 100% | 100% |
| every evaluation set | 100% | **100%** |
| `fields`, TUI | 0 differing | **0 differing** |

**Sixty per cent more screens bought one tenth of a point of row accuracy and nothing else.** The
page scores were already at their ceiling, and the ceiling is the reader, not the corpus.

That is the third measurement saying the same thing — round 16's three verbs moved the model further
than four batches, round 34's full-screen pairs changed nothing, and now 164 more screens change
nothing either. **The model is done learning what its features can express; more of the same data
cannot teach it more.**

The new weights are kept because they are no worse on any axis and were trained on more material —
but the honest description of the change is "no measurable improvement", not "better model".
## Where a perfect page score actually comes from

`ui-lm-capture disagree` compares the model's label for each row with the kind the reader built. The
numbers are unbecoming, and the pages are still perfect:

| set | non-blank rows differing | page score |
|---|---|---|
| Grok corpus (training application) | 3.3% | 100% |
| failure states | 2.0% | 100% |
| vim / tmux / nano | 9.5% | 100% |
| real applications | 12.3% | 100% |
| `git diff` screens | 55.3% | 100% |

**The model's raw labels disagree with the reader on up to half the rows, and the pages are exact.**
Two different reasons, and both matter:

- **The guards absorb genuine mistakes.** A shell prompt `moreweb@…$` is labelled `Meter` — it holds
  digits and slashes — and `ls - list directory contents` is labelled `Diff`. Both are refused by the
  structural rules added in rounds 19–23, so the page never claims them. **A large part of the 100%
  is earned by the assembly, not the weights**, and that should have been said plainly much earlier.
- **The diff number is an artefact of the metric.** A reader calls every row of a diff `Diff`
  because the diff is one component; the model calls the rows text, which is what a row *is* before
  anything groups them. Comparing a row label to a block kind is not comparing like with like, and
  55.3% is the measurement's fault, not the model's.

**The honest summary of the model's part:** it is right about most rows, its errors are the same few
shapes every time (prompts, list markers, box edges), and the project's page scores have never been
a measure of model accuracy. What they measure is the whole machine agreeing with itself — which is
what a consumer needs, and not the same thing.
## A feature that could not help, and why the corpus explains it

The disagreement diagnostic named the model's most common mistake: a shell prompt
`moreweb@HOST:/path$` labelled `Meter`. A twentieth feature was added for exactly that shape —
contains `@` and ends with `$` or `#` — and the model was retrained on 342 pairs.

**It made things slightly worse:** 9.5% → 9.7% on vim/tmux/nano, 12.3% → 12.8% on real
applications, 3.3% → 3.5% on the Grok corpus.

The reason is the corpus, and it is now obvious in hindsight: **`dataset` writes only screens that
hold a structured component**, so shell prompts — which appear in plain screens — are in no training
pair at all. A feature the weights never see examples for is a feature with no weight, and twenty
dimensions fitted on the same data as nineteen fit a little worse.

Reverted: features back to nineteen, weights unchanged, disagreement back to 9.5%.

**What this closes:** the model's residual errors sit precisely in the screens the corpus excludes on
purpose. They cannot be trained away without training on screens the project decided not to learn
from — and they do not need to be, because the structural guards refuse every one of the shapes they
produce. That is the honest end of the model-improvement line for this feature set.
## And the corpus filter turns out to be right, tested instead of assumed

The previous section left an obvious question: if the model's errors sit in plain screens, why not
train on plain screens? `--structured` is opt-in, so plain pairs export fine — 68 of them from the
vim, tmux and CLI sessions — and they were merged with the 342 structured pairs: **410 pairs,
15,368 rows**.

| | structured only (342) | with plain screens (410) |
|---|---|---|
| held-out row accuracy | 98.3% | **94.8%** |
| disagreement, vim/tmux/nano | 9.5% | **31.8%** |
| disagreement, real applications | 12.3% | **37.4%** |
| disagreement, failure states | 2.0% | 3.0% |
| page score, vim/tmux/nano | 100% | 100% |

**Everything got worse, by a factor of three on the screens that motivated the experiment.** The
explanation is the one the filter's own comment gives: most settled screens are prose, and teaching
the model that prose is `Block` — forty times over, on screens the reader flattens anyway — dilutes
the rare rows that matter. The model learned "most rows are blocks" harder than it learned anything
else.

Not committed: the weights were written to a temporary file and discarded. The shipped model is
untouched, and the corpus filter now has a measurement behind it rather than a comment.
## The guard that could not disagree with the reader

A multiplexer prepends its pane's title, so a meter line reads
`\wsl.localhost\... 1.3K / 500K │ [Dashboard]`. The guard required the **first word** to be numeric and
refused every meter on a titled pane — fifteen on one recording, all missed.

The first fix relaxed the rule by hand and won big, then dropped `wsl-df` from 100% to **50%**, below
the do-nothing floor. Reverted.

The second fix removes the possibility of disagreement instead of approximating it: **the guard asks
the reader's own parser**, `as_meter(&[line])`, whether the row is a meter. Same function, same rule,
no second opinion to drift.

| screen | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| agent in a single pane | 53.9% | **100%** |
| agent in a split pane | 79.3% | **96.0%** (meters 0/15 → 15/15) |
| `wsl-df` | 100% | **100%** |
| every other evaluation set | 100% | 100% |

**The lesson is about how guards should be written, not about meters.** A guard that restates the
reader's rule in its own words will eventually disagree with it; a guard that calls the reader cannot.
The two other guards — thought needs its mark, diff needs both sides — still restate, and are now on a
list to be replaced the same way.
## Both remaining guards now ask the reader too

The meter guard was converted last round; the other two were still paraphrasing.

| guard | was | is |
|---|---|---|
| thought | `line.contains("Thought") && starts_with('♦')` | `is_thought_row(line)` — the reader's own tool parser, asked for that one row |
| diff | count of `+`/`-` rows ≥ 2 | `as_diff(lines, unstyled).is_some()` — the reader's own diff test |

Everything held: all evaluation sets 100%, the agent-in-a-pane screen 100%, the agent-in-a-split
96.0% (its remaining 4% is the cross-pane rows, which is ADR 0004's problem, not a guard's).

**What is left paraphrasing is one rule, and it has no reader equivalent:** a row made of box drawing
belongs to its box, because the reader has no notion of grouping yet. That is the guard that exists
for the pane container, and it goes when the container arrives.
## The known-hard screens have a scoreboard

Two recordings cannot be read perfectly — an agent in one pane of a split (96.0%) and in a single
pane (100%). They live in `cases/hard`, which is local and ignored by git because a recording holds
the machine it was made on.

`scripts/check-hard.ps1` scores them against the figures they last reached and **fails if either gets
worse**, while never pretending they are gates:

```
  wsl-agent-in-pane      100.0%  (was 100.0%)
  wsl-agent-in-split      96.0%  (was 96.0%)
known-hard screens are no worse than last measured
```

Proven able to fail by raising an expectation to 99.0 for a screen at 96.0: `REGRESSED (was 99.0)`,
exit 1, *"a known-hard screen got worse; that is a regression, not a gap"*.

**Why a separate scoreboard rather than more gates:** the evaluation sets are claims that everything
passes; these are claims that two things do not. Mixing them would either weaken the first or block on
the second, and the useful signal is *"did a known failure get worse"*, which nothing was watching.
## v0.6.1, and what a release checks that a test cannot

Nine checks, about six minutes, all green, then a release cut from that tree.

`check-release` proves the archive runs the way a stranger would use it. Two things were then read
**out of the downloaded build** rather than out of the working tree:

| claim | from the archive |
|---|---|
| a vertical split declares its panes | **2 groups** |
| an agent in one pane, with a titled meter | **100.0%**, `Meter 22/22 reproduced` |

The second one matters most. That screen was at 53.9% three rounds ago, and the only reason it reads
perfectly now is that the meter guard stopped paraphrasing the reader's rule and started calling it.
**A release is where a fix stops being a code change and becomes something a person can run.**

Ship contents: guards that ask the reader (meter, thought, diff), panes declared on both paths, a
screen that draws a box still recognised as a split, `scripts/gate.ps1`, and `scripts/check-hard.ps1`.
## A breadth experiment that measured the shell instead of the programs

Six widely-used terminal programs were recorded to test whether the catalogue describes anything
beyond agent-style interfaces — `htop`, `less`, `man`, `docker ps`, `df`/`free`, `git log --stat`.

Every one scored **100% model and 100% floor**, which reads like a triumph and is the opposite: a
100% floor means copying the screen already suffices, so the reader found **no component at all**. The
sessions held one or two frames each, and `summarize` reports `frames carrying kinds: 0`.

Then the reason, which is about the recording method rather than the reader: `htop` was started in the
background, `less` and `man` need a keystroke to stay on screen, and a recorder that waits for a
settled screen therefore captured the **shell prompt after the program had gone**. The experiment
measured exactly what it was set up to measure and nothing about the programs.

**What it does establish:** a 100% floor is the signature of "this screen taught the component reader
nothing", and that signature is easy to mistake for success when only the model number is read. The
report has claimed this since round 34; today it produced six fresh examples of the mistake in a row.

Not committed, not added to `cases/hard`: they are evidence about my recording, not about the
project. Recording a full-screen program properly needs keystrokes to hold it on screen, which is what
the interaction scripts already do for vim and tmux.
## The breadth experiment, done properly: two pagers, no components, and that is correct

Redone with keystrokes holding each program on screen. What the recordings actually contained:

| program | captured | why |
|---|---|---|
| `htop` | **a WSL error** — `execvpe(htop) failed: No such file or directory` | not installed on this machine |
| `less` | real pages, lines 24–29 of the file | keystrokes held it |
| `man ls` | real page, `LS(1) … NAME … SYNOPSIS` | keystrokes held it |

Both real captures score **100% model and 100% floor**, and the reason is not a defect: a pager draws
one column of text with a prompt at the bottom. The reader represents that as **one `Block`**, which is
exactly right — there is no meter, no tool call, no diff, and nothing for the vocabulary to say.

**So the honest result of the breadth experiment is a negative one, correctly read:** the catalogue
describes software that renders *state* — agents, and the panels of a working session. A pager renders
a *document*, and a document is one block of text. The 100% floor is the measurement agreeing.

The method failure from the previous attempt is now understood as two separate mistakes, both cheap to
avoid: a program that is not installed looks exactly like a program that produced nothing, and a
full-screen program that is not held on screen leaves only the shell behind it.
## v0.7.0: the container ships, and the archive proves it

Released from a green tree and verified by downloading it, on all three platforms:

```
checking release: v0.7.0
archive: 1,737 KB, capture: True, model shipped: True
the download works the way a stranger would use it
```

Then the claims that only exist because of this week's work, read **out of the downloaded Windows
build** rather than out of the working tree:

| claim | from the archive |
|---|---|
| a structured split declares its panes | **2 groups** |
| an agent in a split pane | **100.0%** |
| an agent in a single pane | **100.0%** |
| every evaluation set | 100% |
| `fields` | 688 compared, 0 differing |
| both round trips | 0 sessions below 100% |

A week ago those two screens scored 53.9% and 96.0%. They are the recordings that drove the pane
container, and **the container is now in a binary anyone can run** — which is the only sense in which
any of this work exists.
## Every recording on this machine, read without an error

`scripts/check-corpus.ps1` walks every session under `eval/` and `cases/` and asks a blunter question
than the score checks: **does the pipeline read all of it without crashing or coming back empty?**

```
recordings: 58, pages read: 288
every recording read without an error
```

Proven able to fail by handing it a session with no frames:

```
recordings: 1, pages read: 0
empty -> exit 1
1 recording(s) broke the reader
```

**What went into building it was mostly the filter.** The first version of this sweep reported six
failures and every one was page *text* containing the word "error" — a `ReferenceError`, a PowerShell
`CommandNotFoundException`, a fixture holding a Rust panic message. It now looks only at exit codes,
standard error, and sessions that yield no page at all. **A check that cannot tell a symptom from the
content it reads produces confident nonsense in the same house style as the real numbers** — twice
this week that happened, and twice it was mine.
## The browser document drops meters and tool calls

`scripts/check-html.ps1` renders each recording to HTML and requires **every line the page holds** to
appear in the document, compared as visible text (markup stripped, entities decoded — the naive
comparison failed on perfectly present lines twice before that).

```
pages checked: 58, lines checked: 611
  banner -> missing: 4.2K / 900K | Storage
  report -> missing: ♦ Read inventory.csv
  report -> missing: ♦ Edited inventory.csv   +2 -2
3 line(s) never reached the document
```

**Three lines, two component kinds, one real defect.** The HTML renderer writes only
`class="block"` and `class="line"`: a `Meter` and a `ToolCall` are simply not drawn. The terminal
renderer draws them, the live viewer serves the page's JSON, and the one output a person opens in a
browser silently loses them.

That is the kind of gap this project exists to catch, and it survived a month because every check
either measured the *page* (which is right) or the *terminal document* (which is right) — nothing
measured the browser document against the page it came from.

**Not fixed yet.** The check is committed in its failing state, with the three lines named, because a
gap that is written down is a task and a gap that is not is a surprise. Next: teach `render_page` to
draw `Meter`, `ToolCall`, `Thought` and `Diff` the way `render_terminal` does, then this check goes
green and joins the others.
## Correction: the browser document was never dropping anything

Yesterday's entry claimed the HTML renderer does not draw meters or tool calls. **That was wrong, and
it was my check that was wrong.**

The renderer draws a tool call as three spans — `Read`, `inventory.csv`, `+2 -2` — and a meter as a
bar with its label; the terminal prints those lines as one string each, with a glyph in front. A check
that compares *whole lines* between those two representations reports differences that are not losses.
Comparing **words** instead:

```
pages checked: 58, words checked: 1524
every line the page holds reached the document
```

So the document holds everything, in every recording on this machine. The check still has teeth — with
the tool-call loop emptied it fails immediately:

```
report -> missing word: Read
report -> missing word: Edited
```

**The lesson is the one this month keeps teaching, and yesterday it caught me:** a check measures its
own filter as much as the system. Three false positives in three rounds — the word "error" in page
text, UTF-8 read as ANSI, markup inside a line — and each time the output looked exactly like a real
finding.
## `fields` across every set: clean everywhere except the container path

The styling axis had only ever run on the TUI set. Run over everything:

| set | components compared | differing |
|---|---|---|
| tui | 119 | 0 |
| apps | 44 | 0 |
| unseen | 449 | 0 |
| structured | 18 | 0 |
| workflow | 6 | 0 |
| nongrok | 15 | 0 |
| **cases/hard** | 308 | **7** |

The seven are all on the agent-in-a-split recording, and they are the opposite of the earlier defect:
**the model page carries a span the reader does not**. Cause is the row-keyed styling pass: on a page
with panes, each component's rows count from the top of *its pane*, so the screen-wide row map can put
one pane's styling on another.

Skipping that pass whenever a page has panes made it **worse** — 96 differing — because the pass was
also supplying styling the pane-by-pane copy misses when the two pages split a pane into different
components. Reverted; the recording keeps `score` at 100% and loses styling on 7 of 308 components.

**This is the container's honest remainder:** structure and text are right, and styling inside a pane
is right where both paths segment a pane the same way. Where they differ, one page carries a span the
other does not. The fix is to stop copying styling between two pages that segment differently and
carry the spans through segmentation instead — a change to `graft_spans`, not another pass.
## The seven spans: styling copied by text, and the row map retired for panes

The remainder was styling inside a container pane, and the fix was to stop keying the copy on
something the two pages do not agree about.

**Text is the one thing they agree on.** Both pages are built from the same screen, and where the
`score` axis says they agree, they agree on the words. So `graft_spans` now copies styling by text
first — find the reader component with identical text, take its spans — and the two older passes
retreat:

- the row-keyed map no longer touches a page with panes, because rows inside a pane count from the
  pane's top and the map is keyed by the screen's;
- it also leaves alone anything the text pass already styled, so it can no longer overwrite a
  correct copy with a wrong one.

| set | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| agent in a split pane | 7 differing of 308 | **0** |
| every evaluation set | 0 differing | **0** |
| both hard recordings, `score` | 100% | 100% |
| browser document | 58 pages, 1,524 words | unchanged, all present |

**The lesson is the same one three failed attempts were circling:** when two descriptions disagree
about structure, copy across the attribute they share — the text — rather than inventing a mapping
between their structures.
## The fresh batch is clean again, and the fix was symmetry

Four recordings fell short after the container work. All four are now at **100%**, and both causes were
the same shape: **the label path's guards could only take a label away, never add one.**

| what the screen said | what the model labelled it | what the guard did |
|---|---|---|
| a boxed meter `│ 1.2K / 500K │` | `Block` | demotion allowed, promotion not — stayed missing |
| five dialog items marked `♦` | `Block` | same |

Two promotions were added, each using the reader's own precise test:

- **a row that parses as a use and a limit is a `Meter`**, checked *before* the box rule, because a
  boxed meter opens with `│` and the box rule would swallow it;
- **a row that carries the tool mark `♦` is a `ToolCall`** — or a `Thought` when it says so, which is
  the order the reader uses.

The first attempt at this promoted on the tool-call *detector* instead of the mark, and every
recording collapsed below its floor (grok-help 97% → 51.8%). **A detector that is safe as a refusal is
not automatically safe as a promotion**, and the difference is what the row actually starts with.

| set | result |
|---|---|
| four fresh Grok/shape recordings | 16.7% and 93.6–97.0% → **all 100%** |
| curated sets | every check passing |
| `fields` | 688 compared, 0 differing |
| round trips, both paths | 0 sessions below 100% |
## A richer fresh batch: 9 of 12 perfect, and the check now says which three are not

The batch that failed the audit is the largest yet — **12 recordings, up to 42 frames each**:

| session | model | floor |
|---|---|---|
| grok-context | 81.0% | 5.6% |
| grok-usage | 85.7% | 11.9% |
| grok-help | 88.9% | 18.9% |
| the other nine | **100%** | 11.9% – 100% |

All three shortfalls are **Block counts** (227/291, 102/120, 110/134) with tool calls close behind. So
the parity between the two paths still breaks on the longest, densest Grok screens — not on the meters
or the tool marks that the last two rounds fixed, but on where one block ends and the next begins.

**`check-fresh` now reports that itself.** A bare percentage sent me hunting through a batch with a
command line three times; it now prints each failing session, its per-kind counts and the first two
components it missed, and keeps the batch. The next failure diagnoses itself.

What this batch also settles: the "four of four at 100%" of two rounds ago was a *small* batch. This
one is bigger and finds bigger gaps, which is what a fresh-material check is for — **the size of the
claim is the size of the material, and no bigger.**
## The three short sessions share one shape: the model page loses a whole pane

Frame 12 of `grok-context`, the worst of the three at 81.0%:

```
reader: Block@1-2 ToolCall@3-4 Block@5-34 Block@34-37  |  Block@1-2 ToolCall@3-4 Block@5-19 ToolCall@19-21 …
model:  Block@1-2 ToolCall@3-4 Block@5-34 Block@34-37
```

**The reader split the screen into two panes — four components on the left, seven on the right. The
model page holds only the left one, and its `panes` list is empty**, so it never split at all. Eleven
components against four is most of the 19-point gap, repeated across the session's 42 frames.

Both paths ask the same function, `panes_hold_structure`, on text taken from the same frame. The
reader's answer is yes and the label path's is no, which means the two paths are not being given the
same text — the reader reads the frame's grid directly, while the label path is handed lines built by
`frame_text`. **That difference is where the next hour goes**, and it is one function's worth of
difference, not a design question.

Left alone rather than guessed at: the honest state is a located defect with a suspected cause and no
evidence yet for it.
## The 81% is the instrument, not the model — self-check cannot describe a paned page

The test that settles it: give the label path **the reader's own labels** and it should rebuild the
same page.

```
label path given the reader's own answers: 81.0% over 42 frame(s)
```

It does not, and the reason is in how the labels are handed over. `self-check` assigns one label per
row by walking the reader's components and asking each for the rows it covers:

```text
for component in reader.components:
    for row in component.rows:
        labels[row] = component.kind
```

On a page with panes **two components claim the same rows** — the left pane's block at rows 5–19 and
the right pane's block at rows 5–34 — so the second one overwrites the first and the labels become a
mixture of both sides. The label path then rebuilds from those mixed labels and disagrees, and the
81% is that mixture, not the model.

**So the fresh batch's three short sessions are measuring the instrument.** The same flaw applies to
`scores` on any paned frame, which means the number is meaningless for those screens in both
directions — the model may be fine or not, and this cannot tell.

**What has to change:** a per-row label is the wrong shape once a row can belong to two components. The
diagnostic needs labels per *pane*, or it needs to compare panes as panes. That is a change to the
measurement, not to the pipeline, and it is the next thing to do — before another fresh batch is
believed.
## Judging each pane separately: right diagnosis, wrong plumbing, reverted

The label path hands one array of labels to both panes — `labeled_page(&left, labels, ...)` — so on a
split screen the left cell and the right cell of a row receive the same label, whatever the model
said. That is a real defect and it explains the three short sessions.

Two attempts to fix it, both measured, both reverted:

| attempt | grok-context | grok-help | grok-usage |
|---|---|---|---|
| before | 81.0% | 88.9% | 85.7% |
| model labels each pane separately | 97.6% | **52.4%** | 85.7% |
| …and splits only when a pane holds structure | 81.0% | 41.3% | 85.7% |

The first fixes one session and destroys another, which says the two paths still disagree about
**when** a screen is split, not only about how it is labelled. The model now splits whenever a divider
exists; the reader splits when the panes hold a meter or a diff; and the reader's own split decision
is made from text the model path may see differently.

**Where that leaves it:** the defect is understood — one label cannot describe two panes — and the
plumbing that would fix it needs the model and the reader to agree on the split *first*, then label
each side. That is an ordering change across two crates, not a patch, and it is the next attempt
rather than a guess made at the end of a round.
## The synthetic reproduction passes, which rules out the explanation I had

A ten-row screen with a divider, a meter on the left of row 1 and a shell command on its right, fed
to `page_from_labels` with a `Meter` label on that row: **one meter comes out, not two.**

So the label path does not duplicate a label across panes in that case, and the explanation "one label
cannot describe two panes" is not yet demonstrated — it is a plausible story that a two-minute test
disproved. That test is now a regression test (`panes_parity.rs`), and the split decision differing
between the paths (13 frames against 11 on one recording) remains the only measured difference.

**This is the fourth time an explanation has looked right and been wrong.** The rule the month keeps
re-teaching: write the smallest case, run it, and only then believe the cause.
## The frames where the paths disagree, and what they contain

Two of eighteen frames on `grok-help` split one way and not the other: **frames 6 and 13**. The
reader declares two panes and fifteen components; the model declares none.

What is actually on frame 6:

| fact | value |
|---|---|
| rows | 35 |
| rows holding a use-and-limit shape | **1** |
| reader's component kinds | `Block, ToolCall, Block, Block, …` — **no `Meter` at all** |
| reader's panes | **2** |

So the reader split a screen on which it then built **no meter**, which means the gate and the
segmentation are looking at different text: `panes_hold_structure` asks `as_meter` of the **sliced**
pane rows, while `segments` asks it of the **whole** row. A row that parses as a use-and-limit only
after the divider is removed can switch the split on and then fail to produce the meter that switched
it on.

**That is a real asymmetry and it is not yet proof.** The model path's failure to split the same frame
is still unexplained — it receives text built by `frame_text`, which I have verified is byte-identical
in construction to the reader's — so either one of those two claims is wrong or there is a third
difference nobody has looked at.

Next step, stated so it can be executed without re-deriving it: **assert the invariant in a test** —
if `panes_hold_structure(lines)` is true, `page_from_labels(lines, labels)` must declare panes — using
this frame's text as a fixture. Whichever side fails that test is the one to fix.
## The invariant holds — the contradiction moved one level down

The test asked: if `panes_hold_structure(lines)` is true, does `page_from_labels` declare panes? On
frame 6's real text (39 rows, exported from the labels view):

```
test result: ok. 2 passed
```

It does. So the label path splits when the gate says so, and the asymmetry I suspected in round 140 —
the gate reading sliced rows while the segmenter reads whole ones — is not what breaks this frame.

What is left is narrower and harder to see: **`page --seq 6` and `page --seq 6 --model` do not read
the same frame.** The reader's page for that seq holds 15 components in two panes; the model's holds
five in none. One of them is using a different text, and it is not `frame_text`'s construction, which
is byte-identical.

Next step, precisely: **print the text each path is handed for that seq**, side by side, and diff it.
Six lines of code, and it ends the guessing.
## Two tests now pin the split decision, and both pass

| test | question | result |
|---|---|---|
| `pages/tests/panes_invariant.rs` | if the gate says split, does the label path declare panes? | **passes** |
| `model/tests/split_parity.rs` | does the model's page declare panes on the exported frame? | **passes** |

So on frame 6's exported text — 39 rows, a real divider, one use-and-limit row — the gate, the label
path and the model path all agree that the screen is split.

**And yet `page --seq 6` gives the reader fifteen components in two panes, while `page --seq 6 --model`
gives five in none.** Both commands read the same frame by the same lookup and pass text built by the
same function. One of those statements must be false, and the tests say it is not the text.

**The next step is a diff, not a theory:** print the first three rows of what each command is handed,
for the same sequence. Two lines each. Everything above this paragraph is a guard against regression;
this paragraph is the whole remaining task.
## The trace ends it: on the real frame the gate says no, and the reader splits anyway

`page --seq 6 --model` with `UI_LM_TRACE=1` set:

```
trace: 39 line(s), gate=false
```

**The gate is false on the real frame.** And the reader's page for that same sequence holds fourteen
components in **two panes** — which the gate says cannot happen.

So the contradiction is inside the reader, not between the paths: `page_from_lines` splits a screen
whose gate it has just computed as false. Two ways that could be true, and both are testable:
either the plain two-pane branch is firing on a page that already has fourteen components, or the
container branch is reached by a path that does not consult the gate.

**And the exported fixture was wrong**: my labels-parse dropped one leading space per row, moving the
divider a column and flipping the gate to true. That is why two tests passed while the CLI disagreed —
they were measuring a screen that does not exist. The tests stay (they are correct about their own
text), but the fixture needs regenerating with its indentation intact before either is trusted again.

**What the trace gives the next hour:** a one-line, always-available answer to "what did this path
compute", which is what five rounds of inference failed to provide.
## The trace, taken from both sides

`UI_LM_TRACE=1` now prints the reader's branch decision as well:

```
reader, frame 6:  40 line(s), 5 component(s) before the split branch, divider=true
reader, frame 6:  40 line(s), 5 component(s) before the split branch, divider=false
reader, frame 6:  40 line(s), 11 component(s) before the split branch, divider=true
model,  frame 6:  39 line(s), gate=false
```

Three reader lines for one page is the recursion: the whole screen, then each pane. **And the reader
counts 40 rows where the model path's text yields 39** — a screen whose last row is blank joins to text
ending in a newline, and `.lines()` drops that trailing blank.

That looked like the whole answer. It was not: restoring `split('\n')` so the model path keeps all 40
rows left every score unchanged (81.0 / 88.9 / 85.7). **The row is real and the theory is not**, which
is the fifth time this month that a difference which explained everything explained nothing.

**What is now measured, for whoever continues:** the reader recurses into the container for this frame,
the model path does not, and the two disagree by one row and one split decision. The trace prints both
facts in one line each, which is more than five rounds of inference managed.
## Found it: the two paths were asking different questions

The trace led here in one round after five rounds of inference. The reader's container branch was
gated on **its own components**:

```rust
let structured = components.iter()
    .any(|component| component.component != "Column" && component.component != "Block");
if allow_split && structured { … }
```

while the label path asked the **line-based** gate, `panes_hold_structure(text)`. They are different
questions: the reader split whenever it happened to find any tool line, the label path only when a pane
held a meter or a diff. On three fresh Grok recordings that meant the reader recursed into panes and
the model did not — for frames where the two disagreed about whether a tool line existed at all.

The fix is one branch: **both paths now call `panes_hold_structure`**.

| session | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| grok-context | 81.0% | **100%** |
| grok-help | 88.9% | **100%** |
| grok-usage | 85.7% | **100%** |

Curated sets unchanged — every check passes, `fields` 688 with 0 differing, both round trips clean,
both hard recordings 100%, and the browser document still holds every word.

**What took six rounds:** the answer was one line away the whole time, and the thing that found it was
a trace printing what each path computed — not another look at the code, and not another theory. Every
explanation I built in rounds 139–144 was plausible, disproved cheaply, and wrong.
## v0.8.0, cut from a tree where everything is green

Eleven checks, about seven minutes, every one passing — including **fresh material at 12 of 12**, the
batch that spent six rounds at 81–89%.

```
checking release: v0.8.0
archive: 1,741 KB, capture: True, model shipped: True
the download works the way a stranger would use it
```

Three platforms, verified by downloading and running the archive; the downloaded build declares **2
pane groups** on the structured split.

What ships since v0.7.0:

- **both paths ask the same question before splitting a screen** — the one-line fix that ended the
  fresh-material gap;
- guards that can **promote** a meter and a tool mark, not only refuse them;
- the terminal renderer and the browser document both verified against the page;
- `check-corpus` (every recording reads without an error) and `check-html` (every word reaches the
  document) as permanent checks;
- `check-fresh` now names the sessions and components it fell short on;
- `UI_LM_TRACE`, which is what actually found the one-line fix.
## One command for the whole question

Eleven checks answer eleven questions, and running them one at a time is how a person stops running
them. `scripts/check-everything.ps1` runs them all and prints one table — twelve lines, because it
also checks the release download when given a tag:

```
gate ok · all ok · corpus ok · html ok · hard ok · validate ok · race ok ·
interaction ok · live-page ok · render-scale ok · fresh ok · release ok

every check passed in 395.5s
```

`-Quick` skips the five slow ones and answers in under forty seconds: that is the loop for working.
The full run is the loop for shipping.

**Proven able to fail:** with one formatting violation in `lib.rs`, the table printed five passes and
`1 of 6 check(s) failed in 44.7s`, naming the gate. Every check always runs, because a table of one
failure and ten passes says more than a run that stops at the first line.
## `audit` runs over the corpus at last — with its failure unproven

The `audit` subcommand checks two things nothing else does: that a frame is self-consistent, and that
**every page row says what the screen says**. Nothing has ever run it outside its own tests.

`scripts/check-audit.ps1` now runs it over every recording:

```
recordings audited: 58
audit: no violations
every recording is consistent with itself
```

**But I could not make it fail.** Two attempts — a session whose declared width no longer fits its
frames, and a frame carrying a masked span at row 999 — both came back clean. Either `audit` reads the
geometry from the frames themselves (so my first edit changed nothing it looks at), or the injected
field is not the one it validates.

By this project's own rule, a check that has never been seen to fail is not yet a check. So this one
**is not wired into `check-everything`**, and it says so in its own header rather than sitting in the
list looking green.

What would settle it: a test in the crate that calls `frame_problems` on a hand-made frame and asserts
the violation text. That is five lines and it is the next hour's work, not a round's.
## The audit's invariant does fail — just not through my fabrications

Five lines in the crate settle what two hand-edited sessions could not:

```rust
let mut frame = Frame::empty(10, 20);
frame.volatile = vec![Span { row: 99, col: 0, len: 2, reason: MaskReason::NoSemanticChange }];
assert!(frame_problems(&frame).iter().any(|p| p.contains("does not fit")));
```

It passes: **`frame_problems` rejects a masked span on a row the screen does not have.** So the
invariant the audit tests is a real one, and it can fail.

What is still unproven is the **script's** ability to fail — my two attempts edited recordings in ways
the command did not object to, which now looks like evidence about my edits rather than about the audit.
`check-audit.ps1` therefore stays out of `check-everything`, and its header says "passes on the corpus;
its failure has been shown at unit level, not yet through the command".
## `audit` reported "no violations" on a session it could not read — so that check is withdrawn

The unit test proved the *invariant* can fail. Then the command was asked to fail on a session whose
`session.json` was `{ this is not json`:

```
audit: no violations
```

**A command that reports clean on an unreadable recording is not auditing it**, and every "58
recordings audited, no violations" I reported through `check-audit.ps1` may have been counting zero
recordings audited and zero checked. The script has been withdrawn — a check that cannot be shown to
look at its material is worse than no check, because it occupies a line in a table that people trust.

What is actually known about `audit`:

- `frame_problems` does reject a masked span outside the screen (unit test, 50 in the crate);
- the command takes `Summarize`'s arguments, and given a directory whose record cannot be parsed it
  reports no violations rather than an error;
- nothing else in this repository has ever run it.

**So the audit is a written invariant with no working path to it.** The next thing to do is not more
fabrication: it is to make the command say how many recordings it read, and to fail when a recording
cannot be read. Until then it stays off the list, and this note is the reason.
## The audit works, and it has been telling the truth about a real defect

Two things were wrong, and neither was the audit's logic.

**First, the invocation.** `audit` takes its directories after `--`:

```
ui-lm-capture audit -- eval\tui\wsl-vim
audit: 1 recording(s), 4 frame(s) checked
```

Every earlier attempt passed `--session`, which the command accepted and ignored, so it audited nothing
and said "no violations". It now prints how many recordings and frames it checked, and **fails when it
checked none** — a command that inspected nothing can no longer be mistaken for a clean one.

**Second, and this is the real finding:** with the invocation right, the audit immediately fails on an
ordinary vim split:

```
eval\tui\wsl-vim: frame 2: row 0 reads "apples 12" in the page and
                                  "apples 12                        |apples 12" on the screen
```

That is the pane container: inside a pane, a page row is a **slice** of the screen row, not the whole
of it. The audit compares page row *n* with screen row *n*, which was exactly right until screens could
be split, and has been wrong ever since — silently, because nothing ran it and the one attempt to run it
used a flag that did nothing.

So the audit is not broken; **it is the only check that noticed**, and it has been saying so to an
audience of no one. Making it pane-aware — compare each pane's rows with its own slice — is the next
change, and it is the first time in weeks that a defect was found by an existing instrument rather than
by a new one.
## `render_text` drew one pane on top of the other, and fixing it exposed a second defect

The audit's failure was not the audit's. `render_text` placed every component by its row number, and
two panes claim the same rows — so the right pane silently overwrote the left, and the audit correctly
reported a page that had lost one. The page had not; the renderer had.

With `render_text` drawing the two sides side by side, as the terminal renderer already did:

```
audit: 58 recording(s), 288 frame(s) checked
eval\apps\cargo-tree: frame 0: two components share the id "block-ed7f9f15d27bb56a"
```

**Everything else is clean, and the audit's first honest run found a real defect**: two components with
the same id. Ids are derived from a block's own text, so two blocks that read alike collide — and a
consumer keyed by id loses one of them.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| vim split, audited | no violations (was 2 per frame) |
| 58 recordings, 288 frames | **one defect, on one recording** |
| curated sets | every check passes; both round trips clean |

The audit is now worth running, which it never was before: the command reports what it inspected, fails
when it inspected nothing, and compares each pane with its own slice.
## The duplicate id is fixed, and the audit now names a limit of the page itself

**Fixed:** two components can no longer share an id. `unique_id` appends a suffix when a block's
text-derived id is already taken, and provenance keeps tracking the *base* id — tracking the suffixed
one made a block look new whenever a page's shape shifted, which turned three carried components into
contradictions the moment ids were made unique.

```
audit: 58 recording(s), 288 frame(s) checked
cases\hard\wsl-agent-in-split: frame 19: row 2 reads "" in the page and "   │" on the screen
151 violations, all on the paned recording
```

**What the 151 are:** on a split screen the page does not record **where the divider is**. Pane lines
are right-trimmed, so the left pane's padding is gone and the divider cannot be put back at the right
column. The rows that fail are the ones where the divider is the only thing on the screen row.

That is a limitation of the page, not of the renderer or the audit: **a paned page describes two panes
and not the column between them.** Fixing it means recording the divider's column on the surface — a
schema change, small, and the next thing on the list.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| curated sets | every check passes (`check-all` green after the id fix) |
| duplicate id | gone from the corpus |
| paned pages | 151 row mismatches, all from the missing divider column |
## The 151 are rows with no component at all — two attempts, both reverted

Both plausible fixes changed nothing, and the count stayed at exactly 151:

| attempt | why it was plausible | what it did |
|---|---|---|
| pad the left pane to its widest row | the divider sits at the pane's width | 151 → **151** |
| a `Meter` claims only the row it fills | one component claimed rows 1–7 and filled one | 151 → **151** |

So the failing rows are not padding and not a mis-sized meter. They are rows the page has **no
component for at all** — the screen draws a divider and the page says nothing, because the only thing
on that row is the column between two panes.

**This is the schema question from last round, now pinned:** a paned page describes two panes and not
the column between them, so a row that is nothing but that column cannot be represented. Fixing it
means recording the divider — a field on the surface — and that is a change to the envelope, not a
patch to the renderer.

Both attempts were reverted. Curated sets green; the duplicate-id defect stays fixed; the 151 stay
named.
## The divider's column is not enough — it needs the glyph, and a row to put it in

Third attempt at the 151, and the first that was a real design change: a `pane_divider: Option<u16>`
field on the surface, filled by both paths, used by the renderer to pad the left pane back to width.

**151 → 151.** The column gives the **width** but not the **character**: the divider lives in the left
pane's text, and the rows that fail have no component there at all, so padding produces spaces where
the screen had `│`. Recording a width cannot draw a glyph nobody recorded.

Reverted — the field is not in the envelope, the curated sets are green, and the count is unchanged.

**What this settles:** the fix is not one field. A paned page would have to record either the divider
character as well, or a component for rows that are nothing but the column. Both are honest options;
both are bigger than a field, and neither is worth guessing at with the remaining space in a round.

Three attempts, three reverts, one measured fact each time: the page cannot say what those rows are.
## A fresh batch after the week's changes: 100%, every kind perfect

The week changed ids (`unique_id`), the pane renderer and the split gate. A fresh batch is the only way
to know whether any of that cost the model anything:

```
model 100.0%  floor 43.9%  over 83 frame(s), trained on 434 screen(s)
  Block      366/366 reproduced
  Meter       53/53  reproduced
  Thought     14/14  reproduced
  ToolCall    44/44  reproduced
```

**Eighty-three frames, four kinds, nothing missed.** The floor at 43.9% says the screens held real
structure, so this is not the "100% of nothing" trap: the model had 410 components to get right and
got all of them.

Everything else held too — `check-everything -Quick` in 31.8 seconds, every check passing.

**Why this matters more than another fix attempt:** the last three rounds each tried to close the 151
and each was reverted. This round asked the opposite question — *did the changes that did land break
anything?* — and it has an answer with a number in it.
## 151 → 0: a meter ends its own stretch

The rows the audit could not place were not a schema problem after all — they were a **segmentation
bug one line wide**. The rule from round 123 pushes a meter as a one-row segment, but nothing stopped
the *next* row from extending it: `found.last_mut()` treated the fresh meter segment as the stretch to
grow. A meter that fills one row therefore claimed seven, and the six rows below it were inside a
component that had no text for them.

The fix is a flag that makes a meter the end of a stretch:

```rust
let was_meter = last_was_meter;   // at the top of each row
last_was_meter = false;
...
Some(current) if !current.boxed && current.tool == tool && !was_meter => { current.last = row + 1 }
```

| check | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| corpus audit | **151 violations** | **no violations**, 288 frames |
| curated sets | green | green |
| `check-everything -Quick` | green | green in 36 seconds |

**Three rounds of schema theories — padding, meter size, a divider column — and the answer was that one
flag, written in the wrong scope.** What found it was printing which component covered the failing rows
and seeing `Meter rows 1–7 lines=0`: a component claiming seven rows and holding one.
## The audit is a check again, and this time it is one

`scripts/check-audit.ps1` is back in the suite, written against the invocation the command actually
takes (`audit -- <dirs>`) and requiring positive proof that it inspected what it was given.

| property | how it is enforced |
|---|---|
| the corpus is consistent | 58 recordings, 288 frames, no violations |
| it can fail | a session with a masked span at row 999 → **exit 1**, the violation named |
| it looked at the material | fails when it inspected fewer recordings than were passed |
| it never inspected nothing | fails when it inspected none |

```
corpus   ok   2.9s  every recording read without an error
audit    ok   3.1s  every recording is consistent with itself
html     ok   2.8s  every line the page holds reached the document
```

`check-everything -Quick` now runs thirteen checks in 34.5 seconds.

**What this closes:** the one check that compares a page against the cells it came from is now part of
the routine, having been the instrument that found the dropped pane, the duplicate id and the
seven-row meter — and having spent three rounds unable to report anything at all.
## v0.9.0, cut from thirteen green checks

```
every check passed in 383.9s
```

Then the release, verified by downloading it — and this time the archive was asked to audit itself:

```
audit: 2 recording(s), 42 frame(s) checked
audit: no violations
```

What ships since v0.8.0:

- **component ids are unique**, with provenance tracked on the base id so carried components stay
  carried;
- **a meter ends its own stretch** — the one-line fix that took the corpus audit from 151 violations to
  zero;
- **`render_text` draws panes side by side**, which is what the audit had been reporting as a lost pane;
- **the audit is a check**, with proof it fails and proof it inspected its material;
- **`check-everything`** runs all thirteen in one table, with a 35-second working loop.

The suite has grown from eleven checks to thirteen this week, and both additions came from asking a
command what it had actually done.
## Fourteen checks: the extremes of screen size now round trip too

Everything is measured at 24x80. `scripts/check-sizes.ps1` records the same program at **10x20 and
60x200** and requires the round trip to return every line, on both paths:

```
10x20   reader 9/9   lines (100%)      model 9/9   lines (100%)
60x200  reader 30/30 lines (100%)      model 30/30 lines (100%)
```

Flat numbers at the extremes are what a drawing pipeline should do — nothing in the page depends on how
wide a screen is — but "should" is not a measurement, and this costs four seconds.

**It also refuses to accept a perfect score on an empty screen:** fewer than eight lines of content is
a failure, because a screen where nothing was drawn round trips perfectly and proves nothing. That
guard is the direct lesson of round 115's "100% of nothing" — the check now knows the difference.

`check-everything -Quick` runs **fourteen checks in 39 seconds**.
## Wide characters: the page is faithful to the grid, and that costs the text a space

Recording `日本語のテキスト` and reading it back:

```
page line:  [日 本 語 の テ キ ス ト]
round trip: 1/1 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)
```

The recorder stores what the terminal draws: `日` in column 0 and the **continuation cell** in column
1, one run each — `row=0 col=0 len=1 ch=日`, `row=0 col=1 len=1 ch=' '`. A wide glyph fills two cells,
so the page's row holds both, and the text gains a space for every wide character in it.

**The round trip is exact**, which is the honest way to describe this: the page is faithful to the
*screen*, not to the *string*. A consumer that renders the text as a string will show the gaps; a
consumer that lays out by column will put every glyph exactly where it was.

Two ways to change it, both larger than a patch:

- record a wide flag per run, so the page can hold `日本語` and the renderer can pad;
- teach the reader to merge continuation cells, which needs to be sure a space is a continuation and
  not a space — and `日 本` is a legitimate screen too.

**Not changed.** The screen is what the project promises to describe, and it does describe it; the
string is a second, desirable property that the envelope has no way to state yet. Written down rather
than guessed at, and the round trip stays at 100% in the meantime.
## The emulator's widths are right; only the text is padded

A marker after two wide glyphs settles it. `sh: 1: 日本: not found` lands in the grid as:

```
col 7 日 · col 8 ' ' · col 9 本 · col 10 ' ' · col 11 ':'
```

The colon is at **column 11**, not 9. Each wide glyph advanced the cursor by two cells, so the
emulator is measuring widths correctly — the blank cell beside each glyph is its continuation, recorded
as a space exactly as a terminal draws it.

**So the limitation is narrower than last round said:** nothing is mis-measured. The page is
column-exact, and the only thing lost is that the *string* carries the continuation cells as spaces.

That also removes the heuristic I had rejected: merging would not be guesswork about widths, it would be
guesswork about which spaces are continuations — and `日 本` is still a legitimate screen. The clean fix
remains a flagged cell in the frame format, which is a format change, not a reader one.
## A screen full of HTML arrives as text

A program that prints `<script>alert(1)</script>` and `A & B <tag> C` is a screen like any other, and
the browser document must hold it as text rather than as instructions:

| what was checked | result |
|---|---|
| raw `<script>alert` in the document | **absent** |
| `&lt;script&gt;` present | yes |
| `A &amp; B` present | yes |

`check-html` now records that screen and requires all three, so the escaping is a permanent guard rather
than a one-off experiment:

```
pages checked: 58, words checked: 1524
markup on a screen arrives in the document as text, escaped
```

**Why this is worth its twenty lines:** the renderer's escaping was never in doubt in the code, but it
had never been *exercised* — no recording in this repository contains markup, so every previous run of
`check-html` proved fidelity on material that could not tell the difference.
## Invalid UTF-8 truncates the screen, and it is not yet clear whose truncation it is

A program that prints `before \377\376 after` and then a second line records a screen holding
**`before` and nothing else** — not the rest of that line, not the line after it:

```
screen:      [before]
round trip:  1/1 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)
```

The round trip is exact, which is the uncomfortable part: **the page faithfully describes a screen that
lost content**, so every check in this repository agrees with it.

Two possible authors, and this round did not settle which:

- the Windows console (ConPTY) may refuse the invalid sequence and never hand the bytes over;
- our own screen decoder may drop the remainder of a chunk when a byte does not decode.

What is measured: the loss takes the **rest of the chunk**, not just the offending line, and no error is
reported anywhere. Attribution needs one experiment — print the bad byte, wait, then print a third line,
and see whether the later write survives. If it does, the loss is per-chunk; if not, it is per-screen.

Written down rather than fixed, because a fix for the wrong author would be a patch to code that was
never wrong.
## Correction: invalid UTF-8 does not truncate a screen

Last round reported that a bad byte ends the recording. **That was my quoting, not the recorder.** The
session's own `args` field shows what actually ran: PowerShell split the command at spaces, so `sh`
received `printf one` as its script and the rest as positional arguments. It printed `one ` and exited
in the same second it started.

Run properly, through a script written to disk:

```
args: -e sh /tmp/ui-lm/bad.sh
started 00:00:31  ended 00:00:37          (six seconds, as asked)
frame 0: [one  two]
frame 1: [one  two] [third line]
```

**The bad bytes are dropped and everything else survives** — the two spaces in `one  two` are where
`\377\376` used to be, and the later line arrives in the next frame. No truncation, no early exit.

So the finding shrinks to something small and honest: **a program that prints bytes which are not UTF-8
loses those bytes and nothing else.** That is a defensible behaviour for a terminal recorder, and it is
the opposite of what I wrote yesterday.

**The lesson is not new and I keep paying for it:** the fourth quoting failure this month, and the second
time one produced a confident finding. Before believing a recording, read the session's own record of
what it ran.
## The recorder now says what it launched, and warns when the program vanishes

Four quoting failures this month, two of which produced confident findings, and the diagnosis was always
the same: open `session.json` afterwards and read the arguments that were actually passed. That is a
detour the command can remove.

```
running: wsl.exe -e sh -c printf one \377\376 two; sleep 2
recorded 1 frame(s) ... (the program exited)
warning: the program was gone after 1925 ms with 1 frame(s); if that was not intended,
         check how its arguments were quoted
```

The first line shows the command **as parsed**, so a shell that split it is visible before anything is
believed. The warning fires when a program exits having drawn at most one frame — which is either a
quiet program or a broken command, and it is worth saying which it might be.

**This is the cheapest thing built all month** — six lines — and it addresses the single most frequent
cause of wasted work in the last three weeks.
## Two clean batches after the week's changes, 170 frames between them

| batch | frames | Block | Meter | ToolCall | Thought | floor |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| first | 83 | 366/366 | 53/53 | 44/44 | 14/14 | 43.9% |
| second | 87 | 372/372 | 58/58 | 48/48 | — | 41.8% |

**170 frames, 955 components, not one missed**, after a week that changed component ids, the pane
renderer and the split gate. The floors — 43.9% and 41.8% — are the useful part of the table: the
screens held real structure, so this is not a perfect score on empty prose.

That is the strongest statement the project can currently make and it is worth stating precisely: *the
model reads new Grok material perfectly, against the same code that ships*.
## A third clean batch, and the kind it cannot contain

Driving the agent to actually edit a file — `alpha` became `omega`, verified on disk — produced another
clean run:

```
model 100.0%  floor 21.8%  over 30 frame(s)
  Block 172/172 · Meter 26/26 · Thought 9/9 · ToolCall 22/22
```

**And no `Diff` component at all**, which is the interesting part. The edit appears as a tool line:

```
♦ Edit edit.txt +1/-1
```

The agent reports an edit as **counts**, not as diff lines, so no screen it draws contains a two-sided
diff for the reader to find. `Diff` is therefore exercised only by the curated `git diff` recordings —
**four of the five kinds appear in fresh material and one cannot.**

That is a coverage statement the project should make about itself, because "100% on fresh material"
would otherwise read as "every kind was tested". It was not: it was four kinds, on 200 frames, and the
fifth has a curated set and nothing else.
## The fifth kind was one question away

Last round concluded that `Diff` cannot appear in fresh material, because the agent reports an edit as a
tool line with counts — `♦ Edit edit.txt +1/-1`. That was true of the agent's *edits*, not of its
*screens*: asking it to run `git diff` in a repository with a real change makes the output block hold a
two-sided diff, and the reader finds it.

```
model 100.0%  floor 33.3%  over 36 frame(s)
  Block 180/180 · Diff 1/1 · Meter 26/26 · Thought 10/10 · ToolCall 16/16
```

**All five kinds are now perfect on material recorded after training.** The previous round's claim — four
of five, the fifth curated only — was itself one command from being wrong, and it is corrected here.

The lesson is small and worth keeping: a kind that does not appear in one workflow is not a kind the
application cannot draw. "It never shows a diff" was a conclusion about *edits*, generalised one step
too far.
## The diff is a check now, not a discovery

Round 170 found that asking the agent to run `git diff` produces the fifth kind on fresh material. This
round turned that into `check-diff-fresh.ps1`: it builds a repository with a real change, drives the
agent to show the diff, and requires two things.

```
diff components: 1
model 100.0%  floor 30.8%  over 37 frame(s)
  Block 183/183 · Diff 1/1 · Meter 28/28 · Thought 10/10 · ToolCall 21/21
a diff recorded after training was read exactly
```

**And it refuses to pass when it measured nothing.** Run with the diff prompt removed, it reports:

```
no diff appeared, so the check measured nothing
a fresh diff was not read exactly
```

That second line is wrong — the model was perfect on those 61 frames — and it is wrong on purpose: a
check that measured nothing must fail, and the strongest way to say so is to fail loudly rather than to
pass quietly on an empty screen.

`check-everything` now runs **fifteen checks**, and the slow half includes both kinds of fresh material.
## v0.10.0, from fifteen green checks and a release that shows its own working

```
every check passed in 441.9s
```

Then the archive, asked to do three things rather than one:

```
running: wsl.exe -e sh -c printf hi\n; sleep 1
recorded 1 frame(s) ... (the program exited)
audit: 1 recording(s), 38 frame(s) checked
```

The recorder prints what it launched; the audit counts what it inspected. Both are diagnostics that did
not exist a fortnight ago, and both are now part of what a download can be asked to prove.

What ships since v0.9.0:

- **the audit joined the suite**, with proof it fails and proof it looked;
- **`check-sizes`** — 10x20 and 60x200 both round trip every line, and fewer than eight lines of content
  is a failure;
- **`check-diff-fresh`** — the fifth kind on material recorded after training, refusing to pass when it
  measured nothing;
- **the recorder shows its parsed command** and warns when a program vanishes with one frame;
- **`check-everything`** now runs fifteen checks in one table, with a working loop of about 40 seconds.
## The browser check only ever looked at half the pages

`render` draws whatever page it is given, and the project has two builders. `check-html` had only ever
rendered the **reader's** pages, so a model page that reached the document short of a component would
have passed without a word — the same shape of gap as the diff that only appeared when someone asked
for it.

Now both paths are checked, and the word count doubles:

```
recordings checked: 58 on both paths, words checked: 3048
every line the page holds reached the document
markup on a screen arrives in the document as text, escaped
```

**Nothing was wrong** — the model's pages reach the browser intact — and that is the second time this
week a gap has been closed by *widening* a check rather than by fixing anything. The first was the
diff; both were found by asking the same question: *what material has this check never been given?*
## The corpus check read half of what the project can build

Same question as last round, applied to the next check: `check-corpus` builds pages for every recording
— but only with the reader. A model path that fell over on one odd session would have gone unnoticed
while fifteen checks stayed green.

```
recordings: 58 on both paths, pages read: 576
every recording read without an error
```

**Both builders survive every recording on this machine.** The number doubling — 288 to 576 — is the
whole content of the change: nothing was broken, and now the claim covers both paths.

**One half-coverage remains, and it is named:** `audit` builds reader pages internally, so the audit
still has no model-path equivalent. That is a flag on the command rather than a rewrite, and it is the
next check to widen.
## Pointing the audit at the model's pages found a defect within seconds

Round 174 named the last half-coverage: `audit` only ever examined the reader's pages. A `--model` flag
fixed that, and the first run said:

```
eval\apps\cargo-tree: frame 0: two components share the id "block-ed7f9f15d27bb56a"
```

Three ids repeated on one recording, **because the label path never received the unique-id fix of round
154** — that round's patch landed in the wrong scope, clippy then removed the unused variable, and the
model's pages were never audited to notice. Both builders now dedupe across the pane merge as well, and
both audit clean:

```
reader: 58 recordings, 288 frames, no violations
model:  58 recordings, 288 frames, no violations
```

**This is the strongest argument for widening a check rather than writing a new one:** the defect was
two rounds old, invisible to fifteen green checks, and found by pointing an existing instrument at
material it had never seen.
## v0.10.1, and the fix proven from the archive it ships in

```
every check passed in 454.9s

checking release: v0.10.1
audit: 1 recording(s), 1 frame(s) checked
audit: no violations
```

That second block is `audit --model` run **from the downloaded archive** against the recording that
carried the duplicate ids. A fix verified only in the working tree is a fix nobody has; this one was
proven in the binary.

What ships since v0.10.0:

- **`audit --model`** — the page-level audit covers both builders, and found three duplicate ids within
  seconds of being pointed at the model's pages;
- **unique ids on the label path**, at the site that had been missed two rounds earlier;
- **dedupe across the pane merge** on both builders;
- **`check-corpus` and `check-html` widened to both builders** — 576 pages, 3,048 words.
## One flake, recorded rather than shrugged at

The v0.10.1 commit failed on `ubuntu-latest` in a test my changes do not touch:

```
a_child_runs_in_the_directory_the_config_names ... FAILED
assertion `left == right` failed
```

The test spawns a child that prints its working directory and compares it with a temporary directory.
It passed on Windows and macOS in the same run, and **passed on all three when the job was re-run**.

So it is a flake — and a flake in a suite is worse than a gap, because it teaches the reader to re-run
rather than to look. The likely cause is that the child's idea of the path differs from the test's on
that runner (`/tmp` resolution, a symlinked temporary directory), which is a comparison that should be
normalised rather than a race.

**Not fixed this round.** What is fixed is the habit around it: the failure is named here, with the run
that proved it transient, so the next person does not have to wonder whether the suite was ever green.
## The promise has a check now, and the check was shown failing

`check-scrub.ps1` exports a pair and holds it to its promise: the catalogue intact, the account gone,
something actually redacted.

```
pairs: 13
a scrubbed pair keeps its catalogue and loses its account
```

**Proven able to fail** by removing the protection the fix added:

```
the catalogue's name was scrubbed away
a shared pair is not safe to share
```

`check-everything -Quick` now runs **sixteen checks in 54 seconds**. That is the sixteenth, and it is
the fastest of them — half a second to test a promise that had been silently broken for the life of the
project.
## v0.11.0, and the scrub fix proven from the archive

```
every check passed in 455.1s

checking release: v0.11.0
catalogue intact: True   account leaked: False
```

That second line is the downloaded build's own `dataset --scrub` run against a screen holding a real
home path. The defect it fixes had been in every pair the project ever shared, and the proof is in the
binary rather than in the working tree.

What ships since v0.10.1:

- **scrubbing no longer renames the catalogue** — `moreweb.terminal/v1` survived being treated as an
  account name;
- **`check-scrub`** — the sixteenth check, half a second, proven to fail without the fix;
- **the directory test no longer depends on which line says the path**, which was the flake that cost a
  re-run on ubuntu.

Sixteen checks, 455 seconds, every one shown failing at least once.
## Flag parity, checked instead of assumed, and a fifth clean batch

A defect class this project hit early was a flag that existed on every command but one. So the whole
surface was asked at once, and this time there was nothing missing:

| command builds a page | takes `--model` |
|---|---|
| `page`, `tui`, `render`, `score`, `fields`, `disagree`, `audit` | **yes, all seven** |
| `record`, `summarize`, `dataset`, `compare`, `self-check`, `provenance`, `baseline` | no — none of them builds a model page |

`dataset` exports the reader's page as a teacher on purpose, `self-check` exists to compare the label
path against the reader, and the rest do not build pages at all. **Seven out of seven is the answer this
time**, which is worth recording: a sweep that only reports failures cannot show that a class is gone.

And a fifth clean batch after training:

```
model 100.0%  floor 50.1%  over 71 frame(s)
  Block 300/300 · Meter 41/41 · Thought 6/6 · ToolCall 33/33
```

**307 frames across five batches, one kind missing from that particular run only** — this recording held
