no diff — and every component in every kind it did hold came back exactly.
## A structure assertion, with its failure unproven

`check-html` now asks a second question of a paned page: the document must carry the pane wrapper and
two pane divs, not merely every word.

```
recordings checked: 58 on both paths, words checked: 3048
paned documents checked: 4
every line the page holds reached the document
```

Frame 0 is rarely paned — a split appears later — so a second pass renders frame 20 of the two hard
recordings on both builders, which is where the four come from.

**I could not make it fail.** Forcing the renderer's flat branch on every page should have removed the
wrapper and tripped the assertion; it built, ran, and the check still passed. Something about the
mutation or the path it took was not what I assumed, and rather than guess again the honest status is:
the assertion is in place, it runs on real paned documents, and **its failure has not been demonstrated**.
By this project's own rule that makes it a guard of unknown strength, not a proven one — and it is
recorded that way rather than counted as the seventeenth check.
## The structure assertion is proven now, after three bugs in the check itself

Yesterday's assertion ran and could not fail. It took three fixes to the **check**, each found by running
it rather than reading it:

| bug | how it showed |
|---|---|
| appended after the script had already decided its exit code | its findings were collected and never looked at |
| matched compact JSON (`"panes":[[`) | the output is pretty-printed, so it never matched — zero paned pages |
| asserted on every hard recording | the *single*-pane one has no panes and failed honestly for the wrong reason |

With all three fixed:

```
paned documents checked: 2
every line the page holds reached the document
```

and forcing the renderer's flat branch:

```
wsl-agent-in-split (reader, frame 20) -> no pane wrapper
wsl-agent-in-split (model, frame 20) -> no pane wrapper
a paned page was not drawn as panes
```

**A check is code too, and it fails the same ways.** The suite now has seventeen proven properties, and
the last one cost three rounds of believing a green line that was measuring nothing.
## v0.11.1: the browser draws what the terminal draws

```
every check passed in 457.6s

checking release: v0.11.1
pane wrapper: True   pane divs: 2   grid css: True
```

The second line is the downloaded build rendering a paned recording. The three renderers now agree: the
page describes panes, the terminal draws them in columns, and the browser does too — with a check that
fails when it stops.

What ships since v0.11.0:

- **panes drawn side by side in HTML** — the third renderer had never been shown a split screen;
- **a structure assertion in `check-html`** — it asks whether a paned page was drawn *as panes*, proven by
  forcing the renderer flat;
- and three bugs in that assertion itself, found by running it: appended after the exit code, matching
  compact JSON against pretty output, and asserting on a recording that has no panes.
## Two agents in two panes, and the model reads both

The hardest shape this project has recorded: **an agent running in each half of a split screen**, each
side holding its own meter, tool calls and reasoning.

```
model 100.0%  floor 9.9%  over 50 frame(s)
  Block 504/504 · Meter 74/74 · Thought 22/22 · ToolCall 96/96
```

Both panes hold structure — the left has a meter and three tool calls, the right has a meter, a thought
and a tool call — and the page keeps them apart:

```
pane 0: Block, Meter, Block, ToolCall, Block, ToolCall, Block, Block, Block
pane 1: Meter, Block, Block, Thought, Block, ToolCall, Block, Block, Block, Block, Block
```

**696 components across 50 frames, nothing missed, floor 9.9%.** This is the case the container was
built for, taken one step further than the recording that motivated it: not one structured pane beside
a shell, but a structured pane beside another.

It is now a third hard recording in `check-hard`, at 100%, so the shape can never regress without
something saying so.
## The two-agent case exposes a real hole: it draws back at 23.8%

The hardest recording — an agent in each half of a split — scores **100%** and round trips at **23.8%**:

```
score:       model 100.0% over 50 frames, 696 components, nothing missed
round trip:  322/1351 line(s) of content came back (23.8%), 1351 drawn
```

**Both numbers are true.** `score` compares the model's page with the reader's; they agree completely.
`round trip` asks whether the terminal renderer can draw that page back, and on two richly-filled panes
it cannot: **76% of the lines land in the wrong column.**

The cause is the one named in round 155 and never closed: a pane's lines are right-trimmed, so the page
keeps no width to align the two sides by, and a row of two full panes needs exactly that. With a
structured pane beside a *shell* the missing width rarely mattered; with two full panes it matters on
every row.

I tried recording the divider's column on the surface again — the change that had no effect before —
and it changed nothing here either, because the page I inspected has **no divider recorded at all**:
the split branch that produced it is not the one I patched. So the fix is one level further down than
the field, and this round ran out before finding which branch runs.

**Reverted.** Everything green, and the hole is written down with its number: the hardest shape this
project can record is perfect to describe and 76% wrong to draw.
## Two rounds of branch-hunting, no fix, and the method that would have found it

I tried twice to record the divider's column so the renderer could align two full panes. Both times the
page came back with `pane_divider: null`, so the field was never set by the branch that runs — and both
times I had patched a branch that looked like the one, because the trace said `divider=true` and the
literal in front of me set `panes`.

```
two agents round trip: 322/1351 line(s) of content came back (23.8%), 1351 drawn
```

**What I should have done first, and will do next:** a unit test that builds the page for one frame of
the two-agent recording and asserts `pane_divider` is set. That answers "which branch runs" in one
second and in the same language as the fix, instead of reading four literals and guessing. Black-box
probing through the CLI was the wrong instrument for an internal question — three probes, three wrong
answers, two rounds.

Reverted again; the tree is green and the number is unchanged. The hole stands at 23.8%, now with the
method for closing it written down beside it.
## The test answered the question in one second, and the answer moves the hole

The round-190 note said to write the test first. Doing that took one second and answered what three CLI
probes could not:

```
BRANCH: container branch
test result: ok. 1 passed
```

The `crates/capture/tests/two_agents.rs` test (written for one round and afterwards removed — it broke CI
on three platforms, because it read a fixture that is not committed) built the hardest recording through
the same entry the
CLI uses and asserts it is two panes. With the field added, it also asserts the divider is recorded —
**and passes**, so the page does carry the column.

The renderer still draws the two-agent screen back at 23.8% **with the divider column and padding in
place**, and looking at a rendered row explains why:

```
  \\wsl.localhost\Ubuntu\tm… 25K / 500K │ [Dashboard]  │     > y    2:06 AM
```

Both divider glyphs are there. What differs is the spacing *around* them: the screen's dividers stand at
columns the page never records, and pane text is right-trimmed before it goes in, so the merged row can
put the divider in the right place only by luck. **Recording the width is necessary and not sufficient**
— the accurate fix has to record the glyph itself, which is the "column-only rows" limit named in round
155, now measured: 76% of rows.

Reverted once more. The test stays in the tree as an untracked file and the hole is unchanged.
## 23.8% to 100%: the renderer was stacking panes, not placing them

Four rounds. The answer was in the first probe, once the probe was pointed at the *right* renderer.

The round-trip metric calls `render_terminal`, not the reader's `render_text`. Drawn with the reader's
own renderer, the hardest screen matched **line for line**; drawn with `render_terminal`, it lost 76% of
its lines. That function's pane branch took each pane's components and **stacked them in order**,
ignoring the row each component claims — so two full panes overlapped themselves. There was even a
`_padding` variable computed and never used, which is what three of those rounds were spent staring at.

The fix is one branch:

```rust
if !page.create_surface.panes.is_empty() {
    return ui_lm_pages::render_text(page).join("\n");
}
```

A paned page is now drawn by the reader's renderer, which places every component at the row it claims —
the logic that would otherwise have been duplicated a second time.

```
two agents:        1351/1351 line(s) of content came back (100.0%), 1351 drawn
two agents model:  1351/1351 line(s) of content came back (100.0%), 1351 drawn
```

Every curated axis still green; all three hard recordings at 100%.

**What the four rounds cost, and what they bought:** two of them were spent patching the schema because
I assumed the loss was in the page, and two more guessing which branch built it. The probe that ended it
took ten minutes and printed the failing rows — the instrument I should have built first, twice.
## The hard cases are asked to draw back, not only to agree

`check-hard` compared the model's page with the reader's, which is a question about **agreement** — and
two full panes agreed perfectly while drawing back at 23.8% for four rounds. Every check in the suite
was green throughout, because none of them asked the second question.

It asks now, and it can fail:

```
wsl-two-agents-in-split draws back at 23.8% (round trip: 322/1351 line(s), 1351 drawn)
a known-hard screen got worse; that is a regression, not a gap
```

That is the output with the fix disabled. With it in place, all three recordings are 100% on both axes.

**The lesson is in the shape of the fix, not the bug:** the suite had a hole exactly where two
correct-looking numbers met — agreement was 100%, round trip was 23.8%, and only one of those was being
watched.
## v0.12.0: the archive draws the hardest screen back at 100%

```
every check passed in 471.6s

checking release: v0.12.0
round trip: 1351/1351 line(s) of content came back (100.0%), 1351 drawn
```

That second line is **the downloaded build** drawing the two-agent recording — the screen that was
23.8% two days ago. The fix is in the binary, not only in the tree.

What ships since v0.11.1:

- **a paned page is drawn by the renderer that places rows**, not by one that stacked components: the
  harness's own renderer had never handled two full panes;
- **`check-hard` now asks whether a hard screen draws back**, not only whether the model agrees with the
  reader — the hole that let 23.8% sit behind eighteen green checks;
- **two agents in two panes** joins the hard recordings at 100% on both axes.
## A sixth clean batch, after the renderer changed under it

```
model 100.0%  floor 44.4%  over 82 frame(s)
  Block 352/352 · Meter 51/51 · ToolCall 54/54
```

**476 frames across seven batches, nothing missed** — including a batch recorded the same week the
terminal renderer changed for paned pages. That is the point of recording new material after a change:
the older recordings would have passed either way, because they were made before it.

Also verified this round: **the live viewer draws panes.** It renders through `render_page`, which
learned the `.panes` grid in v0.11.1; a served two-agent recording shows the wrapper and both pane divs.
The `/state` document carries the newest page, which need not be a paned one, so absence of panes there
is not a defect — checked rather than assumed, because this is exactly where the container has gone
missing twice before.

And the decision behind the fix is now recorded as `docs/adr/0005-paned-pages-drawn-by-the-reader.md`:
one renderer places rows, the other stacks them, and for paned pages the readable one delegates to the
one that knows where the rows are.
## Any screen at all: 416 of them, no panic, no invalid page

Every other test in this repository is given a screen that means something. The recorder runs next to
programs nobody chose, and a page built from a build log or a stack trace is still a page a renderer
will be handed.

`crates/pages/tests/fuzz_screens.rs` walks sixteen deliberate shapes — empty, one cell, boxed tables,
ragged indentation, wide characters, a 500-column row, control characters — and four hundred
pseudo-random ones built from pieces of the vocabulary. Two things are asked of each:

```
test result: ok. 1 passed; 0 failed
```

- **it returns** (no panic on any shape), and
- **`audit` finds nothing wrong** with what it returned.

**Proven able to fail** by making the audit refuse every page:

```
416 screen(s) produced a page the audit refuses
screen 0: "mutation: every page is refused"
```

It lives with `cargo test`, so the gate runs it on every commit — a property that costs ten milliseconds
and covers the case the project has no control over: what a program decides to draw.
## The same lens on the renderers: 300 nonsense pages, nothing dropped

If a nonsense screen can make a page, that page can be handed to a renderer. So the second fuzz test
builds three hundred pages from random screens and asks two things of each:

```
test result: ok. 1 passed; 0 failed
```

- **both renderers return** — text for a terminal, a document for a browser;
- **every word the page holds reaches the document** — the same claim `check-html` makes of the corpus,
  asked of material nothing chose.

**Proven able to fail** by making `render_component` write nothing:

```
1637 page(s) drew badly:
screen 0: "Read" reached the page but not the document
```

1,637 dropped words, and the test names the first one. Both fuzz tests run under `cargo test`, so the
gate runs them on every commit and CI runs them on three platforms.
## The learned path, given three hundred screens it has never seen

The model is the component most likely to meet something unfamiliar: a classifier trained on one
application's screens, pointed at whatever a program draws. So it got the same treatment as the reader
and the renderers.

```
300 page(s) from the model fail the audit:   (with the audit forced to refuse)
test result: ok. 1 passed; 0 failed          (as it stands)
```

Three hundred random screens built from the vocabulary's pieces — including a meter's `25K / 500K`, a
running-tools line, wide characters, tabs and control pieces — and every page it made passed `audit`.
No panic, no duplicate id, no span past the end of its line, no catalogue it does not know.

**Proven able to fail** by making the audit refuse every page, which reports all three hundred at once.

Three fuzz properties now sit under `cargo test`, covering the three stages a screen passes through:
the reader, the renderers, and the model. **The material nobody chose is measured at every stage.**
## The guard, tested end to end on the screens that caused it

Two recordings of the same program, which prints `日本語`:

| screen | before the guard | now |
|---|---|---|
| **3x3** | fine | `recorded 1 frame(s)`, screen holds `本` / `語` |
| **1x1** | panic on the recording thread | `recorded 0 frame(s)` — no crash, no frames |

**A 1x1 screen still produces nothing useful**, and that is the honest description: the emulator's panic
happens while it is drawing the glyph that does not fit, so the bytes are dropped and the screen stays
empty. What the guard buys is that the *process* survives — a recording that would have died now
produces an empty session, which is a fact someone can act on, instead of a crash on the way to one.

The 3x3 case is the one that can occur in practice: a pane narrow enough that a wide glyph has to wrap.
It records, and the screen holds both glyphs.

Both probes also showed the value of the recorder's own `running:` line, which caught my quoting twice in
one round — the argument list printed `printf \346\227\245...` literally, so the shell printed the digits
instead of the characters. **A diagnostic added a fortnight ago has now paid for itself twice over.**
## v0.12.1: a recording survives the emulator falling over

```
every check passed in 470.1s

checking release: v0.12.1
recorded 1 frame(s) to ... (the program exited)
frames from the archive: 1
```

That is the downloaded build recording a 3x3 screen — the size at which a wide glyph wraps — and
producing a session from it.

What ships since v0.12.0:

- **the recorder no longer dies when `vt100` panics** — a wide glyph in a screen too narrow to hold it
  killed the recording thread; the bytes are now dropped and the session survives;
- **four fuzz properties**, which is what found it: 416 reader screens, 300 renderer pages, 300 model
  pages, 300 byte streams — material no person chose.
## Damaged recordings: skipped line by line, refused when nothing is readable

A recorder that is killed mid-write leaves a truncated last line. A tool whose whole job is reading files
written by another process should expect that, and until now the suite never produced one.

```
truncated last line: 37 of 38 frame(s) read, exit 0
nothing parseable:   exit 1
damaged recordings are skipped line by line and refused when nothing is readable
```

Three behaviours, all measured: a bad line is **reported and skipped** rather than taking the file with
it; a file with nothing readable **exits non-zero** rather than pretending; and nothing panics.

**Proven able to fail** by changing the wording of the skip message — which is how the check learns that
the tool still says why:

```
nothing said the frame was skipped
a damaged recording was not handled
```

`check-everything -Quick` now runs **seventeen checks in 75 seconds**.
## Every check green, and the one that took fifty-four minutes

```
every check passed in 3455.6s
  fresh  ok  3249.9s  fresh material matched the model exactly
```

Seventeen checks, all passing — and the fresh-material check took **twelve times longer than usual**
(3,250 s against ~265 s) while still producing a perfect score. The other sixteen were unaffected, which
is what says the machine was not simply slow: `render-scale` and `live-page` ran at their normal times.

So the variance lives in the one check that **starts a program and waits for it**: the agent under tmux,
whose startup is outside this project's control. Nothing was measured wrongly — the pages and the score
are identical — but a check that can take twelve times as long is a check that will eventually be
mistaken for a hang.

**Not fixed this round, recorded instead.** The recorder already bounds a scenario with `--stop-after`;
what is missing is a bound on the *check*, so that a slow start reports "this took too long" rather than
running for an hour and looking like progress.
## The slow check has a clock now

A batch normally records in about four minutes; one took fifty-four. Nothing bounded the *batch* — the
recorder bounds each scenario, but a session that never settles looks exactly like a slow one.

`check-fresh` now times the recording phase against a twenty-minute limit — four times the usual, long
enough for a slow machine and short enough to mean something:

```
recording the batch took 262 s, beyond the 1 s limit
a scenario that starts a program can hang, and this check would rather say so than wait
the log is here: ...\ui-lm-fresh-....log
```

That is the output with the limit lowered to one second to prove the guard fires. At twenty minutes it
says nothing, and the batch recorded in its usual 262 seconds.

**What this buys:** a hang produces a failure in twenty minutes that names the recording phase and the
log, instead of a run that sits for an hour looking like work.
## The second agent is not on this machine, and now that is a fact rather than an assumption

The one claim this project cannot test is that its vocabulary generalises beyond the application it was
trained on. That claim needs a second agent's screens, and this round went looking:

```
for p in claude aider codex gemini chatgpt llm ollama opencode; do command -v $p ...; done
```

Nothing in WSL, nothing on the Windows side. The machine has one agent — the one the vocabulary was
built for — and no second one to compare against.

**Recorded so the gap is a measured fact**: this is not "we assume it generalises", it is "we looked for
the material in every place it could be and it is not here". Anything else that renders *state* is still
welcome: the catalogue describes panels, tabs, tool calls and meters, and a program with those shapes
tests the same vocabulary even if it is not an agent.

Also recorded this round: **nano**, a full-screen editor, reads as one Block holding the file's text —
which is right for the vocabulary as scoped. An editor's state *is* a text buffer; its border and status
line are decoration around one thing, not several components.
## A split with no agent in it: vim side by side, 100% both ways

Every paned recording so far had an agent on one side. This one has a text editor with two buffers:

```
frames 5, paned frames 2
score      100.0%  floor 50.0%   Block 6/6
round trip 80/80 line(s) of content came back (100.0%), 81 drawn
```

**The gate split it**, and it did not have to be told twice: vim's status lines and `~` markers give the
panes enough shape for `panes_hold_structure` to say yes, and the two buffers come back in the right
columns. The round trip is exact on a screen whose content is nothing but text.

That is the fourth hard recording and the first with **no meter, no tool call and no reasoning line** —
just two files side by side. It is now in `check-hard` at 100% on both axes, so a future change to the
gate or the renderer has to keep it that way.
## Both builders must draw back, and the disabled renderer explains the four rounds

`check-hard` measured the *reader's* round trip and the *model's score*, never the model's round trip.
It does now, and with the paned renderer disabled on purpose it says:

```
wsl-agent-in-split      the model's pages draw back at 48.3%
wsl-two-agents-in-split the model's pages draw back at 23.8%
```

**48.3% is the number that explains four rounds.** With one structured pane beside a shell, a renderer
that stacked components still recovered about half the lines — enough to look like a rendering quirk and
not a defect. Only the two-agent screen, where both panes are full, fell to 23.8% and made it obvious.

So the defect was visible for weeks as "half wrong" and read as "fine, mostly" — the two-full-panes
recording is what turned a smell into a number. Both builders are now required to draw back at 100%, and
that requirement was made to fail before it was trusted.
## The first red build in weeks, and why it was the check working

```
1 of 16 check(s) failed in 471.2s
  diff-fresh: a fresh diff was not read exactly
```

Run alone, it said why:

```
diff components: 0
model 100.0%  floor 15.5%  over 53 frame(s), all four kinds it held, nothing missed
no diff appeared, so the check measured nothing
```

**The guard did exactly what it was built for.** The agent answered without running `git diff`, so no
diff reached the screen; the check refused to pass on nothing rather than reporting a perfect score for
material that could not test the fifth kind.

The cause is variance in what the agent does, not a defect in this project. So the scenario is now
attempted up to three times, and a run with no diff is **retried rather than believed**:

```
attempt 1: the agent did not show a diff, retrying
a diff appeared on attempt 2
diff components: 2   Diff 2/2 reproduced   model 100.0%
```

If all three attempts produce nothing, the check still fails — the retry removes the noise without
removing the guard.
## v0.13.0: the archive draws every hard shape back at 100%

```
every check passed in 471.3s

checking release: v0.13.0
  wsl-agent-in-pane        100.0%
  wsl-agent-in-split       100.0%
  wsl-two-agents-in-split  100.0%
  wsl-vim-in-split         100.0%
```

Those four lines are the **downloaded build** drawing each hard recording back onto its own screen. An
agent in a pane, an agent beside a shell, two agents, and two text buffers — all exact.

What ships since v0.12.1:

- **a clock on the slow check** — a batch that hangs fails in twenty minutes and names its log, instead
  of sitting for an hour looking like work;
- **both builders required to draw hard screens back**, not only the reader's — the assertion that would
  have caught the paned renderer at 48.3% instead of waiting for 23.8%;
- **a fourth hard recording**: vim with two buffers, the first hard case with no meter, tool call or
  reasoning line;
- **a retry for the agent's variance** — a run with no diff is retried rather than believed, and still
  fails if three attempts produce nothing.
## A seventh clean batch

```
model 100.0%  floor 42.2%  over 87 frame(s)
  Block 379/379 · Meter 58/58 · Thought 11/11 · ToolCall 45/45
```

**476 frames across seven batches, nothing missed.** Every one recorded after the model was trained, and
every one scored against the code that ships — including the batches recorded either side of the paned
renderer changing underneath them.
## An overlay, measured: it degrades to text and loses nothing

The state page lists "some shapes are not representable — a floating window with a border is a panel, one
without is a line of words". That is a claim about what the page *says*; this round measured what it
*costs*.

Recorded vim with a help window over a buffer — a real overlay, drawn on top of other content:

```
frames 5   kinds: Column, then one Block   panes: 1
score      100.0%  floor 100.0%   Block 10/10
round trip 74/74 line(s) of content came back (100.0%), 75 drawn
```

**The overlay is read as a block of text, and every line of it draws back exactly.** Nothing is lost and
nothing is misattributed: the page does not claim the help window is a separate surface, and no consumer
is misled about where the lines are.

That is the right shape of a limitation. A kind that *cannot* be expressed costs fidelity; a kind that
degrades to text costs structure and keeps content. **This one is the second kind**, and it is now
measured rather than assumed — which is the difference between a known limit and an excuse.

## An eighth clean batch

```
model 100.0%  floor 44.2%  over 82 frame(s)
  Block 366/366 · Meter 53/53 · Thought 15/15 · ToolCall 44/44
```

**558 frames across eight batches, nothing missed** — every batch recorded after training, every one scored against the code that ships.
## The repository is eleven megabytes, and every check is green

```
every check passed in 473.6s
.git: 11.2 MB
largest tracked file: docs/reports/2026-10-28-model.md at 0.18 MB
```

A project that records terminals could bloat easily — every frame of every batch is a file. It has not:
the recordings that ship are the curated corpus and four hard cases, the experimental batches live in
`%TEMP%` and are deleted by the checks that make them, and the whole history of 350-odd commits weighs
eleven megabytes.

That matters for the same reason the checks matter: **a clone has to be cheap enough that somebody will
make one.** A repository nobody clones is a repository nobody verifies.

## A number written in words escapes the check that counts digits

`check-docs` compares the script count in the documents with the folder, and it matches digits. The review
said **"seventeen scripts"** — in words — while the folder held eighteen, and the check passed happily.

Fixed the sentence. **Not fixed the check**: a regex that also accepts English numerals would have to know
where a number ends, and the honest tradeoff is that a phrase containing "seventeen" is still prose. What
is worth keeping is the observation itself: **the same slip that made this number wrong three times in
digits found one more way to be wrong in words.**
## The README, claim by claim

Two rounds were spent checking what the README tells a stranger. What was wrong, and what was right:

| claim | verdict |
|---|---|
| the four commands it documents | **missing four more** — `audit`, `fields`, `disagree`, `baseline` added |
| `ui-lm-serve --model` | **right** — the flag exists; my suspicion that it did not was wrong |
| "trained on 270 screens" | **wrong** — the model reports 434 |
| "four batches recorded after training" | **wrong** — eight |
| "141 frames from software it was never trained on" | **wrong** — the seven sets hold 185; replaced with a pointer to `check-all` |
| "150 ms of quiet" | **right** — `QUIESCE_GAP` is exactly 150 ms |

**Two of six claims were correct, four were stale, and one of my two attempts to "fix" a claim was itself
wrong.** That ratio is worth keeping: a sweep that only records the errors overstates how broken things
are, and a sweep that assumes its suspicions are right *creates* errors. The README now says what the
checks say, and sends the reader to the check wherever a number would drift.
## The another-application procedure, run on the only other applications here

The state page now tells a reader how to test the vocabulary on material from another program. So it was
run, on every non-Grok recording on this machine:

| recording | model | floor | round trip |
|---|---|---|---|
| banner | 100.0% | 50.0% | 100.0% |
| edits | 100.0% | 33.3% | 100.0% |
| listing | 100.0% | **100.0%** | 100.0% |
| os-listing | 100.0% | **100.0%** | 100.0% |
| panel | 100.0% | **100.0%** | 100.0% |
| prose | 100.0% | **100.0%** | 100.0% |
| report | 100.0% | 33.3% | 100.0% |
| table | 100.0% | **100.0%** | 100.0% |

Every one is perfect on both axes — **and five of the eight prove almost nothing**, because a floor of
100% means the text-only converter already reproduced the screen exactly. Those screens carry one block
of words and no structure.

The three that do carry structure (banner, edits, report) are the three with real floors, and all three
are perfect. That is the honest summary of the non-Grok evidence: **three recordings that could fail, and
none of them did.**

Which is exactly the trap this project has documented before — a set everybody passes has stopped
working — and the reason the fresh-material batches exist.
## The trap now announces itself

Running the another-application procedure exposed something the project had documented but never said at
the point of reading: five of the eight non-Grok recordings have a **floor of 100%**, so a perfect model
score on them proves nothing at all.

`score` now says so:

```
model 100.0%  floor 100.0%  over 1 frame(s), trained on 434 screen(s)
  the floor reproduces this set exactly, so a perfect score here proves nothing
  Block      2/2 reproduced
```

and stays quiet on a set where the floor is 33%:

```
model 100.0%  floor 33.3%  over 1 frame(s), trained on 434 screen(s)
  Block      2/2 reproduced
  Diff       1/1 reproduced
```

**The floor existed to make this visible and was read past.** A warning beside the number is worth more
than a table in a document, because it arrives exactly when someone is about to believe a perfect score.
## v0.14.0: the tool refuses to flatter

```
every check passed in 472.8s

checking release: v0.14.0
  model 100.0%  floor 100.0%  over 1 frame(s), trained on 434 screen(s)
  the floor reproduces this set exactly, so a perfect score here proves nothing
```

That second block is the **downloaded build** scoring a non-Grok recording. The warning is in the binary.

What ships since v0.13.0: `score` tells you when a set cannot distinguish the model from a converter that
splits paragraphs — five of the eight non-Grok recordings are in that state, and until this release the
only way to know was to read a table in a document.

## Re-measured: the divider-column limitation is not demonstrated

The closing notes said a pane row that holds only a divider "loses its structure while keeping its text".
Two days of work later that needed checking rather than repeating, so it was measured on the two paned
hard recordings:

```
wsl-vim-in-split         frame 1:  20/20 rows covered by a component
wsl-two-agents-in-split  frame 14: 20/20 rows covered by a component
```

**Every row of both screens is described**, the divider glyphs sit inside the components' own text (the
meter's text is `... 25K / 500K │ [Dashboard]  │`), and both recordings round trip at **100%**. So the
limitation, as written, is not demonstrated by any material this project has.

That does not prove there is no such row — a split whose panes are blank on both sides at once would be
the case, and nothing recorded contains one. But **a limitation nobody can produce a failing screen for
belongs in the "unmeasured" column, not the "limitation" column**, and it has been moved there.

## Freebuff is a desktop application, not a terminal program

The second Agent CLI the project needed turns out to be here already — as a **GUI**:

```
C:\Users\moreweb\AppData\Local\Programs\@codebufffreebuff-desktop\Freebuff.exe
resources/app.asar              27.9 MB   Electron
resources/orchestrator/ui/      index.html, assets   a Chromium window
resources/bun/bun.exe           84 MB     the runtime the agent runs in
```

There is no `freebuff` or `codebuff` on PATH in WSL or on Windows, and the bundled
`orchestrator.js` has no terminal mode — the `process.argv` references in it are Emscripten
boilerplate, not a CLI.

**This matters for the generality claim, and not in the way I expected.** The rig records Frames of
Cells from a TUI. Freebuff draws Widgets in a Chromium window, so the rig cannot record it. The second
agent is on the **output** side of this project (what a runtime would render), not the input side.

So the claim stays open, but the reason changes: it is not "no second agent exists here" any more, it is
"the second agent is a desktop app, and this rig reads terminals". Training on it would need a different
capture path — the app's own event stream, not a Frame — which is new work, not a new recording.

## The second agent is here, and the vocabulary carried

Freebuff — a different Agent CLI, installed from npm, logged in from its own desktop config — records as a TUI and was captured on the first attempt:

```
frames 28   kinds before: Block only (192)
score 99.2%  floor 52.4%  round trip 754/754 (100%)
```

Its tool call reads `• Read notes.txt`, a bullet where Grok draws `♦`. The reader knew one glyph, so freebuff's structure was invisible: every row became a block.

**One rule, in both builders** — a bullet marks a tool row only when the word after it starts with a capital letter, which is how both applications spell a tool name and how neither spells a list item — and the same marker decides Thought, with "Thinking" accepted beside "Thought":

```
freebuff   99.8%   Block 181/183   ToolCall 12/12      floor 35.0%
Grok sets  100.0%  every one       assembly 212 frames  hard cases 100%
```

**The floor fell from 52.4% to 35.0%**, which is the proof that matters: the screen now holds structure the
text-only converter cannot reproduce. This is the first evaluation set in the project that is not at
100%, and the first evidence that the five kinds exist outside the application they were learned from.

### The two remaining misses on freebuff, and a guard that did not take

`disagree` named them: the model calls **Freebuff's ASCII logo** a reasoning run.

```
reader  model    row
Block   Thought  ███▀▀▀▀▀▀▀▀                        ██                 ██    ██▀
Block   Meter    MiMo v2.6 Flash Fast · … · Chat: New chat
```

The reader is right and the model is wrong: it has never seen a full block in its training screens,
so five logo rows read as reasoning and one status row as a meter.

I tried the project's usual fix — a structural guard in the label path that demotes a "thought" run with
no letters in it — and **it had no effect**: `score` stayed at 99.8%. The guard was reverted rather than
left in the tree pretending to work.

That is worth knowing precisely: `settled()` is not the place where these labels are corrected, or these
rows do not reach it as one run. Finding the real hook is the next piece of work, and it is the kind of
question a test answers in one second and a probe does not.

### Training on freebuff changed nothing, and that is the finding

Two freebuff sessions recorded (27 of 27 tool calls detected on the second). 100 structured pairs
exported from all 58 recordings, a model trained on them with 600 epochs, and then the comparison:

```
second-agent     shipped 99.8%   trained on 100 screens 99.8%
second-agent-2   shipped 99.6%   trained on 100 screens 99.6%
```

**Identical.** The extra application taught the model nothing it did not already do, because the pages are
not produced by the model — the reader and its guards produce them, and the model's labels are corrected
before they become components. The shipped weights were kept.

So "train on freebuff" has an answer, and it is not the one the plan expected: **the bottleneck for a new
application is the reader's vocabulary, not the model's weights.** Freebuff needed exactly one new rule
(a bullet marks a tool row) and that rule is code, not training data.

The remaining difference on the second session is 17.3% of rows and 22 components, and `disagree` names
them as Freebuff's block art (the model reads the logo as reasoning) plus one status row read as a meter.
The label path already demotes a thought that the reader would not call one, so **why these rows survive
that guard is not yet known** — that is the next question, and it needs a direct look at the model's page
rather than another probe from outside.

### The 22 misses are a reader bug, and the second agent found it

Comparing the two pages frame by frame, the difference is not a kind at all — it is where a block ends:

```
reader:  Block:0-1  Block:1-2  …    (two blocks)
model :  Block:0-2  …               (one block)
```

And the rows say which is right:

```
row 0   │ 2 day streak                              │
row 1   └───────────────────────────────────────────┘
row 2     [09:39 AM]
```

**The reader splits a panel's last content row from its own bottom border.** The model keeps them
together, because the label path has a guard that says a box is one thing — the same rule was added there
after a popup's closing border was split into a block of its own.

So the model is right and the teacher is wrong, on Freebuff and on every screen with a bordered panel whose
last row is not its border. Grok's screens hide it: the model copies the reader, so both agree, and the
score stays at 100% while the page is wrong in the same way twice.

**This is the first defect the second application has found that the first one could not**, and it is in
the reader — the component the whole assembly trusts. The fix belongs in `segments()` where a closing
border should continue the stretch that the opening border began; it needs a test in the reader's own
terms, and it changes labels, so it needs a retrain and a full re-measure afterwards.

### The split is not in `segments`, and the test that proved it

I rebuilt the two Freebuff rows as text and asked the reader for a page:

```
fixture:  │ 2 day streak … │
          └─────────────────┘
          [09:39 AM] …        five rows of an answer

page_from_text → Block 0..6        one block, no split
page_from_frame (the recording) → Block 0..1, Block 1..2   the split
```

Same rows, two paths, different grouping. So the defect is **not** in `segments()`, where the box rules
live — it is in the styled path that the recorder uses, which decides boundaries again once spans are
present.

That matters because it is the second time a rule has had two implementations (the pane-split gate was
the first, and cost six rounds). The text path merges the border with its panel; the styled path splits
it. **A rule that exists twice will disagree with itself**, and here the record is clear about which one
is right: the model, reading the unstyled rows, keeps them together.

The failing test was removed rather than committed red; the fixture is preserved in this note verbatim,
so the next attempt starts from the exact bytes. Next: find the styled path's boundary decision and make
it call the same rules `segments()` uses, then re-measure both applications.

## The fix, and the regression it caused first

The defect was in `segments()` after all: a closing border with no opening in the frame got a stretch of
its own. Fixed by joining it to the panel above — the row above is drawn as one (`│ … │`) — and that
took both Freebuff recordings to 100%.

**It broke `cargo-tree` in the same commit**: a tree branch also starts with `└` (`└── crates/`), and the
new rule merged branches into the block above, costing 6.7 points. The second attempt requires the row to
end with a corner (`┘`, `╯`) and so carry no name:

```
apps       100.0%   (cargo-tree restored)
freebuff   100.0%   over 65 frames
every set  100.0%   assembly 250 frames   hard cases 100%
```

Two lessons, both already in the record and both paid for again: **a grouping rule moves every page**, so
one measurement is never enough; and the second application is what made this visible — Grok's own corpus
had the same defect on both builders, agreeing with itself at 100%.

## A third Freebuff session, and one rule that was written twice

The third recording asks Freebuff to write a file and then run a command that fails. It does both:
32 frames, a ToolCall for the write, the failing exit code in a block.

```
freebuff   100.0%  over 96 frames (three sessions)   Block 231/231   ToolCall 21/21
apps/tui/unseen/workflow/…  100.0% each              assembly 281 frames   0 contradictions
```

While reading it, one more rule turned out to exist twice: the reader accepted only `thought` as a reasoning
word, while the label path already accepted `thinking` — so Freebuff's `• Thinking` rows were tool calls to
one path and reasoning to the other. Both now call `is_thought_word`, and every set stayed at 100%.

**But Freebuff still produces no `Thought` component.** Its reasoning is drawn expanded — `• Thinking` on
one row and the words on the rows below — so the bullet row is not a lone call, and the reader's rule for
`Thought` is "a run with exactly one call in it". Whether that rule should also cover an expanded heading is
the open question; it is not a bug in either path, it is a decision nobody has made.

### Why Freebuff's reasoning is still a tool call: the parse strips one glyph

The component says it plainly:

```
kind=ToolCall  calls=[{ "tool": "•", "target": "Thinking", "text": "  • Thinking" }]
```

`parse_tool_line` strips `TOOL_GLYPH` — the diamond — and nothing else, so a bullet row is split at its
first space and the *glyph itself* becomes the tool name. Every Freebuff tool row has been parsed this way:
they look like `ToolCall`s because that is the fallback for any run of marked rows, and the thought never
appears because its tool word is `•`, not `Thinking`.

`tool_marker` — added for the bullet — knows both glyphs; `parse_tool_line` never learned about it. **That is
the same defect as the pane gate, the border and the thought word, for the fourth time: one rule, two
readers.** The fix is one line in `parse_tool_line` (strip whatever `tool_marker` matched), and it was not
finished this round: the edit tool kept refusing a file that `cargo fmt` had touched, and a half-applied
change is worse than none.

The tree is clean and green; the next round starts with that one line.

### The bullet fix works, and it cannot ship alone

Stripping whatever `tool_marker` matched in `parse_tool_line` does what it should:

```
second-agent    Thought 3    ToolCall 9
second-agent-2  Thought 27   (those were never tool calls — every `•` row there is `• Thinking`)
second-agent-3  Thought 12   ToolCall 9
```

**Freebuff's reasoning appears for the first time in a non-Grok application.** And the same commit drops
freebuff's score to 72.4% and the assembly check to 90.6%, because the label path groups those rows
differently: the model puts the heading and the words under it in one run, the reader does not. A guard
that promotes a lone heading cannot fix a run that is not lone.

So both changes were reverted and the tree is back to 100% everywhere. **The lesson is the one this
project keeps re-learning at a higher cost each time: a rule that decides grouping must change on both
paths in the same commit, and only then can it be measured.** The next attempt starts with `runs()` in the
label path, not with the parse.

What is left to do, precisely: make the label path break a run at a reasoning heading (the same boundary
the reader's segmenter makes), then apply the parse fix, then measure. That is one change, not two.

### Where the label path actually decides a kind — not where I looked

Three changes were made together this round: the parse fix, a run boundary at a reasoning heading, and a
promotion in `settled`. All three built, and the model's page still said:

```
reader   Thought 3..4      model   Block 3..4
```

So `settled` is not the function that assigns that row's kind, or it is not called for that run. **The
label path has more than one place where a kind is decided**, and I have been patching one of them.

That is the fifth time this month that a rule turned out to live in two places. The difference is that this
time the measurement said so immediately — `Thought 0/27` on a page whose reader said `Thought 27` — instead
of after a week.

Everything was reverted; the tree is at 100% again. The next move is not another guess: it is to make the
label path print the kind it assigns for one row, or to call `labeled_page` directly in a test with known
labels and see which branch runs.

### A test in ten seconds, three patches, and a fourth place

The test that names the problem:

```rust
#[test]
fn a_reasoning_heading_the_model_called_a_tool_becomes_a_thought() {
    let page = page_of(&["  • Thinking"], "ToolCall");
    assert_eq!(kinds(&page), vec!["Thought".to_owned()]);
}
```

Run against the tree, it fails — and each patch moved the answer without reaching it:

| patched | result |
|---|---|
| nothing | `["ToolCall"]` |
| `parse_tool_line` through `tool_marker` | still `["ToolCall"]` |
| promotion in `settled` | `["Block"]` |
| the guard that tests for `♦` | still `["Block"]` |

So the kind for a single `• Thinking` row is minted in a **fourth** place, after or beside all three. The
label path is 500 lines with kind strings at eleven sites; guessing among them has now cost this round and
the two before it.

**The next move is a trace print, not a patch.** One `eprintln!` in `labeled_page` showing the run's label,
its lines, and the kind it assigned — one run of the test, and the branch names itself. The month's record
says this every time it comes up, and the cost of ignoring it is now three rounds.

## Four places, one rule — and the second application now has thoughts

The instrument-first attempt found it in one pass: `body_for` carried **its own copy** of the reasoning
rule, and knew only `thought`, never `thinking`. That is the fourth site, after `parse_tool_line` (which
glyph), the ToolCall guard (which marker) and `settled` (the promotion).

All four ask the reader now, and the result is the first non-Grok application whose reasoning is described:

```
second-agent    Block 182/182  Thought 3/3    ToolCall 9/9    100.0%
second-agent-2  Block 325/325  Thought 27/27                  100.0%
second-agent-3  Block 231/231  Thought 12/12   ToolCall 9/9    100.0%

Grok: every set 100% · assembly 281 frames 100% · 1486 components, 0 contradictions, 0 differing
```

**Freebuff's 27 "tool calls" were 27 reasoning headings all along**, and Grok never noticed because its own
screens put the word `Thought` where freebuff puts `Thinking`.

The test that failed for two rounds now passes:

```rust
let page = page_of(&["  • Thinking"], "ToolCall");
assert_eq!(kinds(&page), vec!["Thought"]);
```

## All five kinds, on an application the model never saw

Two more sessions, both kept:

| session | frames | what it shows | result |
|---|---|---|---|
| second-agent-4 | 197 | long reasoning, a command that failed honestly | **100%**, Block 1728/1728, Thought 191/191 |
| second-agent-5 | 30 | a real `git diff` in a Windows repo | **100%**, Block 176/176, **Diff 15/15**, Thought 7/7 |

**Freebuff now exercises Block, ToolCall, Thought and Diff** — four of the five kinds, on screens from an
application the model was never trained on, all reproduced exactly. Only `Meter` is missing: freebuff shows
context use as `1h left · 15K (1%)`, and `as_meter` does not recognise that shape yet.

Two things that cost time and are worth remembering: freebuff runs on **Windows**, so a `/tmp` path from a
WSL shell does not exist for it — the first diff attempt failed for that reason and the agent said so
plainly; and a survey modal blocks the session until it is dismissed, which swallowed 77 frames of one
recording.

## The second agent has a check of its own

`check-agents` scores every Freebuff recording, draws each back through the model's pages, audits them, and
counts the kinds:

```
5 recording(s), 328 frame(s): Block=2642, Column=328, Diff=15, Thought=240, ToolCall=18
a second agent CLI reads perfectly, and was never trained on
```

It joins the suite, and it refuses to pass when there is nothing to score — proven by moving the recordings
away: *"no second-agent recordings on this machine, so nothing was exercised"*.

**`Meter` is the one kind Freebuff does not produce**, and the decision is recorded rather than forced: its
usage row reads `40m left · 15.4K (1%)` — a used figure and a percentage, with no limit anywhere on screen.
The catalogue's `Meter` is a use-and-limit pair, so a limit invented from the percentage would be a page
claiming something the screen does not say. It stays a `Block`.

Adding the check moved the counts everywhere — 19 checks, 66 recordings, 676 frames — and `check-docs` caught
each of the three stale numbers in turn, which is what it is for.

## The second-agent check cost what it should

It walked the same 328 frames four times — score, draw, audit, and a second `page --all` just to count kinds
— and the last pass was free to remove: `score` already prints a line per kind. One pass fewer, and the check
moved to the slow half of the suite so the working loop stays short:

```
check-agents   30 s     the suite: 19 checks, quick loop 160 s, full run ~10 min
```

## v0.15.0: a second agent, read without ever being trained on

```
every check passed in 610.4s

checking release: v0.15.0
  model 100.0%  floor 30.7%  over 30 frame(s), trained on 434 screen(s)
    Block 176/176 · Diff 15/15 · Thought 7/7 reproduced
```

That second block is the **downloaded build** reading Freebuff's `git diff` session — an application the
model has never seen, on the surface the model has never seen either.

What ships since v0.14.0:

- **a bullet marks a tool row** (`•` beside `♦`), narrow enough that a list item is not a tool;
- **a reasoning heading is a Thought** in both applications, in all four places the rule lived;
- **a closing border joins the panel above it**, and only a border does — tree branches are not borders;
- **`check-agents`** — five recordings of a second agent CLI, scored, drawn back, audited, and counted;
- and the guard against the whole class: the label path asks the reader instead of keeping its own copy.

## A third application: `top`, and the first time a floor of 100% appeared in earnest

Recorded interactive `top` — a live table, box drawing, a memory summary — and it reads as one block:

```
8 frames, round trip 147/147 (100%)
model 100.0%  floor 100.0%
  the floor reproduces this set exactly, so a perfect score here proves nothing
```

**That warning was written for exactly this moment and had never fired on real material.** The screen is a
table of words and numbers: the text-only converter reproduces it perfectly, so the score says nothing about
the model. Round trip still means something — every line of a live-updating screen comes back — and that is
the claim recorded.

`MiB Mem: 32099.7 total, 866.8 used` looks like a use-and-limit pair and is not one: it carries four figures
(total, free, used, buff/cache). A `Meter` is one use against one limit, so this row stays a block. Extending
`as_meter` to accept it would be inventing structure from a status line — the same mistake the narrow rules
in this project exist to prevent.

## How much structure each application actually yields

Counted over every corpus set — components per frame, and the share of frames holding more than one:

| set | frames | components/frame | frames with structure | kinds |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **freebuff** | 328 | **8.89** | **98%** | Block, Diff, Thought, ToolCall |
| unseen | 85 | 5.28 | 86% | Block, Meter, Thought, ToolCall |
| structured | 7 | 2.57 | 43% | Block, Diff |
| apps | 19 | 2.32 | 37% | Block, Diff |
| scratch | 19 | 1.95 | 95% | Block |
| nongrok | 8 | 1.88 | 62% | Block, Diff, Meter, ToolCall |
| tui | 84 | 1.56 | 56% | Block |
| workflow | 6 | 1.00 | 33% | Block |

**The second agent is the richest source of structure in the project — 8.89 components a frame, 98% of frames
structured — and the model was never trained on a single frame of it.** It is richer than Grok's own held-out
set, and it is read at 100%.

The bottom rows are the other half of the same fact: `tui` and `workflow` average one component a frame
because they are text — a pager, an editor, a command's output. **Structure appears where software renders
state; text appears where software prints.** That sentence now has a table behind it, measured on three
applications rather than asserted from one.

## A sixth session, and a recording that went wrong usefully

Asked Freebuff to run a command that fails and explain the exit code. It did not, and the reason is in the
transcript: my keystroke meant to dismiss a survey landed in the prompt instead, because no survey appeared
that time — `run a command that fails …means0`. The session is real screen material (37 frames, 309 blocks,
16 thoughts, 100%) and is kept as that, **not** as a failure demonstration.

The second-agent set now stands at:

```
6 recording(s), 359 frame(s): Block=2951, Diff=15, Thought=256, ToolCall=18
100% of frames hold more than one component, across 4 kind(s)
a second agent CLI reads perfectly, and was never trained on
```

## What a failure looks like, and what it must not become

A shell's `not found` and a Python traceback — indented frames, caret markers, file names in quotes:

```
starting
/tmp/ui-lm/fail.sh: 1: nonexistent-command: not found
---
Traceback (most recent call last):
  File "<string>", line 1, in <module>
    obj, end = self.raw_decode(s, idx=_w(s, 0).end())
               ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
```

The page describes it as **one Block of eleven lines**, and it draws back at 100%. None of it becomes a tool
call, a reasoning line or a meter, which is the right answer: a failure is text.

That is now a test — `a_traceback_is_one_block_and_nothing_else`, with the eleven lines kept as a fixture —
because the rules that recognise bullets, diamonds and box drawing all learn from *shapes*, and a traceback
is full of shapes it must ignore.

## The counterweight: a sentence that begins with "Thinking"

`• Thinking about the problem` is a list item. The rule that recognises reasoning was widened to two
applications last week, and it had begun to answer yes to that sentence — in **two** places at once, because
`body_for` still kept its own copy of the rule (the fifth time this month a rule has been written twice).

Two narrowings, both measured:

- a heading ends after its word, or names a duration — `♦ Thought for 3.0s`, `• Thinking`;
- and decoration is not speech: Freebuff draws a scrollbar in its last column, so the row is really
  `• Thinking … ▄`, and the block glyphs are stripped before the question is asked.

With both, every real heading returns and the sentence does not:

```
freebuff 100.0% on all three sessions, Thought 3/3, 12/12, 191/191
all sets 100%, assembly 551 frames
```

The test `a_bullet_that_starts_with_thinking_is_not_reasoning` holds the line. **Widening a rule for a second
application is what makes it over-reach; measuring the sentence is what keeps it honest.**

## The boundary the tool-call rule cannot cross

A bullet list at the margin reads as one tool call:

```
• Read the docs
• Write tests          → one ToolCall component, two calls
```

That is a real limit and it is now pinned by a test that says so. **It is also not a defect any application in
the corpus has shown**: every list a real program draws sits inside a panel — a changelog, a menu, a dialog —
and the box guard keeps those rows together as one `Block`. No rule here can tell `• Read the docs` from
`• Read notes.txt` without inventing a vocabulary of verbs, and a verb list would silently miss the next

The test is written to fail the moment the boundary moves, in either direction:

> either way it moves, this line stops being true and somebody has to decide why.

## A tree is not a box: three rules, two opinions, one screen

`cargo tree` draws

```
│   [dev-dependencies]
└── vt100 v0.15.2
```

and that screen became **17 components** — one per branch row — because `closes_box` matched any row starting
with `└`. Narrowing it to rows that also *end* in a corner halved the page to 8 components, and dropped the
score to 57.1%: the label path keeps a **third** rule (`named`, a row starting `└── `) that still splits at
every branch.

So the reader and the label path hold different opinions about what a tree is, and both are self-consistent.
Reverted; the tree is 17 components again and every set is 100%.

**Which is right is a decision, not a bug**: one block per branch is a fine description of a listing, and one
block for the whole tree is a fine description too. What is not fine is that the two builders disagree — and
the fix, as always this month, is to change both in the same commit and then measure. That is the next round's
work, with the 17 → 8 measurement already in hand.

## The tree question, settled: one listing is one block

Both paths changed in one commit, as the last three rounds kept insisting:

- `closes_box` requires the corner — a border ends in `┘`/`╯`, a branch carries a name;
- the label path asks that function instead of testing the first glyph;
- and `named` — the rule that made every `└── ` row start a run — is gone.

```
cargo tree   17 components → 8, and both builders now produce the same page
every set    100%   ·   assembly 551 frames   ·   3930 components, 0 contradictions
hard cases   100%   ·   second agent 359 frames, 4 kinds, 100% structured
```

**The floor on `cargo-tree` rose from 25% to 100%, and that is the honest cost of the decision**: a listing
read as one block is a less *differentiated* page than a listing read as seventeen. Structure per component is
not the goal; a faithful description is, and a tree is one thing. The measurement is recorded so that if a
consumer ever needs per-branch components, the trade is visible rather than rediscovered.

## Under different work, the kinds hold

Seven sessions now, and the seventh asks for something the others did not — search several files for TODO
markers and summarise each one:

```
52 frames   Block 386/386 · Thought 66/66 · ToolCall 66/66   100.0%   floor 13.7%
```

**Sixty-six tool calls and sixty-six thoughts in one session**, all reproduced, against a floor of 13.7% — the
lowest floor any recording has produced, which means it carries more structure than anything else here. The
question the round asked was whether the four kinds survive a different *kind of work*, and they do.

The set now:

```
7 recording(s), 410 frame(s): Block=3336, Diff=15, Thought=322, ToolCall=84
100% of frames hold more than one component, across 4 kind(s)
```

Three attempts were needed to record it, and both failures were the recorder's own diagnostics doing their
job: a script line may not wait longer than 60 seconds (*"a delay of 70000 ms is longer than the 60000 ms
maximum"*), and a prompt typed and Entered in the same keystroke stays in the input box — the two have to be
separate events.

## Which agent draws more structure, and why the number does not answer that

| set | frames | components/frame | kinds |
|---|---|---|---|
| **freebuff (untrained)** | 417 | **9.01** | Block, Diff, Thought, ToolCall |
| unseen (Grok) | 85 | 5.28 | Block, Meter, Thought, ToolCall |
| structured | 7 | 2.57 | Block, Diff |
| scratch | 19 | 1.95 | Block |
| nongrok | 8 | 1.88 | Block, Diff, Meter, ToolCall |
| apps | 19 | 1.84 | Block, Diff |
| tui | 84 | 1.56 | Block |
| workflow | 6 | 1.00 | Block |

Freebuff looks twice as dense as Grok's richest set, and **that number says more about me than about the
agents**: I asked Freebuff for long tasks — search several files, write one, show a diff — and those tasks
draw many components. The Grok `unseen` recordings are shorter sessions on simpler questions. Different work,
different density; the sample sizes differ by a factor of five as well.

**What survives that caveat is the thing worth keeping**: both applications produce Block, ToolCall, Thought
and Diff, and every one of those components is reproduced on material the model never saw. Density is a
property of the task; the kinds are a property of the vocabulary — and only the second is a claim this
project needs.

## The eighth session is a full fix cycle

Asked Freebuff to run a program that exits non-zero, find the bug and fix it. It ran it, read the failure, and
changed `return a - b` to `return a + b` — the file on disk is fixed:

```
57 frames   Block 445/445 · Diff 22/22 · Thought 23/23 · ToolCall 49/49   100.0%   floor 18.2%
```

**All four kinds in one recording, every component reproduced, on material the model never saw**, and the
screen described the change as diffs rather than as prose. The set:

```
8 recording(s), 466 frame(s): Block=3781, Diff=37, Thought=345, ToolCall=133
100% of frames hold more than one component, across 4 kind(s)
```

The recorder's own warning is worth keeping too: *"the program stopped with 1 scripted keystroke(s) still to
send, so the session is shorter than the script asked for"* — Freebuff exited on its own, and the capture said
so instead of pretending the script had completed.

## The first Freebuff screen the two builders disagree about

Twenty-six frames, a task that cannot succeed — run `flurble --version`, install nothing. The agent tried,
failed (`bash: flurble: command not found`, exit 127), and said so without inventing a version. And the score
is **99.7%**, the first Freebuff material that is not perfect:

```
frame 24  reader: … Block@12 Block@26 …
          model : … Block@12 Thought@17 Block@18 Block@26 …
```

Row 17 is `• Thinking … █` — a reasoning heading, and **the model is right while the reader folds it into the
block above**. Five frames carry the same disagreement, six blocks in all.

The recording is kept as a hard case (`freebuff-refusal`) with a measured baseline of 99.7 and a ceiling of 13
components, so the defect has a number to beat rather than a paragraph to forget. When the reader learns what
the model already knows, that number rises and the table says how far it had to come.

## The scrollbar was the bug all along

`freebuff-refusal` scored 99.7% because an advertisement panel stopped being a panel:

```
│ New API Access Deserves a Second Review.                    Ad [Report 2] │  █
╰────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╯  █
```

The border ends in `╯`, and then the screen draws a **scrollbar** in its last column. The round-26 rule asked
whether the line *ends* in a corner — and it does not, it ends in `█`. So the panel never closed, the reader
swallowed every row below into one block, and the `• Thinking` heading inside was never its own component.

`undecorated` now strips the block glyphs a terminal uses for bars before either rule asks its question — the
border rule and the heading rule share it.

```
freebuff-refusal  99.7% → 100.0%      every set 100%      assembly 658 frames
4995 components, 0 contradictions, 0 differing
```

**The lesson is narrower than "test your rules": a rule that asks about the end of a line is asking about a
column the screen owns.** A terminal paints its own furniture there, and that furniture is not part of what the
application said.

## The mirror case, measured rather than assumed

Yesterday's bug was a marker hidden *after* a border. The mirror is a marker hidden *behind* a leading glyph.
A scan of all 25,348 lines in the corpus found seven:

```
│   • Answered questions now have a blank line between them, except in compact mode
│  ♦ Thinking…                                              █│
```

Both are rows drawn **inside a panel** — Grok puts its changelog and its reasoning in boxes. And both builders
treat them the same way: the row belongs to the panel's block.

**That is a decision, and it is now pinned**: a panel is one thing, and its interior is described as part of
it. Nothing here needed fixing; what was missing was a sentence saying which answer the project had chosen.

## The rendered document was already covered, and now it says so

The round set out to find an untested seam — the second agent's pages through the HTML renderer — and found
none. `check-html` walks `eval` and `cases`, which is every recording there is:

```
recordings checked: 71 on both paths, words checked: 3090, paned pages: 0
  of those, 9 are recordings of a second agent CLI, which this project was never trained on
every line the page holds reached the document
```

**Nine Freebuff recordings, 500 frames, both paths — a thousand page renders — and every word reached the
document.** The check was right all along; what was missing was a sentence in its output saying which material
it had just proved something about.

The remaining seam is the **live viewer**, and it is not the same kind of thing: `ui-lm-serve` types back into
a running program through a control file, so it needs a live session, not a recording. That path is covered by
`check-live-page`, and extending it to a second agent would mean running Freebuff live under the viewer — a
thing to try deliberately, not to assert from a stored file.

## The layout was never the point

Every Freebuff recording so far ran in a scratch directory I had built for it. The ninth runs in a repository
it has never seen: two commits, a `.gitignore`-less worktree, an untracked log, and a filename with a
non-ASCII letter — `ünsorted data.csv`.

```
38 frames   Block 271/271 · Diff 10/10 · Thought 16/16   100.0%   floor 19.8%
9 recording(s), 503 frame(s): Block=4060, Diff=47, Thought=361, ToolCall=133
```

**Nothing about the reading depended on my layouts.** The reader found the same four kinds in a repository with
history, and the model — still trained only on Grok — reproduced every component.

That matters for a specific reason: a converter that worked only on directories its author prepared would be a
demonstration, not a converter. This one has now read nine sessions across four different kinds of work, in
repositories it did not build.

## The fix generalised: a second refusal, clean at 100%

Asked for something impossible a second time, and in a different shape — rename `config.yaml`, which does not
exist, then validate the YAML. The agent tried, failed, and reported:

```
50 frames   Block 402/402 · Thought 24/24 · ToolCall 25/25   100.0%
10 recording(s), 552 frame(s): Block=4462, Diff=47, Thought=385, ToolCall=158
```

**The first refusal needed the scrollbar fix; the second did not.** That is the answer to the question every
fix this month has had to face: the change was about a column the terminal owns, not about one screen, and the
next screen of the same kind arrived already correct.

Ten sessions now, and the set holds: every recording at 100%, four kinds, no screen under one component.

## The second shape of a diff: a rename

Every diff this project had read was the `+++`/`---` kind. The tenth session stages a move and an edit, so the
screen carries what git prints for a rename:

```
similarity index 72%
rename from old_name.txt
rename to new_name.txt
```

The reader calls it seven diff components — the hunk headers, the similarity line, the move — and the model
reproduces all seven:

```
29 frames   Block 190/190 · Diff 7/7   100.0%
11 recording(s), 580 frame(s): Block=4652, Diff=54, Thought=385, ToolCall=158
```

**Two shapes of the same kind, both read correctly, on an application the model has never seen.** The `Diff`
rule was written against one screen in one application; it has now survived a second application and a second
shape without a change.

## Why `Meter` is still a Grok-only kind, stated after looking

The round set out to prove `Meter` outside Grok. It could not, and the reason is worth keeping. The programs on
this machine that draw progress say:

```
40,000,000 100%    1.16GB/s    0:00:00 (xfr#1, to-chk=0/1)      rsync
MiB Mem :  32099.7 total,  30166.4 free,    866.8 used          top
% Total    % Received % Xferd  Average Speed                    curl
```

Each carries a **percentage**, a **count of items**, or **several figures at once** — and none prints a limit as
a number beside the used amount. `as_meter` wants a use-and-limit pair (`25K / 500K`) and refuses to invent the
limit from a percentage, which is the same decision recorded for Freebuff's `15.4K (1%)`.

So `Meter` is not Grok-specific by accident of rule-writing: **an agent CLI prints a budget because a budget is
the thing it manages.** A file copy prints a percentage because that is what a person wants to see. The kind is
real, and the applications that produce it are the ones that report use against a ceiling.

To test it elsewhere I would need a program that prints `used / limit` — a build tool with a step count, or a
watcher with a quota. That is a corpus question, not a code change.

## The documents that were still describing last week

Two files said things that stopped being true when the second agent was recorded, and neither is checked by a
script:

- **`docs/model-card.md`** listed "does not generalise beyond what it was trained on, and nobody has measured
  that it does" under *what it cannot do*, and its results table held three rows, none of them a second agent.
- **`HANDOFF.md`** listed "a corpus from a second agent" as one of the three things to do next.

Both now say what happened: eleven recordings, 580 frames, four kinds — Block 4652, ToolCall 158, Thought 385,
Diff 54 across two shapes — every component reproduced, and `check-agents` holding it. The model card grew a
section for the one claim the model exists to test, and the handoff moved that item from *next* to *done*
while keeping the three next steps honest (`Meter` from a non-agent, a `Frame` trigger, interaction).

**The lesson is the same one `check-docs` exists for, one level up**: the scripts verify counts and links, and
none of them can tell that a paragraph is describing a project that no longer exists.

## The README was the most wrong file in the repository

A stranger's first read, and it said:

- *"There is no `Table` or **`Thought`** yet"* — `Thought` has been a kind for weeks and appears 385 times in
  the second agent's recordings alone;
- *"**`Diff`** — No screen in the corpus has drawn one yet … the detector is tested against the shape rather
  than a capture"* — the corpus holds 54 diff components across two shapes, one of them a staged rename;
- *"`ToolCall` — a run of `♦`-prefixed lines"* — Freebuff marks its tool rows with `•`, and its reasoning
  heading with the same glyph.

All three now say what is true, `Thought` has its own entry, and the page carries the one sentence the project
exists to earn: the model was trained on Grok alone and measured on Freebuff, 580 frames, every component
reproduced.

**`check-readme` passed the whole time.** It verifies that every subcommand is documented and that every named
script exists — which is a real guarantee about *commands* and says nothing about whether the page still
describes the software. Three documents in two rounds have now been corrected by reading them, and no script
would have caught any of the three.

## The glossary had every word except the five that matter

`GLOSSARY.md` defined Page, Frame, Cell, Envelope, Catalogue, Component, Provenance, Teacher, Student,
Round-trip — and **not one of the five kinds**. The words the entire project is written in were the only ones
missing from the page that decides what words mean.

Five entries now, and each carries the narrowing a real screen taught this month:

- **Block** — a run of rows that reads as one thing;
- **ToolCall** — marked rows, `♦` or `•`, naming a tool and a target;
- **Thought** — a reasoning *heading* and nothing more: the words under it are a block, and a bullet that keeps
  talking is a sentence;
- **Meter** — one quantity against one *drawn* limit; a percentage without a limit is not one, and neither is a
  status row carrying four figures;
- **Diff** — an addition and a removal together; a staged rename is one.

**The narrowings are the definitions, not footnotes to them.** Each exists because a wider rule was tried on a
real screen — a changelog bullet became a tool call, `15.4K (1%)` became a meter it was not, a tree became
seventeen blocks. Writing them down here means the next person meets the constraint before the mistake.

## The ceiling that moved, and why it was allowed to

The full suite found two hard cases one component over their ceilings:

```
wsl-agent-in-split       busiest frame holds 11 components, the ceiling is 10
wsl-two-agents-in-split  busiest frame holds 20 components, the ceiling is 19
```

The cause is the scrollbar fix: a panel whose border is followed by the terminal's own column now closes, so the
content beneath it is no longer swallowed into it. One more component — and a truer page.

**A ceiling moves when a decision moves it, never to make a run pass**, and the comment above the number says
which decision. That sentence is the difference between a baseline and an excuse.

The suite also took **727.6 s** this time against 610 s last week: the hard and agent checks now bound shape as
well as score, which means walking each recording's frames twice. Worth watching, not yet worth optimising.

## v0.16.0: the shape of a page is checked, not just its score

```
checking release: v0.16.0
  model 100.0%  floor 28.4%  over 28 frame(s), trained on 434 screen(s)
    Block 190/190 reproduced · Diff 7/7 reproduced
```

That is the **downloaded archive** reading the second agent's rename diff — an application the model never saw,
in a shape the `Diff` rule was never written for.

What ships since v0.15.0, in 29 commits:

- **`check-hard` and `check-agents` bound the shape of a page** — the busiest frame's component count — because
  a page can score 100% and still be cut into pieces the score cannot see;
- **a tree is one listing**: `cargo tree` went from 17 components to 8, with the reader, the label path and a
  test that had pinned the old answer all changed in one commit;
- **the scrollbar column belongs to the terminal**: a border followed by `█` closes a panel, and a heading
  followed by `▄` is still a heading — one shared rule, `undecorated`;
- **three more second-agent recordings**, including the first the two builders disagreed about (99.7%, now
  100% after the scrollbar fix), a full fix cycle, and a staged rename;
- and the documents that described a one-application project now describe this one.

## The component that has never fired, now on screen

The design has a `Frame` for a floating window — a box that sits *over* the base layer rather than beside it.
vim and tmux both put panes in plain rectangles, so it has never had a screen. Now it does:

```
~                          ╔Confirm═════════════════╗
~                          ║                        ║
~                          ║  Floating dialog       ║
```

`popup_create` draws a double-bordered dialog centred over the buffer. **The page describes the whole screen as
one `Block` of 23 lines**, role `text` — the floating window is not recognised at all, and a consumer cannot tell
that a dialog is on screen, let alone what it says.

Kept as the seventh hard case (`vim-floating-popup`), scoring 100% with a floor of 100%: the text-only converter
reproduces it exactly, so **the score proves nothing here** and the ceiling of 1 component records the real
state — this screen is read as one thing, and it is two.

Adding `Frame` is the largest change left in the design: a sixth kind means the catalogue, the reader, both
renderers, the label path, the checks and the glossary. `docs/adding-a-component.md` is the procedure, and this
recording is the material it asks for.

## Step zero of a new kind: the decision, with the evidence

Adding `Frame` touches seven places, and `docs/adding-a-component.md` names all of them. Doing that in the tail
of a round is how a half-change ships, so this round produced the step the procedure actually requires first:
**ADR 0006.**

It records the screen, the measurement, and the four decisions that make the implementation mechanical:

- `Body::Frame { title, rows, lines }`, with `lines` holding the rows **as drawn** — so the round trip places
  them back onto the screen and no renderer needs a new path;
- the predicate is narrow: a **double-ruled box** (`╔`…`╗` above `╚`…`╝`), which is what vim's popup uses and
  what nothing else in the corpus does;
- both builders call one function, beside `as_meter` and `as_diff`;
- **no retraining** — the kind is recognised structurally, as the rare kinds are.

The ADR ends with what would make it wrong: *if a non-floating panel uses double rules, this predicate would
lift it out of the page as a dialog.* So the corpus was checked for `╔` before the decision was written:

```
every recording that draws a double rule:  cases\hard\vim-floating-popup
```

One recording. That is the whole population, and the ADR says so rather than implying a survey nobody ran.

## `Frame`, attempt one: the kind compiled and never fired

The ADR's predicate is right and the change was mechanical — a `Body::Frame`, arms in `component_text`, `place`,
`audit`, both renderers and the capture binary. It built. And the popup screen still said:

```
popup kinds: Block:0-23
```

**Because the floating window is not a segment.** The run covering the screen starts at row 0 with a line of the
vim buffer; the `╔` appears eleven rows in. `as_frame` is asked about a *run*, and no run begins at the dialog.

So the missing piece is not a predicate but a **boundary**: the reader has to split at the row that opens a
double-ruled box, and the label path has to split at the same row in the same commit — which is exactly the
shape of the tree decision in round 26, and exactly the mistake that cost a round there when only one path
moved.

Everything was reverted; the tree is green and no inert kind ships. The next attempt starts with `segments()`
and `runs()`, and the ADR's predicate is already written for it.

## The floating window is its own component now — and it took a boundary, not a kind
