Round 46 established that `Frame` needs a boundary before it needs a predicate. This round found what the
boundary actually is: **`╔` sits at the window's left edge, not the screen's.** The row reads

```
~                          ╔Confirm═════════════════╗
```

so `opens_box` — which asked what a line *starts* with — never saw it. The box rules now accept a heavy corner
anywhere on the line, and closing likewise:

```
popup kinds: Block, Block, Block      floor 100% → 75%      score 100%
```

**Three components where there was one, and the floor fell by a quarter** — the page now carries structure the
text-only converter cannot invent. Every other set held at 100%: freebuff 580 frames, assembly 772 frames, 5940
components with no contradiction and nothing differing.

The hard case's ceiling moved from 1 to 3, which is the first ceiling in this project that was raised by a fix
rather than by drift.

The kind itself is still to do — `Frame` will be the third of those three components rather than a `Block`. The
boundary was the load-bearing part, and it is now measured.

## The sixth kind ships: `Frame`

With the boundary from round 47 in place, the kind went in as the ADR said it would — mechanical:

```
popup kinds: Block(), Frame(Confirm), Block()     score 100.0%   floor 75.0%
```

**The title is read off the top border** and the rows are the screen's own, so the round trip is unchanged at
100%. Both builders ask one predicate, `as_frame`, beside `as_meter` and `as_diff`; the catalogue lists seven
names; both renderers draw it; the HTML carries `class="component frame"` and the title as an attribute.

Everything else held:

```
freebuff 580 frames 100%   ·   hard cases 100%   ·   assembly 772 frames 100%
5940 components, 0 contradictions, 0 differing   ·   html, audit, scrub green
```

The first kind added since the vocabulary settled, and the procedure in `docs/adding-a-component.md` was right
about every place it named — including the one I would have missed: `place`, which draws a page back onto its
screen, needed its own arm or the round trip would have silently dropped the window's rows.

## The kind fires in the code and not in the output

Round 48 reported `Frame(Confirm)` from the popup screen. This round measured it again and got three blocks:

```
Block rows 0..9    Block rows 9..15    Block rows 15..23
```

The middle segment **is** the window — its first row is `~  ╔Confirm═════════════════╗` — and `as_frame` is
wired into `page_from_lines` at line 907, the same function that built these components. So something between
the predicate and the emitted kind is not what the previous round's measurement showed.

Possibilities, all checkable and none assumed: the binary measured in round 48 was built from a tree that has
since been reverted; a branch earlier in the chain claims the run first; or `as_frame` returns `None` on this
exact text for a reason the corpus screen would reveal.

**A requirement was added to `check-hard` — the popup case must contain a `Frame` — and it did its job
immediately: it printed *"no Frame component, and this case exists for it"* and failed the suite.** That
requirement is the right one and it is also why this round could not end with a green tree and a claim: the
claim and the measurement disagreed, and the check said so first.

## The discrepancy was a stale binary, and the check caught that too

Round 49 reported the kind firing in code and not in output. This round instrumented `as_frame` and found it
called with exactly the right rows:

```
as_frame: 6 row(s), first=Some("~╔Confirm═══"), last=Some("~╚══════════")
reader kinds: Column, Block, Frame, Block      score 100.0%
```

**The code was right in round 48.** What was wrong was my own workflow: after restoring a file from a backup,
`cargo build` reported success without rebuilding, so I measured the blinded binary and concluded the feature
was missing. The same stale build bit me twice in one round — once reading the page, once re-running the check.

`check-hard` now requires the popup case to contain a `Frame`, and the requirement was proven the way the
procedure asks: blinding the predicate printed

```
vim-floating-popup   no Frame component, and this case exists for it
a known-hard screen got worse; that is a regression, not a gap
```

and restoring it — with the file's timestamp touched so the build actually ran — turned the suite green again.

The lesson is worth more than the fix: **a backup-restore is not a rebuild, and a measurement of a binary you
did not rebuild is a measurement of the past.**

## Every kind is now proven by breaking it

The ledger's claim for a kind used to be a score. It is now a named failure, for all six:

| broken on purpose | what printed |
|---|---|
| `as_frame` (round 50) | `vim-floating-popup — no Frame component, and this case exists for it` |
| `is_thought_word` (this round) | `freebuff-refusal — no Thought component, and this case exists for it`, the same for `wsl-agent-in-split`, and `no Thought component anywhere in the set` from the second-agent check |
| `as_diff` (this round) | `no Diff component anywhere in the set, and the set exists for it` |

Both checks now carry a `$required` table: a hard case names the kinds it exists for, and `check-agents` names
the kinds the whole set must exercise. **A ceiling counts components; it cannot say what they are** — which is
exactly how `Frame` could have been three anonymous blocks and passed.

And the stale-binary trap bit once more, usefully: renaming `as_diff` broke the build, and the check then
reported a failure from the *previous* binary. The break has to compile, or the message is a memory.

## v0.17.0: the vocabulary grows its sixth kind

```
checking release: v0.17.0
  archive: 1,765 KB, capture: True, model shipped: True

downloaded build, on the floating-window screen: Column, Block, Frame, Block
```

The **downloaded archive** names a dialog as a `Frame` — the first kind added since the vocabulary settled, and
the first whose proof needed a boundary before it needed a predicate.

What ships since v0.16.0, in ten commits:

- **`Frame`**: a floating window, read from a vim popup. Heavy-ruled boxes (`╔`…`╗`) are windows drawn over the
  page; the title comes off the border and the rows are the screen's own.
- **ADR 0006** says why, with the corpus check that `╔` appears in exactly one recording.
- **the box rules see a border drawn mid-line** — the window's edge is at the window, not the screen, which is
  what made round 46's kind compile and never fire.
- **every kind is now proven by breaking it**: `$required` tables in `check-hard` and `check-agents` name the
  kinds each case exists for, and `docs/checks.md` carries the message each break produced.

The lesson this release cost: **a ceiling counts components, it cannot say what they are** — three anonymous
blocks satisfied the popup's ceiling while the `Frame` was missing. And twice, a check reported a failure from a
binary that had not been rebuilt. Both are written down now.

## A second floating window, from a second application

whiptail's dialog is now a `Frame`, and the measurement is the strongest this project has recorded:

```
whiptail-dialog   model 100.0%   floor 0.0%   Frame 1/1 reproduced
vim-floating-popup model 100.0%  floor 75.0%
freebuff survey sheet: Block, Block, Block…   (unchanged — it is a sheet, not a window)
```

**A floor of 0%** means the text-only converter reproduces *nothing* of that screen: the page carries all of it.

Two shapes, one function:

| shape | test | source |
|---|---|---|
| heavy rules | `╔`…`╗` over `╚`…`╝` | vim |
| centred, titled | indented `┌`, `└` in the **same column**, title between `┤` and `├` | whiptail |

And the mistake worth keeping: the first version of the second predicate asked *"does the box fail to reach the
last column?"* to decide whether it was centred — but rows are right-trimmed, so every box reaches its own last
character. It rejected whiptail's own dialog. **Alignment answers "centred"; width cannot.**

## The first counterweight `Diff` has ever had, and what it found

Two prose rows —

```
- buy milk
+ buy eggs
```

— are read as a **`Diff`**. The rule asks for a row beginning `-` and a row beginning `+`, and a shopping list
satisfies that as easily as a patch does. That is a real false positive, found the first time anyone wrote a
counterweight for this kind.

Narrowing it to require a hunk header (`@@`) fixed the list and **broke a pinned measurement elsewhere**: the
CLI suite's floor test reported `structure 40.0% → 60.0%` and two kinds no longer reproduced, so the change
was reverted in full. A rule that fixes one screen and costs another has not been understood yet.

What is recorded instead of a fix: the boundary, the counterweight as a test that documents current behaviour,
and the evidence that the shape needs a decision — **a one-sided diff (new file) is a `Diff` only because
`--- /dev/null` satisfies the "removal" test**, which is correctness by accident.

## Both `Diff` boundaries, pinned

The narrow rule was tried and reverted; what is left is the answer this project will defend until a screen
demands otherwise, and it is now two tests:

```
two_prose_lines_read_as_a_diff      - buy milk / + buy eggs  →  Diff
a_new_file_diff_is_a_diff           --- /dev/null, +++, @@   →  Diff
```

The first is the same class of boundary as `a_bare_bullet_list_reads_as_one_tool_call`: at the margin, a list
satisfies a rule written for a patch. Real lists are drawn inside panels, where the box guard keeps them whole,
and the corpus contains no bare list at a margin.

The second is **correctness by accident that happens to be stable**: a new file's diff has no removals, and it
qualifies only because `--- /dev/null` supplies one. Every patch git prints carries that header, so the answer
holds — but a future rule that drops headers will change it, and the test will say so.

Why the narrow rule was not kept: it fixed the list and moved a pinned floor measurement (`structure 40.0% →
60.0%`, two kinds no longer reproducing) on a synthetic fixture whose "diff" is two bare rows with no header.
**Two of three changes were understood; the third was not, so none of them shipped.**

## A check was recording the wrong screen

`check-validate` failed on every run with

```
matching dump: K5: the emulator's screen and the draw buffer disagree on 3 of 400 cell(s)
```

which reads like an emulator fault. It was a quoting fault in the check itself: it recorded
`wsl.exe -e sh -c "printf abc; sleep 3"`, PowerShell strips the inner quotes before `wsl` sees them, and the
shell then ran something that printed more than `abc`. The dump held three cells; the screen held more.

The command now goes into a script file and is run from there, and the check says what it should:

```
matching dump:        agree on all 400 cell(s)
one wrong cell:       disagree on 1 of 400 cell(s)
```

**A check that fails must be read as "which of the two is wrong" — the code or the check.** This is the third
time today the same quoting fault has bitten (a whiptail run, a fixture recording, and now a check), and the
first time it was hiding inside the suite rather than in my shell.

## A real capture with every kind on one screen

The floor fixture is hand-built JSON whose "diff" is two bare rows with no header — which is what objected to
the narrow `Diff` rule in round 55. A real capture of the same shapes turned out to be easy to make, once the
capture method was fixed:

```
a real screen with every kind:  Block=1, ToolCall=1, Diff=1, Meter=1
score 100.0%  floor 0.0%
```

**A floor of 0.0%** — the text converter reproduces none of it, and the model reproduces all of it. That is a
much better fixture than the one in the tree, whose floor is 40% structure / 14.3% weighted.

It is kept as `cases/hard/every-kind-one-screen` with all four kinds required, and the **fixture was left
alone**: switching it changes a pinned measurement and the comparison the CLI test is making, which is a
decision for its own round rather than a side effect of this one.

## Two fixtures, two lessons

The switch I planned — replace the hand-built fixture with the real capture — turned out to feed **four pinned
expectations** across two test files (`cli.rs` three times, `structured.rs` once). Changing all four to make one
point is a poor trade, and the pin that would move is the one that teaches the most.

So both fixtures exist, each with a test:

| fixture | what it shows | floor |
|---|---|---|
| `fixtures/session` (hand-built) | a converter that only splits paragraphs recovers **every** block and scores 40% of structure — the trap the floor exists to expose | 40.0% / 14.3% weighted |
| `fixtures/every-kind` (**a real capture**) | the text converter recovers **none** of a screen carrying all four kinds, so a 100% from the model there means something | **0.0%** |

**The hand-built fixture earns its place by being misleading on purpose.** Replacing it would have deleted the
only case in the suite where a high floor is demonstrated rather than warned about.

## Does a patch need a hunk header? The corpus answered

Round 55 tried to narrow `Diff` to require `@@` and reverted when a pinned floor measurement objected. This
round asked the question directly — *how many diffs in the corpus carry a hunk header?*

```
Diff components: 61
  carrying a hunk header: 27
  without one:           34
```

**Thirty-four of sixty-one have no header at all**, and they are not artefacts:

```
    -     return a - b
    +     return a + b
```

That is how Freebuff shows an edit — the changed lines beside the line they replace, with no `@@` anywhere.
Requiring a header would have destroyed more than half the project's real diff components to stop a shopping
list from being read as a patch.

**So the loose rule is right, and the prose false positive is its price**, paid knowingly: real lists are drawn
inside panels, where the box guard keeps them whole, and the corpus contains no bare list at a margin. Both
pinned tests now cite this measurement, so the next person meets the reason before the rule.

That also disposes of the floor fixture's objection: its two bare rows are the **same shape** as Freebuff's
previews, which is why it objected at all.

## `Meter` is narrow by formatting, not by principle — and round 39 was wrong about why

Round 39 recorded a conclusion that reading the corpus now overturns: *"an agent CLI prints a budget because a
budget is the thing it manages"*. It does — **but Freebuff prints one too**, and this rule cannot see it:

```
Grok     : 1.3K / 500K │ [Dashboard]        → Meter, used 1.3K, limit 500K
Freebuff : │ 2/5 hours remaining │          → Block
```

`as_meter` looks for a token that **is** a slash. Grok writes `25K / 500K`; Freebuff writes `2/5`. The kind is
being decided by a space, not by whether the line reports a budget against a limit.

The corpus says so plainly:

| | |
|---|---|
| `Meter` components | **191, in 9 recordings — all Grok** |
| declined rows containing N/M | **35 distinct**, including `2/5 hours remaining` (a budget), `quick question 1/11` (a step counter), `DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 10/17` (a version) |

Widening the rule to accept the glued form would gain Freebuff's budget and lose the ability to tell it from a
step counter — **both are `N/M` with words after them**. That is a decision nobody has made, so today's answer is
pinned by a test: the glued form stays a block, and the reason is recorded beside it.

## The meter widening works, and it breaks the round trip — so it does not ship

The rule was written exactly as the decision said: a glued `2/5` is a budget when a **word** follows it.

```
meter: used=2 limit=5 text=[│ 2/5 hours remaining │]     ✓
quick question 1/11                                      → Block  ✓
DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 10/17                                → Block  ✓
every evaluation set                                     → 100%   ✓
```

Six ceilings then moved — a budget is now a component of its own rather than part of the block around it — and
the last of them exposed the cost:

```
freebuff-refusal   draws back at 59.8% (round trip: 477/797 line(s) came back, 750 drawn)
```

**Splitting a meter out of a panel loses the rest of its row.** The Meter holds the whole line, but the block it
was taken from still claims the rows around it, and the round trip — which places every component's lines back
onto the screen — can no longer account for what it draws.

Everything was reverted; the tree is green, the kind is unchanged, and the pinned test still says what round 62
said: *the glued form stays a block until someone decides*. What round 63 adds is the shape of the decision and
the evidence that it cannot be made inside a panel without solving placement first.

## Reproduced: the meter widening costs 320 lines of round trip

Applied the widened rule again and measured the same case, to be sure the number was real and not a side effect
of the ceilings I had also raised:

```
widened:  round trip 477/797 line(s) came back (59.8%), 750 drawn
reverted: every hard case draws back at 100%
```

Thirteen `Meter` components appear in that session's pages, so the rule is doing what it was written to do — and
the page as a whole stops drawing back. **The cost is far larger than thirteen rows**: something about splitting
a budget out of the panel that contains it destabilises the rest of the screen, not just that line. That is a
hypothesis with a shape, not an answer: the next measurement is to print the component list for one frame with
and without the rule and see which rows moved.

The rule stays out of the tree. The pinned test still says the glued form is a block until this is understood.

## The instrumentation contradicted the measurement, so neither is trusted yet

Dumped every component of all 26 frames with the widened rule and with the rule reverted:

```
wide:   228 component lines
narrow: 228 component lines
differences: 0
```

**Identical.** Yet the same session measured `477/797` lines of round trip with the widened build and `797/797`
with the reverted one. Both cannot be true: if the pages are the same, the drawing is the same.

One of those two measurements came from a binary that did not carry the change — the stale-build trap that has
now bitten three times this week (rounds 50, 51, and here). The honest state is: **the 59.8% figure is not
established**, and the widened rule is neither proven harmful nor proven harmless.

What the next attempt must do first, before measuring anything: **show that the binary carries the change** —
one line of output only the new code can produce — and only then compare.

## The binary can now say what it was built from

Three measurements this week came from a binary that did not carry the change being measured — rounds 50, 51 and
65 — and each looked like a finding. The cheap fix is a stamp:

```
$ ui-lm-capture version
ui-lm-capture 0.1.0  commit 28d6421  tree 0375259ea81a8e19
```

**`tree` is a digest of `git status` plus the diff**, not the word "dirty": dirty is true before and after an
edit, which is exactly the case the stamp exists for. Proven by editing `lib.rs` and rebuilding:

```
before the edit:  23183683d34857f8
after the edit:   dfd6f54372e42f56
```

From now on a measurement that matters names the stamp it was taken with, and the question "which binary said
this" is answered by the binary rather than by memory. `build.rs` watches the sources and `.git/HEAD`, so the
stamp cannot go stale behind an edit or a checkout, and it degrades to `unknown` on a machine without `git`
rather than failing the build.

## The meter question, settled with stamps on both measurements

Every number below names the binary that produced it, which is what round 66 built the stamp for.

| build | stamp | kinds on `freebuff-refusal` | round trip |
|---|---|---|---|
| narrow rule | `tree 0375259…` | Block 206, Thought 22 | **797/797** |
| widened rule | `tree 2a3d365…` | Block 207, **Meter 13**, Thought 22 | **477/797** |

The regression is real, and its size is the explanation: **320 lines lost for 13 meters — about 24 each**, which
is the height of the panel each one was cut out of. Splitting a row out of a boxed run leaves every row below it
one position short, so the whole panel stops drawing back.

And that decides the question from round 62: **Freebuff's `2/5 hours remaining` is inside a panel, and a panel
owns its rows.** The kind was never hidden by a space alone — it was behind the rule that keeps a boxed segment
whole. Grok's meters are status lines *outside* panels, which is why they are the ones this project has.

So the narrow rule stays, and now for a measured reason rather than a formatting accident. What would change it
is a meter that stands alone on a line outside any panel — which no application here has drawn yet.

## The invariant that would have said it in one line

The audit now checks that **a component describes every row it claims** — a cheap comparison of `rows` against
the number of lines it carries. On the corpus as it stands:

```
audit: no violations
```

On the widened meter rule, which splits a row out of a panel:

```
audit: 1433 violation(s)
  component "block-04ece401ef242cf6" claims 7 row(s) and describes 1
```

**That is the sentence three rounds were missing.** Rounds 62–67 chased a 320-line round-trip loss, guessed at
formatting, blamed a stale binary, and finally found the mechanism by arithmetic (13 meters × ~24 rows). The
audit says it directly, on the page, before anything is drawn.

The widened rule is out again; the check stays. This is the fourth rule this week whose real cost appeared as a
*structural* failure rather than a scoring one, and the first where an existing check could have named it.

## A row can have one owner — but the page does not say who, when it splits

The invariant is simple: two components may not claim the same row, because a page describes one screen. Written
and run against the corpus:

```
audit: 442 violation(s)
  wsl-agent-in-split: frame 19: component "block-77d1…" and "block-ce1820…" both claim row 0
```

**Every violation is a split screen**, where the left and right panes legitimately share rows. The check tried to
exempt them by looking for a block whose role is `pane`, and found none — because the page does not mark its
panes at all:

```
Column
Block  role=text   rows=0..1
Meter              rows=1..2
```

A consumer holding this page cannot tell that two blocks came from different panes, only that their rows overlap
— which is exactly what a *defect* looks like. **So the invariant cannot be enforced until the page says which
components are panes**, and that is a change to the page format, not to the check.

The check was reverted; `audit: no violations` again. The next step is named: mark panes in the page (the reader
already builds them), then the ownership rule becomes checkable and this round's 442 become zero.

## A row has one owner, and the page already said who

Round 69 concluded the page could not tell a legitimate split from a double claim. **That was wrong.** The
surface has carried `panes: [left_ids, right_ids]` all along — I looked at the components and not at the field
beside them.

With it, the invariant is enforceable: two components may not claim the same row **unless they are in different
panes**.

```
audit: 77 recording(s), 979 frame(s) checked
audit: no violations
```

Two tests hold both sides, so neither can drift:

- two blocks on rows `0..2` and `1..3` → `both claim row 1`;
- the same two, in different panes → no violations, because a split screen gives left and right the same rows.

**The lesson is the same one as the pane-split gate five weeks ago**: when a page looks like it cannot express
something, read its own format before concluding it does not. Twice now the field was there, unread.

## The procedure now lists what the audit will ask

`docs/adding-a-component.md` said the new kind must be reachable by `audit` and left it there. The audit has
since grown from two invariants to five, and a kind added tomorrow would meet them without warning. Step one is
now a table:

| invariant | what breaking it looks like |
|---|---|
| unique ids, all reachable from the root | two alike-reading blocks collided on one recording |
| every kind is in the catalogue | a page no renderer could draw |
| spans parallel to lines | styling on a row that does not exist |
| **a component describes every row it claims** | *"claims 7 row(s) and describes 1"*, 1433 violations |
| **one owner per row**, but panes may share | two blocks both claiming row 1 |

The page also says when it was last run — and this round re-ran it, because **a procedure that names a check has
to be re-run when the check changes**:

```
check-audit exit=0   check-all exit=0   check-html exit=0   check-corpus exit=0   check-hard exit=0
```

## What each instrument cannot see

Every check can fail, and every one is blind to something. None of the blind spots below is hypothetical — each
was found by a defect that got through it.

| instrument | it says | it cannot see | the defect that proved it |
|---|---|---|---|
| `score` | the two builders agree | **two builders agreeing wrongly** — the model copies the reader | `cargo tree` read as 17 components, at 100% |
| `floor` | what a text-only converter recovers | nothing, when it reads 100%: the set proves nothing about the model | `top`, and whiptail before `Frame` existed |
| `audit` | the page is coherent with itself | whether it matches the screen it describes | every rule error that left a valid-but-wrong page |
| round trip | the page draws back onto its rows | whether the *kinds* are right | the meter split: 320 lines lost while the kinds looked fine |
| a component ceiling | how many components a frame holds | **what they are** | three anonymous blocks satisfying the popup's ceiling while `Frame` was missing |
| a `$required` table | which kinds must appear | where they sit, or whether they own their rows | the same meter split, caught only by the round trip |
| `check-corpus` | every recording reads without an error | whether it reads *correctly* | the whole reason `score` exists |
| the build stamp | which source state produced a binary | whether that state is right | three measurements from stale binaries in one week |

**The pattern, stated once: a measurement can only be trusted about the axis it names.** Every expensive mistake
this month has been of one shape — trusting an instrument outside its axis because it was green.

And one structural lesson from writing this table: `docs/checks.md` counts its own rows, so a table added to it
broke two checks at once. The ledger holds proofs; prose about instruments belongs here.

## The gate now runs the documents

A command added in round 66 went unlisted in the handoff for six rounds, and every gate stayed open, because
the gate ran clippy, formatting and tests — not the two document checks. They take about a second each:

```
  clippy   ok
  fmt      ok
  test     ok
  docs     ok
  readme   ok
the gate is open
```

Both new steps were proved failable in the only way that counts — by breaking a document and watching the gate
refuse:

| broken on purpose | what the gate said |
|---|---|
| a README line naming a script that does not exist | `readme FAILED (exit 1)` |
| the state page claiming **4** hard recordings instead of 9 | `docs FAILED (exit 1)` |

**A gate that covers the code and not the claims about the code is half a gate** — and the half it left out is
the half a reader of the repository sees first.

## v0.18.0: the instruments, hardened

```
every check passed in 719.4s
checking release: v0.18.0 — archive 1,769 KB, three platforms

ui-lm-capture 0.1.0  commit d9d9e4a  tree clean      ← the released binary, stamping itself
audit: 2 recording(s), 5 frame(s) checked — no violations
```

The **downloaded archive names its own source state**, which is the point of the stamp: a stranger can tell what
they are holding, and so can I.

What ships since v0.17.0, in twenty-four commits:

- **the audit enforces five invariants** — ids and reachability, catalogue names, spans parallel to lines,
  *a component describes every row it claims*, and *one owner per row* (panes excepted, which the surface's
  `panes` field already distinguished);
- **the gate runs the documents** — a command added in round 66 went unlisted in the handoff for six rounds
  while every gate stayed open;
- **the build stamp** — commit and a digest of the working tree, proven to move when a source file is edited,
  after three measurements in one week were taken from binaries that did not carry the change;
- **`Frame`'s second shape** — whiptail's centred dialog, with Freebuff's full-width sheet as the counterweight
  that must stay a block;
- and two fixtures with opposite lessons: one hand-built to show a misleading floor, one recorded to show a
  floor of zero.

The month's recurring defect — a rule that exists in two places — has not appeared in four rounds, which is the
longest gap since the work began.

## A third application, and three blank screens that teach the same thing

Trying to widen the corpus to applications that are not agents:

| program | launch | result |
|---|---|---|
| `man ls` | `sh -c "man ls | head -40"` | **usable**: 19 lines, round trip 100% |
| `man ls` | `wsl -e man ls` | 3 frames, all blank |
| `nano` | `sh script` with `TERM` exported | 2 frames, no content |
| `nano` | `wsl -e env TERM=… nano` | 2 frames, no content |
| `less` | `wsl -e env TERM=… less` | 2 frames, one empty block |

**The pattern is the launch, not the program**: a full-screen application started through `wsl.exe -e <program>`
draws nothing here, while the same program under `wsl.exe -e sh <script>` — or a non-full-screen invocation like
`top` — records normally. The pty the recorder provides is real; what these programs need is a terminal type they
accept, and `TERM` set inside a shell does not reach them the same way.

So the corpus gains one real capture — a `man` page, a third application's text with section headers and
overstrike bold — and the recipe that works is written down beside the three that do not. A round that produced
one recording and a negative result is worth more than four rounds of guessing, which is what the last hour was.

## Correction: round 75's conclusion was wrong, and the real cause was a missing file

Round 75 recorded that full-screen applications launched through `wsl.exe -e` "draw nothing here" and blamed the
terminal type. This round asked the pty what it advertises:

```
TERM=[xterm-256color] COLS=[] ROWS=[]
```

**The terminal type was always right.** Then the same `less` invocation that had produced a blank frame printed:

```
["wsl.exe"] exited with status 1 ... the program was gone after 94 ms
```

It was not drawing nothing — it was exiting because `/tmp/ui-lm/long.txt` **did not exist**. WSL's `/tmp` had
been cleared between rounds, and the file-creating command had run in a different round.

Recorded properly — the file created in the same script as the pager — `less` works first time:

```
eval/tui/less-pager   20/20 lines round trip, 100%
```

So the corpus gains a **pager**, and the lesson is the one this month keeps re-learning in new clothes:

> **A program that produces nothing is not evidence about the program.** Before concluding that an application
> cannot be recorded, check whether it ran at all — `wsl.exe` reports its exit status, and the recorder prints
> it when it is non-zero.

Round 75 kept the one capture that worked and wrote a plausible mechanism for the three that did not. The
mechanism was wrong; the recipe is: **create the input in the same script that consumes it.**

## The record now says how the program ended

Round 76 spent a round concluding that a pager "draws nothing here" when `less` had exited with status 1
because its input file did not exist. The evidence was in the terminal and **nowhere in the record** — so the
record gained the field:

```
record: wsl.exe -e sh fail3.sh   →   exit recorded: 2, frames: 1
```

A session could already say what it ran, in what environment, and when; it could not say whether the program
**worked**. That is the fact a reader needs first when a capture holds almost nothing, and it was the one
missing.

The recorder already knew it — it printed a warning — but confidence that a person would read a warning is not
the same as a record a check can read. `exit` is now part of every session, `null` when the program was still
running when the span ended.

## Attempted: the record says how its program ended

Round 76 found the fact a reader needs first when a capture holds nothing — *did the program work?* — and the
record did not carry it. The field was added (`exit: Option<i32>`), wired through `finish`, and tested:

```
record: wsl.exe -e sh fail3.sh   →   exit recorded: 2, frames: 1
```

Then it was reverted, because **four construction sites** build `SessionRecord` — two in the recorder, one in
each of two test files — and my edits matched two of them. The gate caught the other two, and the tree went back
to green rather than shipping a schema change half-applied.

The work is small and unstarted in one sense only: the field, the wiring, and the reason are all recorded here,
and the next attempt starts from a compiler error that names the two sites I missed.

## The record says how its program ended — on the sixth attempt

```
record: wsl.exe -e sh fail3.sh   →   exit=2
```

The field, the writer, and one call site:

- **`exit: Option<i32>`** on `SessionRecord`, `#[serde(default)]` so old recordings still load;
- **`record_exit(dir, code)`** in `crates/frames` — re-reads the record, sets the field, rewrites it through the
  same staging-file path, and treats a missing record as "nothing to stamp" rather than an error;
- **four lines inside the existing wait block** in the recorder, where the status is already in hand.

Five earlier attempts failed by trying to *move* the wait above the record write — with string anchors, line
arithmetic, and a second `try_wait`. None of it was needed: the status was one `if let` away from the record it
belonged to, and the shortest route was to write it afterwards.

**What the field is for**: a recording that holds almost nothing is usually a capture of a program that failed,
and until now the record could not say so. Round 76 spent a round concluding that a pager "draws nothing here"
when it had exited with status 1 because its input file was missing.

## The field has a consumer, and it found the defect it was built for

`check-corpus` now asks the one question a reader cannot: **did the program work?**

```
git-log-exit: the program exited 128 and drew 1 frame(s)
a recording exists whose program failed and which holds almost nothing
```

That specimen was recorded by accident — a `git log` pointed at a directory that no longer existed — and the
check named it immediately. It was removed from the corpus afterwards, because a broken capture is evidence, not
material; the message is in the ledger.

Measured before the rule: **all 79 corpus recordings predate the field** and carry no exit status at all. The
first two that do are new ones, and the check refuses to pass until at least one exists — the same
empty-material rule the other three checks follow.

## Eleven checks refuse empty material, and the ledger said three

The ledger's closing line read *"three checks also refuse to pass on empty material"*. Counting them from the
scripts:

```
check-agents  check-corpus  check-corrupt  check-diff-fresh  check-docs  check-hard
check-html    check-readme  check-release  check-scrub        check-sizes  check-audit
```

**Eleven of nineteen.** The sentence was true when it was written, and nobody counted again — which is exactly
what a rule kept in prose rather than in code is worth.

It is now stated once, with the eleven named and what each refuses on, and with the reason the project keeps
rediscovering one level down:

> **A check that passes on nothing has proved nothing** — and a green tick from an empty set is worse than a red
> one, because it looks like evidence.

The same sentence is the floor's, the ceiling's and the `$required` table's: every instrument needs a floor of
real material beneath it.

## The state page said five kinds, and there are six

The claim was written when `Frame` arrived and nobody updated the sentence beside it. `check-docs` compares the
corpus counts, the hard-case count, the release tag and the script count — and not this. So the sentence drifted
for four rounds, in the one document whose job is to say what is true *now*.

Both halves are fixed:

- the page now says **all six kinds**, twice, in the paragraph about new material;
- `check-docs` derives the number from the code — `COMPONENTS` minus `Column`, which is the container rather
  than a kind — and fails when the page disagrees:

```
the page says all five kinds; the catalogue defines 6
```

**A claim kept in prose drifts, and the only defence is to derive it from the thing that knows.** That is now
the fifth count this document check derives rather than trusts.

## The model card's numbers were all three stale

Checking the card against the artefacts it describes:

| claim | measured | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| 100 parameters | 100 numeric values in `models/page.json` | ✓ |
| 2.8 KB | 2,880 bytes | ✓ |
| `433465EADD618E45` | same | ✓ |
| seven evaluation sets, **185 frames** | **234 frames** | ✗ stale |
| second agent: **580 frames** | **591 frames** | ✗ stale |
| "across **five** kinds of work" | nine, listed | ✗ understated |

The three that were right are the three the code produces — the file's size, its digest, its parameter count. The
three that were wrong are the three a person wrote down, which is the same split `check-docs` exists for and the
same reason it derives counts instead of trusting them.

Corrected; the sentence about the second agent now lists the work rather than counting it, because a list cannot
drift the way a number does.

**What is still not derived**: none of the card's corpus numbers are checked. They change whenever a recording is
added, and only this audit catches them — which is honest for now and wrong for a document a stranger reads.

## The model card's numbers are derived now

`check-docs` counts two slices of the corpus the totals cannot give it — the seven evaluation sets and the
second agent's recordings — and compares them with what the card says. Both were proved failable with the exact
numbers that had drifted:

```
the card says 185 evaluation frames, the sets hold 234
the card says 580 second-agent frames, the recordings hold 591
the model card counts its material wrongly
```

And both carry the empty-material guard, because a slice that is absent on some machine would otherwise pass:

```
the card's two slices are empty on this machine, so nothing was compared
```

**That is the last unchecked claim in a front-page document.** Every number a stranger reads is now either
derived from the code or the corpus, or it is a sentence describing a decision — and the sentences are the ones
this audit has had to fix by reading them.

## A table, which the corpus had none of

`df -h` is fifteen rows of aligned numeric columns — sizes, percentages, mount points. No recording in the corpus
had that shape; the closest was `top`, which is a live monitor with a different rhythm.

```
round trip: 15/15 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)
kinds: Block, Column     exit recorded: 0
```

It reads as one block, which is honest: nothing here names columns, and the catalogue has no `Table`. What it
does exercise is a text screen whose lines are wide, numeric and right-aligned — the sort of thing a future
`Table` kind would have to earn its place on, and the material for that argument now exists.

The derived counts did their job on the way in: `check-docs` refused the build twice, once for the corpus totals
and once for the model card's evaluation slice, and each message named the number to change.

## The seventh kind, proposed and not built

`Table` has been the obvious missing kind since `df -h` was recorded, and three screens now draw one: `df`, `top`
and `cargo tree`. All three read as a single `Block`, which keeps the rows and throws away the alignment.

**ADR 0007 is written and left at *Proposed*.** The reason is in it: the predicate's false-positive rate is not
measured, because the measurement I ran this round was defective — a PowerShell regex overload failed silently and
the counter reported every row as aligned. Building a kind on that number would have been the month's most
expensive mistake yet, since a *panel* read as a table would be cut up and only the round trip would notice.

The ADR carries what has to be true before acceptance: the false-positive rate measured across the whole corpus,
`df`/`top`/`cargo tree` producing a table with the round trip intact, and Freebuff's status rows and every panel
staying blocks.

**A proposal that names what it does not know is worth more than an implementation that assumes it.**

## `Table` is rejected, and the measurement is why

ADR 0007 proposed a table as *a run of rows whose columns line up*. Measured across all 81 recordings:

| rule | recordings matching | worst |
|---|---|---|
| 2+ shared boundaries | **28** | Freebuff, 33 runs |
| 3+ shared boundaries | **20** | Freebuff, 33 runs |

The rows it keeps finding are these:

```
│ 1 Me   2 My employer   3 Both   4 Nobody, I stick to free tools   0 Doesn't apply
```

**A survey's answer row has more spaces in it than `df -h`.** Raising the boundary count changes nothing, because
the gap count was never the signal.

So the predicate cannot exist in the form proposed. A `Table` would need **semantics** — columns whose values
share a type across rows — which is inference, not reading, and a far bigger design than a seventh kind.

The ADR now says *Rejected*, with both measurements in it and the acceptance cases for whoever tries the type-based
route. The three screens stay `Block`s: the page keeps their rows, and simply does not claim to know they are a
table.

**This is the first kind the project has refused, and it was refused by counting.**

## A flaky agent was reporting itself as a broken reader

The full suite went red on `check-diff-fresh` — *"a fresh diff was not read exactly"* — and the same check passed
on its own a minute later. The cause was the agent not showing a diff in its three attempts, and the check had
one message for both failures:

| what happened | what it said |
|---|---|
| the agent never showed a diff | *"a fresh diff was not read exactly"* |
| the reader got the diff wrong | the same sentence |

Two different failures, one message, and the misleading one blamed the reader. Now:

```
the agent showed no diff in three attempts, so nothing was measured
```

That is the ledger's own rule — nothing is judged from nothing — applied to a check that had been reporting a
shortage of material as a defect in the code. The retry line was corrected too: it announced "a diff appeared on
attempt N" whether or not one ever appeared.

**A check whose message is wrong is worse than no check**, because the message is what a reader acts on.

## A flaky source needs more attempts, and a check needs to say which failure it had

`check-diff-fresh` failed **inside the full suite twice and passed alone twice**, and the two runs that failed
were not the same failure: once the agent showed no diff at all, once it showed one the reader did not reproduce
exactly. Neither is a defect in this project — the source is a live agent, and it answers from context about one
run in three instead of running the command.

Two changes, both small:

- **five attempts instead of three**, since three left the check red roughly half the time; five leave it under
  1%, and the reason is written above the number;
- the **two failures are named separately** (`the agent showed no diff in five attempts, so nothing was measured`
  versus `a fresh diff was not read exactly`), so a reader knows whether the project or its source is at fault.

The alternative — relaxing the check until it always passes — would have traded a noisy signal for no signal.

## v0.19.0: the record says how its program ended

```
every check passed in 699.3s

checking release: v0.19.0 — archive 1,772 KB, three platforms

ui-lm-capture 0.1.0  commit 18e77e6  tree clean      ← downloaded from the release
```

What ships since v0.18.0, in sixteen commits:

- **`exit: Option<i32>` on every session record**, and `check-corpus` catches a capture whose program failed and
  which holds almost nothing — the defect that cost a round in 76;
- **`check-docs` derives more**: the kind count from the catalogue, and the model card's two corpus slices;
- **ADR 0007 is rejected on measurement** — a column-alignment predicate matched 28 of 81 recordings, all
  Freebuff surveys, so a `Table` needs semantics rather than alignment;
- **the diff check retries five times** and names its two failures separately, after failing twice in the suite
  and passing twice alone;
- and the ledger says **eleven** checks refuse empty material, not the three it had claimed for a week.

The tag itself carries the build stamp, so the archive can be asked what it is.

## A commit graph, which stresses the box rules

`git log --graph --oneline --all` draws graph art — `*`, and with branches `│ ├─╮`. The reader's box rules treat
`╭ ┌ ╰ └ ╔ ╚` as borders, so a graph is the screen most likely to be miscut by the very rules added this month.

```
round trip: 25/25 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)     kinds: Block, Column     exit: 0
```

One block, nothing miscut, and every line back. This repository's history is linear, so the graph is a column of
`*` — the interesting case (two branches, `├─╮`) is not in the corpus yet, and that is now the argument for
recording a merge, not a guess about whether the rules would survive one.

Two attempts: the direct `wsl -e git …` produced an empty frame, and the same command under `sh -c` with a
`sleep` produced two. The recorder needs the program alive long enough to settle — the recipe is in the round-76
note, and this is the fourth time it has cost a run.

## The first `├─╮` in the corpus, and the rules survive it

A repository with two branches and a real merge:

```
*   b2a18d7 (HEAD -> master) merge feature
|\
| * a7400bb (feature) feature more
| * e67ace6 feature work
* | 6fe3a2a mainline work
|/
* 104ed8f second
```

```
round trip: 8/8 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)     kinds: Block, Column     exit: 0
```

**One block, nothing miscut.** Two rules were at risk and neither fired wrongly: the box rules look for
`╭ ┌ ╰ └ ╔ ╚` and the graph draws `|`, `\` and `*`; the pane gate looks for a column of vertical bars, and a
graph's `|` column at the far left is not one — the page is a single block, not two panes.

Both are now in the corpus as evidence rather than as argument: `git-log-graph` (linear) and `git-merge-graph`
(branched).

## The case I said could not be produced here, produced

Last round recorded that *"the remaining untested thing is a real pane beside a graph"* and that it was
unproducible because the machine had no tmux. **It has tmux 3.4** — my earlier availability check listed ten
programs and simply did not include it.

```
panic-free split: Column + Block + Block      panes: 1 + 1
round trip: 30/30 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)
score 100.0%   floor 0.0%
```

A `git log --graph` in the left pane, `seq 1 30` in the right, and the page describes **two panes** — so the
pane gate accepts a screen where a graph's `|` column is present alongside a real divider. Both hazards at once,
and both handled.

**A floor of 0.0%** means the text converter reproduces none of it; every line in the page is structure the
reader found.

The lesson is not about graphs: it is that **"cannot be produced here" is a claim about my own survey, not about
the machine**, and it deserves the same measurement as any other claim.

## An agent in one pane, a shell in the other

The shape the orchestration design is about, recorded for the first time:

```
≡ main ~/D/W/m/Projects/UI-LM        │ cd … && git status --short | head -6
                                     │ moreweb@DESKTOP…$ cd /mnt/c/…
```

```
components: Column, Block, Block   panes: 1 + 1
round trip: 64/64 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)
score 100.0%   floor 0.0%
```

**A live agent on the left, a shell on the right, described as two panes** — and a floor of 0.0%, so every one of
those 64 lines is structure the reader found rather than text reflowed.

Three attempts to get it: the first used a directory that had been cleaned away, the second called `grok` without
`.exe`, and the third worked. Each failure was visible in the frame — `No such file or directory`, then
`No such file` again — which is what the exit field and the recorder's warnings are for.

## A conversation inside a pane

Round 96 got the agent in a pane; this round got it **working** in one:

```
14 frames   Block 67 · ToolCall 9 · Meter 8
round trip 344/344 line(s) came back (100.0%)
score 100.0%   floor 23.1%
```

A prompt typed into the left pane, the agent reading a file, its tool calls and usage meter described as
components inside a split screen, beside a shell in the right.

Three attempts failed before this one, and the reason is worth keeping: **`tmux send-keys` does not work here,
but the recorder's own key script does.** `check-diff-fresh` has been driving Grok this way for weeks; the method
was in the repository the whole time and I kept reaching for the other one.

## Two agents on one screen

The orchestration claim, recorded:

```
panes: 7 + 8 components      two Meters, ToolCalls in both
round trip: 72/72 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)
score 100.0%   floor 60.0%
```

Two Grok sessions side by side in one terminal, each with its own usage meter and its own tool calls, described as
**one screen in two panes** — and every component reproduced against a model trained on single-pane screens.

That is the project's own scenario: a person running two agents at once, with a description that says what each
one is doing rather than a wall of text.

Two attempts: the first had its working directory cleaned away between rounds (`No such file or directory` in both
panes), the second created the directory **inside the recorded script** and worked first time. That recipe has now
been learned three times — round 76, round 93, and here.

## A divider that moves

The corpus has split screens, but every one of them holds still. `tmux resize-pane`, typed into the right pane
while the session is running, moves the boundary mid-recording:

```
frame 2: divider at column 55        frame 4: divider at column 70
round trip: 104/104 line(s) of content came back (100.0%)
score 100.0%   floor 0.0%
```

Five frames: the first before the split existed (a single pane), then two-pane frames at two different widths.
**Every frame round-trips perfectly and every frame is described** — the pane gate re-derives the divider per
frame rather than remembering it, which is what a page of a *live* screen has to do.

A floor of 0.0% again: none of it is text the converter could recover.

## The orchestration shapes are guarded now, not just recorded

Four recordings moved from `eval/apps` to `cases/hard`, so `check-hard` protects them the way it protects the
paned screens:

| case | busiest frame | kinds required |
|---|---|---|
| `agent-beside-shell` | 2 | Block |
| `pane-conversation` | 9 | Block, Meter, ToolCall |
| `two-agents-one-screen` | 15 | Block, Meter, ToolCall |
| `pane-resize` | 2 | Block |

**Thirteen hard cases**, every one at 100% on score, round trip and shape. Recorded once is a demonstration;
guarded is a regression test — and these four are the shapes the orchestration story rests on.

One mistake on the way: I copied them first, which put the same recordings in two places and made the corpus
count jump from 88 to 92. **Moving, not copying**, is what a promotion is.

## The month review now says what happened, not what was true a week ago

It still claimed, under *what is not proven*, that **"every screen ever recorded came from one application"** —
which the second agent had already disproved eleven times. Three sections rewritten:

- **what is not proven** now names the two real gaps: a `Table` (rejected with numbers) and a meter inside a
  panel (blocked on row ownership);
- **where it stands** says 24 releases through v0.19.0, 494 commits, and the audit at 699.3 s;
- **what I would do next** no longer leads with the second agent — it was done — and a new section, *what the
  second agent changed*, lists the four rules it forced and their evidence.

**A review written at the end of a month is a claim about the past, and it goes stale the moment the work
continues.** This one was four days old and wrong in its headline limitation.

## The handoff described a project from weeks ago

A successor's first document said **"seven scripts, about six minutes"**, verified **v0.5.0** by download, and
named as *the one thing worth doing next* a pane-split change that was finished long before this week. It is now
the handoff for the project that exists:

- **what is proven** — six claims, each with a number taken today: 779 frames assembled at 100%, 5,957 components
  with 0 differing and 0 contradictions, 591 frames of a second agent at 100%, v0.19.0 verified by download;
- **where it stands** — nineteen checks, 88 recordings across three applications, thirteen hard cases;
- **attempts that were abandoned** — seven rows, each a route somebody should not try again, including the three
  from this week (hunk headers, column alignment, moving the wait);
- **the two things worth doing next** — a `Table` by semantics, and a meter inside a panel.

**The recurring failure of this project is documentation that is right when written.** The README, the model
card, the state page, the review, the ledger and now the handoff have each been found describing a previous
version of the software — and only the numbers have been derivable by machine, which is why the numbers were
never the wrong part.

## The release order was the defect, not the check

`check-docs` has compared a front page's version with the newest tag since the beginning. It passed every time —
because it was run *after* the pages were fixed. Twenty-four releases were tagged **first** and had their pages
corrected minutes later, so for those minutes `main` named the previous release, and the check that would have
noticed was the one being run afterwards.

`docs/release.md` is now the procedure, and its order is the fix:

1. the full suite green;
2. **pages before tag** — the documents name the new version first;
3. gate and commit the documents;
4. tag and push **last**, so the commit carrying the tag already names it;
5. wait for the three assets, run `check-release`;
6. download it once and ask `version` what it is.

| step | catches |
|---|---|
| 1 | a release of code the suite never saw |
| 2 | a front page naming the previous version |
| 3 | formatting, lints, tests, drifted counts |
| 4 | nothing — mistakes stop being visible here |
| 5 | a missing platform, or an archive that does not run |
| 6 | a binary built from a tree that is not the one released |

**A sequence that lives in someone's habit is the same problem as a rule that lives in two places**, and this
project has now found both.

## v0.20.0, released in the order the procedure requires

```
every check passed in 859.2s
```

The first release made **pages first, tag last**:

- the two front pages name v0.20.0 and count 25 releases, committed through the gate — which was green because
  `check-docs` now allows a page to be **one release ahead** of the newest tag, and fails only when it names an
  older one;
- the tag was created after that commit, so at no point did `main` name the previous release.

That ordering was impossible before this round: the check demanded an exact match between page and tag, which
forced the tag first and made the documented procedure unrunnable. Both directions are proven now —

```
page ahead  (v0.20.0 while newest tag is v0.19.0) → green, "a release is in progress"
page behind (v0.18.0 while newest tag is v0.19.0) → "the state page names an older release than the newest tag"
```

**A procedure that cannot be followed is a procedure that has not been tested.**

## The procedure can be followed now

Both front pages use the same rule, and both directions are proven:

```
page ahead  (v0.21.0 in the review while the newest tag is v0.20.0) → green
page behind (v0.18.0 in the review while the newest tag is v0.20.0)  → "the review names an older release than the newest tag"
```

The state page got this in round 104 and the review in this one; until both agreed, `docs/release.md` was a
document that could not be carried out, and v0.20.0 went out tag-first because of it.

**What remains to prove it end to end is a release made in that order** — pages, commit, tag — which is the only
evidence that counts, and the next release will be it.

## v0.21.0: the first release made pages-first

```
every check passed in 751s
```

The order `docs/release.md` asks for, carried out at last: **suite green → pages updated → gate and commit →
tag**. The tag was created after the commit that names it, so `main` was never describing the previous release —
which was true of every release until v0.20.0 and false from here.

Why it could not be done before: both front pages were checked for an *exact* match with the newest tag, so
naming the next version made the gate red, and the only way to green was to tag first. A rule meant to protect
the page from going stale was forbidding the page from being early.

**The difference between a check and a procedure is who is holding it.** The checks were green for twenty-four
releases while the sequence that used them was wrong.

## What actually generalises: the assembly, not the model

First measurement of the model's **raw** rows on an application it was never trained on, using `disagree`:

| application | model disagrees with the reader on |
|---|---|
| Grok (trained) | 2.0% · 10.3% |
| Freebuff (never trained on) | 17.6% · 19.0% · 24.9% · 26.3% |

**Two to two and a half times worse** on the unseen application — and the pages are still exact there, because
the guards correct what the model gets wrong.

That is the honest shape of the claim this project has been making all month. "A second agent reads at 100%" is
true; *"the model generalises"* is not, and never was. What generalises is the assembly — the vocabulary, the
predicates asked of both builders, and the guards that hold the page together when the hundred weights guess.

Recorded in the model card under *what it cannot do*, where a reader deciding whether to trust it will look first.

## Which guards carry the 20%: the narrow ones

The disagreements on untrained material have one shape, by confusion pair:

| reader says | model says | rows |
|---|---|---|
| Block | **Thought** | 177 (session 8) · 155 (session 1) · 129 (session 4) |
| Block | **Meter** | 56 · 27 · **381** |
| Thought / ToolCall | Block | 23–90 |

**The model over-reads reasoning and meters** on screens it has never seen — ASCII banners and status lines look to
it like the rows it was taught. And what stops it is precisely the narrow rule from round 23 (a reasoning heading
ends after its word) and the one from round 39 (a meter needs a drawn limit, not a percentage).

Both were written weeks ago for a *different* purpose: the first to stop `• Thinking about the problem` becoming
reasoning, the second to stop `15.4K (1%)` becoming a meter. On an application neither was written for, they are
what holds the page together.

**A narrow rule earns its keep twice** — once when it is written, and again on material its author had not seen.
That is the opposite of what intuition says about generalisation, and it is the most useful thing this project has
learned about it.

## Training on the second agent makes the model worse — on both applications

The dataset now holds 237 structured pairs from every recording, freebuff included, and the labels are the ones
the reader produces today. Trained for 600 epochs, then measured with `disagree`:

| session | shipped model | retrained on everything |
|---|---|---|
| freebuff `second-agent` | 24.9% | **40.7%** |
| freebuff `second-agent-8` | 17.6% | **35.4%** |
| Grok `unseen-tests` | 2.0% | **10.3%** |
| Grok `cargo-tree` | 10.3% | **72.4%** |

**Worse everywhere, including the application it was already trained on.** The corpus is now 591 freebuff frames
against roughly 430 Grok ones, so the class balance has swung; and a hundred-parameter softmax has one set of
weights for one vocabulary, not two dialects of it.

This is the honest answer to the question round 4 got wrong in the other direction ("retraining changed nothing").
Retraining now *changes things*, and all of them for the worse. The shipped weights stay.

**The guards were never a stand-in for a better model — they are the mechanism**, and this is what happens when
the model is asked to carry material it cannot represent.

## Is 17.6% a data problem or a capacity problem? A bigger model reaches 94.8%

The corpus now supplies **9,250 rows, 19 features, 5 classes** through `dataset --structured`. Trained on the same
pairs with the same featurisation, but with one hidden layer of 64 units — **1,921 parameters against 100**:

```
mlp holdout accuracy: 0.948      (grouped holdout, whole screens held out, not rows)
```

**94.8% of rows, on screens it never saw, across both applications.** The shipped softmax's per-class recalls on the
same data are 54.5% (ToolCall), 88.9% (Diff), 100% (Meter, Thought) — so the comparison is not like for like, but
the shape is clear.

What that says about the 17.6%: it is **capacity**, not data. A hundred weights cannot hold two applications'
dialects; two thousand can. That is the answer to the question the last two rounds opened, and it points the
project's next step away from the guards and toward a model that could replace some of them.

**What was not finished**: the linear baseline on the *same* split, for a fair side-by-side. The script that would
have produced it failed on a parameter mix-up twice, and rather than spend the round debugging numpy, the MLP
number is recorded alone — with the caveat stated rather than glossed.

## Correction: capacity is *not* the limit — round 110 compared two different splits

Round 110 reported that a hidden layer reached 94.8% where the linear model could not, and concluded the 17.6% was a
capacity problem. `scripts/compare_capacity.py` now trains **both models on one split**, same features, same rows,
same grouped holdout:

```
rows 7346 train / 1904 held out, 19 features, 5 classes
linear, 100 parameters:   0.944
hidden, 1605 parameters:  0.948
```

**Sixteen times the parameters buys 0.4 points.** The linear model reaches 94.4% on this mixed data, so the earlier
number was the split, not the capacity — the two runs had different holdouts and I read the difference as a result.

What that leaves: the model is not capacity-limited on this data, which restores the project's own earlier finding
("it is at its ceiling, measured three ways") and reopens what the 17.6% on Freebuff actually is. The paired
measurement — `disagree`, model against reader on pages — says 2–10% on Grok and 17.6–26.3% on Freebuff, and those
are the numbers to explain, not the row accuracy of a mixed dataset.

**Two measurements of different things are not a comparison**, and the fair version of this one took ten minutes to
write once it was clear what "fair" meant.

## What the 17.6% actually is: a small row error, amplified by multi-row components

`dataset` now writes a `source` on every pair, so a measurement can ask how the model does on *this* application
rather than on a mixture. Same model, same split, held-out whole screens:

| rows from | held-out rows | row accuracy |
|---|---|---|
| a second agent (Freebuff) | 1,280 | **93.6%** |
| Grok (trained) | 624 | **96.2%** |

**A 2.6-point gap, not seventeen.** And the honest arithmetic reconciles the two numbers: `disagree` counts a
component wrong if *any* of its rows is mislabelled, so a 6.4% row error becomes a 17–26% component error on
components averaging four or five rows — `1 − (1 − 0.064)^4 ≈ 23%`.

So the earlier characterisation holds and gets sharper: **the model degrades a little on an unseen application, the
guards carry a lot, and the frightening percentage was a counting unit rather than a defect.** A component-level
error rate is the right thing to report — it is what a consumer sees — but a row-level number is what says how much
of it is the model's fault.

Four measurements, four corrections: retraining (worse), capacity (0.4 points), fairness (same split), and now the
unit (rows versus components). Every one came from measuring again rather than from arguing.

## The chain works end to end

```
$ PAIRS=… python scripts/compare_capacity.py
rows 7346 train / 1904 held out, 19 features, 5 classes
linear, 100 parameters:   0.944
hidden, 1605 parameters:  0.948
  from a second agent             1280 rows  accuracy 0.936
  from the trained application     624 rows  accuracy 0.962
```

Three small changes made that possible, and only the first was interesting:

1. `dataset` writes a **`source`** on every pair (round 112);
2. `load_pairs` keeps it, and `build_dataset` returns the list beside the rows;
3. `compare_capacity.py` splits its held-out rows by it.

**A measurement is only as good as the record underneath it.** The export had no field for "which application",
so for three rounds the only per-application number was `disagree`'s component count — a strict statistic that made
a 6.4% row error look like 17–26%. One field, written when the pair is made, replaced all of that inference.

That is the same lesson as the exit status and the build stamp: **a fact that is not recorded at the moment it is
known has to be reconstructed later, and reconstruction is where the mistakes live.**

## Every score names the model that produced it

```
model 100.0%  floor 100.0%  over 1 frame(s), trained on 434 screen(s), model 735ec1029ec704bb
```

The digest rides on the line a person reads. It is small, and it closes the gap rounds 109–112 fell into: a score
was comparable to another score only if both came from the same weights, and until this round nothing in the output
said so.

Three places now answer "what produced this": a **binary** names its build, a **recording** names how its program
ended, a **page** names its model — and the score, which compares pages, prints the model it used.

## v0.22.0: the provenance release

```
every check passed in 753s
```

Four facts about how this project's artefacts are produced, all of them written down where they are known rather
than reconstructed later:

| artefact | says what produced it | how |
|---|---|---|
| a **binary** | commit + a digest of the working tree | `ui-lm-capture version` |
| a **recording** | how its program ended | `session.json` → `exit` |
| a **page** | which weights assembled it | `surface.model`, absent on reader pages |
| a **score** | which model it measured | printed on the line, `model 735ec1029ec704bb` |

Each was added after its absence cost something: three measurements from stale binaries, a round spent concluding
that a pager "draws nothing here" when it had exited 1, and four rounds comparing pages that could not say which
model had made them.

Also in this release: `dataset` records the **application** a pair came from, and `scripts/compare_capacity.py` uses
it — which is how the 17.6% turned out to be a component-level count of a 6.4% row error rather than a defect.

## v0.22.0, verified end to end

```
checking release: v0.22.0 — 1,775 KB, three platforms
downloaded build: model 100.0% … model 735ec1029ec704bb
the archive says:  ui-lm-capture 0.1.0  commit df7dd9d  tree clean
```

**The downloaded binary prints the model digest on its score line and its own commit on `version`** — so the
provenance chain holds in the artefact a stranger receives, not only in the tree it was built from.

Released pages-first for the second time, with `main` never naming the previous release.

## Sixty per cent of the evaluation corpus cannot tell the model from a paragraph splitter

Measured the floor on every evaluation recording — the share a text-only converter recovers:

```
recordings scored: 75
at floor 100%:     45
```

**45 of 75 recording are reproduced exactly by a converter that splits paragraphs.** Those recordings still prove
something — the round trip, the audit, the panes — but they say nothing at all about which kinds a page carries,
because a page with one `Block` is what the floor produces.

Two of them are in a set called `structured`: `diff-stat` is `git diff --stat`, a summary table (`f1.txt | 3 ++-`),
and the page reading it as one block is **correct**. The name of a folder is not evidence; the floor is.

What this changes is how the project's own headline should be read. "Every evaluation set at 100%" is true, and on
60% of the corpus it is a statement about text. The sets that carry kinds are the agent recordings — Freebuff's 591
frames, `unseen`, and the thirteen hard cases — and those are where the vocabulary claim actually lives.

**A number can be true and still be about less than it appears to be.** The floor was built to say exactly this, and
until now nobody had pointed it at the whole corpus.

## The floor is printed beside every score now

```
apps        100.0%  over 18 frame(s), floor  88.9%
freebuff    100.0%  over 580 frame(s), floor  18.2%
scratch     100.0%  over 19 frame(s), floor 100.0%  (floor 100%, proves nothing about kinds)
workflow    100.0%  over  4 frame(s), floor 100.0%  (floor 100%, proves nothing about kinds)
unseen      100.0%  over 73 frame(s), floor  24.9%
```

Two sets now say out loud what round 118 measured across the corpus: `scratch` and `workflow` are reproduced exactly by
a paragraph splitter, so their 100% is a statement about text and not about the vocabulary.

The other six carry real floors — from 18.2% on Freebuff to 88.9% on `apps` — and those are the sets where "100%"
means the model found something a converter could not.

**A score printed without its floor is half a sentence**, and the half it leaves out is the one that says whether
the sentence is about the model at all.

## Curation: two recordings added, two removed, and the share of text-only material moved

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| evaluation recordings | 75 | 75 |
| at floor 100% | 45 | **43** |
| total recordings | 88 | 88 |

Two **new** agent sessions — `unseen-count` (37 frames, ToolCall 23, Meter 31, floor 21.6%) and `unseen-listing`
(30 frames, Thought 9, ToolCall 18, floor 21.2%) — and two **removed**: `scratch/chord-test` and `scratch/sep-test`
were prompt-shaped shell screens at floor 100%, near-duplicates of the two that remain.

The share of text-only evaluation material fell from 60% to **57%**, and the frames rose by 67 while the count stayed
at 88: **the corpus is denser, not longer.**

That is a better trade than either adding without removing or removing without adding — and it is only visible
because round 118 measured the floor on every recording instead of trusting a folder name.

## The 43 text-only recordings, read one by one

The question was whether any of them is a **structured screen misread as text** — the defect class this whole
exercise hunts. Reading each first line:

| shape | examples | verdict |
|---|---|---|
| pager and help text | `cargo-help`, `git-help`, `pip-help`, `wsl-vim-help`, `man-page`, `less-pager` | text, correctly one block |
| git output | `git-graph`, `git-merge-graph`, `git-status`, `diff-stat`, `log-stat` | text: a graph is a listing, a stat is a summary table |
| listings and tables | `listing`, `table`, `wsl-ls`, `wsl-ps`, `df-table`, `pip-list` | text: no `Table` kind exists, by ADR 0007's decision |
| shell transcripts | `scratch/*`, `gh-runs`, `dev-workflow` | text |
| full-screen monitors and editors | `wsl-top*`, `wsl-nano`, `wsl-vim-*`, `wsl-tmux-*` | text: `top`'s summary is a status row, not a meter |
| panel and prose | `nongrok/panel`, `nongrok/prose`, `cargo-tree` | one block is the *right* answer for a titled panel |

**Not one is a defect.** Every one of the 43 is a screen whose structure the vocabulary legitimately calls a single
`Block` — and the floor said 100% because a paragraph splitter reproduces them, which is true of a pager and of a
process table alike.

That is the useful outcome: the floor measures **the screen**, not the reader's failure. The corpus is text-heavy
because most programs print, and the kind-carrying material lives where programs *render state* — the agent
recordings, which are 18.2% to 24.9% floor and carry meters, tool calls, thoughts and diffs.

## A flake that only ubuntu could see, and the reason it existed

`test (ubuntu-latest)` failed on `06e8d26` while windows and macOS passed:

```
assertion `left == right` failed
  left: None
  right: Some(0)
```

In `a_child_runs_in_the_recorders_directory_when_none_is_configured`, four tests asserted
`d.try_wait().unwrap() == Some(0)` **immediately after** `read_to_end`. On Linux the terminal reports end-of-stream
when the child closes its output, a moment before the process is reaped — so `try_wait()` answers `None`, and the
assertion is a race that ubuntu loses occasionally and the other two platforms win.

The fix is a helper with the same deadline `read_to_end` already uses: **wait for the status rather than demand it**.

That is the exact defect this week's other lesson names — a check that fails for a reason unrelated to what it is
about teaches people to re-run it, and the next real failure gets re-run too.

## An assertion I could not make fail, reverted rather than shipped

The plan was to close the last unproven item — *"`check-html` cannot tell whether a page's arrangement matches the
screen's"* — by asserting that a paned page's stylesheet lays its panes out horizontally rather than stacking them.

Three attempts, and the mutant passed every time:

1. the assertion searched the whole document for `display: grid` **and** for `grid-template-columns`, and found
   them in different places;
2. rewritten to read one `.panes` rule, it still passed;
3. pointed at the list that actually decides the exit code, it still passed.

At that point the honest conclusion is not "one more fix" — it is that **I was editing a script whose control flow I
had not read**, and any assertion I added would have been a decoration. Both files were reverted; the tree is clean
at `e655413` and the layout item stays open.

**A check that cannot be made to fail is not a check**, which is this project's oldest rule and the one I broke while
trying to add to it.

## The layout check, done by declaring rather than parsing

Round 122 reverted an assertion that no mutant could fail. The reason was not the assertion but the source of its
evidence: it tried to read a **cascade** from a stylesheet, and the two facts it wanted (`display: grid`,
`grid-template-columns`) live in different rules.

The renderer now says what it does in the document:

```html
<div class="panes" data-layout="row">
```

and `check-html` requires it. Both directions, with the build verified between them:

```
normal                        → paned documents checked: 4
data-layout="column" mutant   → a paned page was not drawn as panes   (exit 1)
restored                      → paned documents checked: 4
```

**The old assertion also fired on the renderer change before I touched it** — adding an attribute to that div made it
report "no pane wrapper", which is a check doing its job on a change it had never seen.

What that closes from *what is not proven*: `check-html` can now tell that a paned page is laid out **side by side**,
not merely that both panes are present.

## The meter-in-a-panel decision has no material at all

The open item has been written down for weeks as *"blocked on row ownership"*. Measured today: there is **no
recording of the screen it is about**.

```
freebuff sessions:                    11
rows saying "… hours remaining":       0
rows with any quota-panel wording:     0
```

The `0/5 hours remaining` panel exists in this project only in **live attempts that failed and were discarded** — a
session that timed out, a frame with an empty pane. Every recorded Freebuff session starts after that screen has
scrolled away.

So the decision is not blocked on a rule; it is blocked on **material that was never kept**. Changing `as_meter` now
would be a change with nothing to measure it against, which is the one thing this project has been unwilling to do
since round 40.

The fix is a ten-second recording taken when the quota resets: **the startup screen of the second agent, with its
panel and its glued budget intact**. That is now the first item on the list, and it is smaller than everything that
has been written about it.

## A check that records on another machine, and the guard it needed on the day it was written

Every recording in this repository came from one Windows laptop. `scripts/check-second-machine.ps1` records a real
program on whatever machine runs it, pages it, and fails if the screen does not round trip. It runs in CI on
`ubuntu-latest`, which is the first screen this project has described that was **not recorded here**:

```
a screen recorded on Windows round trips exactly (1 frame(s))
```

And it caught itself being wrong immediately. Recorded against `cmd /c exit`, the round trip reads

```
round trip: 0/0 line(s) of content came back (100.0%), 1 drawn
```

— which contains `(100.0%)` and would have **passed**. The first version of the check accepted an empty screen as a
perfect one. It now requires a non-zero line count, and the empty case is the proof recorded in `docs/checks.md`.

**Twelve checks now refuse empty material**, and this one joined them by being written badly first.

## The same command on two machines reads as the same shape

The check now records **the same POSIX command** on both machines — through WSL on this host, directly on the Linux
runner — and asserts the *shape* rather than the text, because the files a listing names are different and always
will be:

```
here:   kinds: Block=1, Column=1      29/29 lines came back (100.0%)
runner: kinds: Block=1, Column=1      (second machine (ubuntu) success)
```

A directory listing is one block in one pane on any machine. A reader that called some of those lines meters or
reasoning would still round trip perfectly and would be wrong — so the check refuses any `Meter`, `Thought`,
`ToolCall`, `Diff` or `Frame` on a listing, and the proof that it can refuse is a page that has them:

```
applying the same rule to cases/hard/pane-conversation:
  a plain directory listing was read as Meter, which no machine's listing is
```

**Text cannot be compared between machines; shape can.** That is the difference between "it works there" and "it
reads the same thing there", and it took a check on a second machine to say it.

## Two screens, two shapes, both machines

The check now records **two** POSIX screens — a listing and a process monitor — and asserts for each: a block, no
meter or reasoning, and no panes:

```
a directory listing    1 frame(s)  kinds: Block=1, Column=1   29/29 lines came back (100.0%)
```
