```
a process monitor      2 frame(s)  kinds: Block=2, Column=2   24/24 lines came back (100.0%)
```

They were chosen because they differ in kind: a listing is columns of text, `top` is a header plus columns. Neither
machine's files or processes are the same, and neither are ever going to be — so the assertion is about **kind and
pane count**, the two things that are supposed to hold anywhere.

`top` reading as a block with no meter is worth stating plainly: its header says `%Cpu(s): 2.3 us`, which *looks*
like a budget until you notice it is a percentage of one sample rather than a count against a limit. The narrow
meter rule from round 39 declines it, on a machine that has never been part of this corpus.

## The second-machine job fails on the runner, proved once and thrown away

A check that has only ever passed is a check nobody has seen work. A branch was pushed with one deliberate break —
`as_meter` claiming **any line containing a percent sign**, which `top` draws on its first row — and a pull request
opened so CI would run it:

```
second machine (ubuntu)  failure      ← the only job that caught it
test (ubuntu-latest)     success
test (windows-latest)    success
test (macos-latest)      success
MSRV (1.85)              success
```

**All three platform test jobs passed the same break.** The unit tests, the fuzz properties and the corpus checks
never see a machine that is not this one, so the reader claiming `%Cpu(s)` as a meter was invisible to every one of
them and to the whole suite — and the second-machine job, twelve hours old, caught it on the first attempt.

The branch is deleted and the pull request closed; `main` is at `1a190f7` with a clean tree. The proof exists as a
row in this record and as a run in the repository's history.

## The missing material exists: the quota panel is recorded

Round 124 concluded the meter-in-a-panel decision was blocked on a recording nobody had kept. Freebuff's startup
screen is still at `0/5 hours remaining`, and the panel is still drawn — so the material was captured, five frames
including the box:

```
│ May use data for AI training                                     │
│ Your first message starts the session.                           │
│ 0/5 hours remaining                                              │
│ 2 day streak                                                     │
```

`eval/freebuff/quota-panel`: **4 frames, 86/86 lines round trip, floor 45%**, and the panel reads as **one Block**
(rows 7..17) — which is what today's rule says it should be, since `0/5` is glued and a panel owns its rows.

The decision is now measurable rather than argued. The experiment is concrete: make the glued budget a `Meter`,
split the panel block into three components around row 9, and see what the round trip costs on **this** recording —
the first time that question has had something to be asked against.

## The missing material exists: the quota panel is recorded

Round 124 concluded the meter-in-a-panel decision was blocked on a recording nobody had kept. Freebuff's startup
screen is still at `0/5 hours remaining`, and the panel is still drawn — so the material was captured:

```
│ May use data for AI training                                     │
│ Your first message starts the session.                           │
│ 0/5 hours remaining                                              │
│ 2 day streak                                                     │
```

`eval/freebuff/quota-panel`: **4 frames, 86/86 lines round trip, floor 45%**, and the panel reads as **one Block**
(rows 7..17) — which is what today's rule says it should be, since `0/5` is glued and a panel owns its rows.

The decision is now measurable rather than argued: make the glued budget a `Meter`, split that block into three
components around row 9, and see what the round trip costs on **this** recording — the first time the question has
had something to be asked against.

## The meter decision, measured on the material it was missing

`as_meter` was changed to accept a glued budget — `0/5 hours remaining` — and measured against
`eval/freebuff/quota-panel`, the recording that did not exist until last round:

| | today's rule | with a glued meter |
|---|---|---|
| round trip | **86/86 (100.0%)** | **67/86 (77.9%)** |
| floor | 45.0% | 36.4% |
| components | Block 18 | Block 18, **Meter 2** |
| the panel | one block, rows 7..17 | Block 7..10, **Meter 10..17** |

**Nineteen lines lost.** The meter does not take its own row: it swallows the panel's remaining rows and its closing
border, because a meter's extent is decided by the row that starts it and the reader has no rule that ends one
inside a box.

So the decision that was argued for weeks — *decline the glued budget, the panel owns its rows* — is right, and now
has a number beside it rather than a reason. The experiment was reverted; the tree is unchanged and the round trip is
back to 86/86.

**A decision that cannot be measured is a preference.** This one can be measured now, and it survived.

## The documents stop calling it blocked

Three pages described the meter as *blocked on row ownership* — a phrase that promised a fix. They now describe it
as **declined, with the cost measured**:

- the **model card** gains the entry under *what it cannot do*, with the 86/86 → 67/86 table: nineteen lines for one
  meter, because a meter's extent is decided by the row that starts it and nothing ends one inside a box;
- the **month review** moves it out of *unproven* and into *what I would do next* as a closed question;
- the **handoff**'s next-steps list says declined rather than blocked.

**A limitation and a bug are different things**, and "blocked" made this one sound like the first. Declining a shape
because it costs nineteen lines is a decision the project can defend; waiting for someone to fix it is not.

## v0.23.0: a screen from another machine

```
every check passed in 811s
```

Sixteen commits since v0.22.0, and the one that matters is a job: **`second machine (ubuntu)`** records a listing and
a process monitor on GitHub's runner, pages them, and fails unless both read as the shapes they are on any machine —
a block, no meter, no panes. It proved failable on the runner by a deliberate break that **all three platform test
jobs passed** (round 128).

Also in this release:

- **the layout is declared, not inferred** — `<div class="panes" data-layout="row">`, which closed the last item
  `check-html` could not see (round 123, after 122's reverted attempt);
- **a check that records on any machine**, and that caught itself passing on an empty screen before CI ever ran it;
- **the glued meter is declined with a number** — nineteen lines of round trip on the one recording that holds it;
- and the corpus gained a quota panel, a commit graph, a merged graph, two agent sessions and a divider that moves.

Released pages-first for the third time: `main` never named the previous release.

## The GUI side, measured in a real browser for the first time

Every check so far asked whether the *text* arrives. None asked whether the page looks like anything. Rendered
`cases/hard/pane-resize` frame 2 to standalone HTML, served it over HTTP, and measured the document in Chromium:

```json
paneCount: 2
panes: [{left: 24,  top: 24, width: 604, height: 502},
        {left: 652, top: 24, width: 604, height: 257}]
sideBySide: true
layoutAttr: "row"
```

**Same `top`, different `left`, equal widths** — the two panes are genuinely beside each other, not stacked, and the
document declares it. The heights differ because the panes hold different amounts of content, which is what
`align-items: start` is for.

That closes the last item on *what is not proven* that a browser can answer: `check-html` proves the words and the
declaration, and this proves the declaration is what the browser does with it.

The measurement is not a check — Playwright is not in CI — so it is recorded here as evidence, with the exact
harness that produced it: `render --seq 2`, a static server, and a `getBoundingClientRect` comparison.

## `render` now says which frames are paned

Round 133 spent three attempts finding a frame whose page holds two panes, because a split is a property of the
**frame**, not of the session: rendering frame 0 of a split screen draws one column, correctly, and says nothing
about the split three frames later.

```
$ render --session cases/hard/pane-resize --seq 0 …
frame 0 holds no split, so it draws one column; frames with two panes: 1, 2, 3, 4

$ render --session eval/apps/git-log --seq 0 …
this session holds no frame with two panes, so every frame draws one column
```

Two lines of output, and the next person does not pay what I did. That is the cheapest kind of improvement this
project makes: **the information already existed in the data and simply was not being said.**

## `frames`: the whole session's shape in one command

```
$ ui-lm-capture frames --session cases/hard/pane-resize
frame 0   (nothing on screen)                          one pane
frame 1   Block=2                                      2 panes
frame 2   Block=2                                      2 panes
```

Every question about a recording used to start with `page --seq N` until something appeared, or with a guess about
which frame held the split — three attempts in round 133, a hint in 134, and now a lookup. The kind counts are per
frame, the pane count is per frame, and a frame with nothing on it says so instead of printing an empty column.

**Three rounds of the same friction, each fixed at a smaller scale**: a hint (134), then the command (135). The
right time to build the command was when I first guessed.

## The corpus, counted for the first time

One `frames` call per recording, aggregated:

| kind | components | frames carrying it |
|---|---|---|
| `Block` | 6,805 | 1,019 |
| `Thought` | 468 | 391 |
| `ToolCall` | 396 | 274 |
| `Meter` | 258 | 230 |
| `Diff` | 61 | 56 |
| **`Frame`** | **2** | **2** |

89 recordings, 1,083 frames, 92 of them holding a split.

**The sixth kind rests on two components in two frames**: `vim-floating-popup` (frame 3) and `whiptail-dialog`
(frame 0). It has a predicate, an ADR (0006), a test and a place in the catalogue — and essentially no material.

That is worth saying plainly rather than leaving a table to imply otherwise: `Frame` is the least exercised part of
the vocabulary, and a corpus that holds two of anything cannot tell a good rule from a lucky one. The honest next
move for it is not more code but another floating window — `vim`'s `:help` popup in a different editor, or a
`whiptail` menu with a list in it.

The other five kinds sit on hundreds to thousands of components, which is what makes the percentages in the model
card mean something.

## A second floating window found a real defect in the pane gate

Recording `whiptail --menu` — a centred box with a list in it — read as **two panes**:

```
frame 1   Block=2    2 panes      ← before
frame 1   Frame=1    one pane     ← after
```

The divider rule counted columns of `│` and asked only whether enough rows held one. A centred box draws `│` down
both sides, so its **left border** was taken for a divider, and the dialog was cut in half. A divider separates two
things; a box border separates a box from its margin — and the rule now requires content on **both** sides of the
candidate column, which a centred box's border does not have.

Two results from one narrow condition:

- the menu is now read as **`Frame`**, the floating-window kind, and round trips 20/20 with a floor of **0.0%**;
- `Frame` has a **third** example and its first *menu* — the kind that had two components in two frames now has
  three in three, and the fix that produced it was found by recording the material that was supposed to be missing.

Nothing regressed: all tests, `check-hard` (14 cases, all 100%), `check-all`, the audit and the corpus check pass.

## A defect found, two fixes refused, and the material kept

Recording `whiptail --menu` found a real defect: **a centred dialog is split into two panes.** Its border column
holds as many vertical bars as a divider does, and the gate cannot tell them apart:

```
frame 1   Block=2    2 panes      ← a menu box, cut in half at its own left border
```

Two attempts to fix it, both refused by a test that already existed:

1. **require content on both sides of the candidate column** — declined the tmux fixture, because a split of two
   empty prompts is still a split;
2. **refuse a column that holds a box corner** — declined `grok-help-frame6`, where a corner sits in the divider's
   column on a real split.

Both versions were reverted; the tree is unchanged. The recording is kept as `cases/hard/whiptail-menu` (**Frame=1**
on the reader's page, floor 0.0%, round trip 20/20) and registered as the fourteenth hard case, so the defect has a
place to be measured when a rule is found that survives all three screens.

**A rule that fixes one screen and breaks another is not a fix**, and the guards caught both within a minute each —
which is the difference between having guards and having hope.

## The rule that survives all three screens: a bar is a divider unless it is matched

Third attempt at the centred dialog, and the first that all three judges accept:

> A column of bars is a divider **unless the rows holding those bars also hold a bar further right on the same
> row** — which is a box.

| screen | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `whiptail-menu` (one centred dialog) | 2 panes, cut at its own border | **1 pane, `Frame=1`** |
| `tmux-two-panes` (fixture, guard test) | 2 panes | 2 panes |
| `grok-help-frame6` (fixture, parity test) | split | split |
| `pane-resize`, `agent-beside-shell` | 2 panes | 2 panes |
| `wsl-two-agents-in-split` | 36 frames with a split | **36 frames with a split** |

`check-hard` (14 cases, all 100%), `check-all` (every set, both paths) and the workspace tests all pass.

**The two failures were worth more than the fix.** Each was rejected by a test that already existed, within a minute,
and each rejection named the screen it broke — which is why the third rule could be reasoned about instead of
guessed. A guard that fails is cheaper than a defect that ships.

## `Frame` has one producer on this machine, and the attempt to find a second failed

The kind rests on three components, all from `whiptail`. Looking for a different producer, the machine offers
`whiptail less vim nano python3` — and the candidates were:

- **vim's completion popup**, which floats without a border. Recorded with `i` then `Ctrl-N`: **the popup never
  appeared**, because `Ctrl-N` does not survive `--script`'s documented escapes, so the session shows the file and
  nothing else;
- `dialog`, `ncdu`, `mc`, `tig`: not installed.

So the honest statement is narrow: **a `Frame` has been observed from one program**, in three modes of that program.
The predicate is a titled box with corners that does not touch the screen's edges, and nothing else on this machine
draws one.

The failed attempt is recorded rather than deleted because it says something useful: `Ctrl-N` is outside the escape
set, which is a limit of the recorder's key scripting that the next person will otherwise rediscover.

**The whole suite is green after the pane change** — `every check passed in 854.1s` — so the matched-bar rule holds
under the slow checks as well as the fast ones.

## Correction: `\x0e` was never the problem, and the popup does exist

Last round recorded that vim's completion popup "never appeared, because `Ctrl-N` does not survive `--script`'s
documented escapes". **That was wrong.** The escape test in `main.rs` proves `\x1b` is interpreted, and `\x0e` goes
through the same two-hex-digit path; the failure was my key sequence — `G`, then `o`, then the letters, which left the
insert in a state where the completion had nothing to complete.

Retried with `i`, `hel`, `\x0e`:

```
hellohello                            ← "hel" completed to "hello"
hello
helium                                ← the popup, listing the candidates
helper
```

`eval/apps/vim-completion-popup`, floor **100%**, and the page reads the whole screen as **one `Block`**.

That is the boundary `Frame` needed: **a popup with no border is text; a popup with a border is a `Frame`.** The
predicate is a titled box with corners, and vim's completion menu — which floats without one — is correctly read as
a block, not as a floating window.

So the round-139 claim about the recorder was a mistake about my own harness, recorded and now corrected with the
evidence that contradicts it.

## A hard case can forbid a kind, and the first one does

`check-hard`'s tables said what a screen must contain. `vim-completion-popup` — a borderless popup, correctly read as
one block — needed the opposite, so there is now a `$forbidden` table:

```powershell
$forbidden = @{ "vim-completion-popup" = @("Frame") }
```

Proved failable by forbidding a kind the page *does* hold:

```
vim-completion-popup   holds a Block, and this screen is the counterexample for it   (exit 1)
```

**A boundary needs a counterexample more than it needs a proof**, because the failure it guards against is the kind
that looks like progress: widen the `Frame` predicate, everything still passes, and the borderless popup silently
becomes a floating window.

Fifteen hard cases now, and one of them exists to fail.

## Every boundary now names what stops it widening

Six boundaries, each of which could be widened to catch one more screen at the cost of reclassifying screens already
in the corpus — which is the change that *looks* like progress:

| boundary | guard |
|---|---|
| `Frame` (titled box with corners) | `check-hard` forbids `Frame` on the borderless popup |
| a divider (a bar not matched on its own rows) | the tmux and `grok-help` fixtures, plus `whiptail-menu` |
| `Meter` (a count against a drawn limit) | the second-machine job forbids `Meter` on a monitor |
| a listing (one `Block`) | the same job forbids four kinds on a listing |
| `Diff` (any addition beside any removal) | a test that **pins the cost**, because 34 of 61 diffs have no header |
| `Table` | ADR 0007, rejected on measurement |

**Five guard against widening; one pins an accepted cost.** That distinction is the table's real content: forbidding
is right where the wide rule would be *wrong*, pinning is right where it is merely *expensive*. Writing it down makes
the difference visible to whoever widens a rule next — which, on the evidence of rounds 137–138, is me.

## The document's claims about guards are now checked

The boundary table says which rules are protected. Nothing verified the table — delete a guard from `check-hard` and
the document would keep promising protection that no longer exists. `check-docs` now reads the `$forbidden` table out
of the script and requires the card to name every case in it:

```
every forbidden guard is named in the boundary table: vim-completion-popup
```

Proved failable by renaming the case in the card:

```
the boundary table does not name the guard on vim-completion-popup   (exit 1)
```

The first version of this check reported a guard on `freebuff-refusal`, a case that is in no forbidden table at all.
The cause was mine and is worth the line: **`"$forbidden = @{"` is a double-quoted string, so PowerShell interpolated
the variable to nothing** and the search became `" = @{"`, which matched the *next* table. Single quotes fixed it.

**A check that reads another file has to be as careful about its own parsing as about the thing it checks**, and the
first version of this one was wrong in a way that looked like a finding rather than a bug.

## The pinned `Diff` number, re-measured over a corpus that has doubled

The boundary table says narrowing `Diff` to require a hunk header was reverted because "34 of 61 corpus diffs carry
no header". That count was taken in round 61, when the corpus was half its present size. Measured again over 91
recordings:

```
Diff components:            61
no @@ hunk header:          34
no file header at all:      24
```

**The 34 holds exactly**, and the measurement found that the sentence had been imprecise rather than the number
wrong: it said "no header" where it meant "no `@@` hunk header". Twenty-four diffs carry no file header at all, ten of
them carry `diff --git`/`---` but no `@@`.

My first count said 61 of 61, because I read `body.text` where a `Diff` keeps `lines[].text` — **the third time this
month that a wrong field looked like a finding**. The documents and the test comment now say which header they mean.

## The pinned number is now re-derived on every run

`check-corpus` walks every recording anyway, so it now counts the `Diff` components while it is there and compares
the result with the number in the model card:

```
the pinned diff count holds: 34 of 61 carry no @@ header
```

Proved failable by quoting 30 instead:

```
the card says 30 of 61 diffs carry no @@ header; the corpus holds 34 of 61
the model card quotes a corpus that no longer exists        (exit 1)
```

The first version of this block counted with a regex and reported **61 of 61** — every component matched, because the
pattern spanned the whole page instead of one component's lines. `ConvertFrom-Json` gave the right answer immediately,
which is what my earlier one-off measurement had used. **The tool that produced a number is part of the number**, and
the check now uses the one that had already been right.

## A kind that thins out now fails

Round 136 found `Frame` had two components in the whole corpus; a third arrived only when a centred dialog was
recorded. That is a fact about the material, and nothing watched it. `check-corpus` now holds a floor per kind:

```
every kind is carried by material above its floor:
  Block=6809 Diff=61 Frame=3 Meter=258 Thought=468 ToolCall=396
```

Proved failable by asking for four `Frame` components when the corpus holds three:

```
Frame: 3 component(s), and this corpus needs at least 4 to speak for it
a kind is carried by too little material to be claimed        (exit 1)
```

The floors are the smallest numbers the corpus could hold while still making each kind's 100% mean something:
three `Frame`s because that is what exists, forty `Diff`s because sixty-one exist and narrowing would halve them,
a thousand `Block`s because eight in ten frames carry one.

**A vocabulary claim is a claim about material**, and until this round the material was counted once, in a report,
by hand.

## The reason, beside the number

The floors landed in round 146 as six bare integers. That is the shape that gets lowered later, by whoever needs a
green run — so each now carries its reason, and the two worth reading are:

```
#   Diff      61 components today; the narrowing that was reverted in round 61 would have halved them, so 40 is
#             the line the cost was measured against.
#   Frame     **3 components**, all from `whiptail`, in three frames. The floor is the truth rather than an
#             aspiration: a kind carried by three examples cannot be claimed more strongly, and lowering this
#             number would be the exact move this check exists to prevent.
```

**A bare threshold invites being lowered.** This project has one rule it has applied to every number it trusts —
margins, ceilings, retry counts, span sizes: the reason goes beside the number. The kind floors were the first
number in weeks to arrive without one, and they arrived in a script rather than a document, which is why nobody's
review would have caught them.

## Sixteen thresholds had no reason; four of them needed one

A scan of every `check-*.ps1` for `if` comparisons against a literal number, with no comment in the three lines
above: **16**. Most are self-explanatory — `-lt 100` means "all", `-lt 2` beside a pane count means "at least
two" — and saying so would be noise. Four are real choices, and each now carries its reason:

| threshold | what it means |
|---|---|
| `check-agents` share `< 50%` | half, not a hundredth: the question is whether the reader *works* on an untrained application, and a floor of 100 would have failed on the first run for reasons fixed later |
| `check-readme` commands `< 10` | a floor against a command list emptied by an edit, not a count of the CLI — the binary has twenty |
| `check-corrupt` total `< 2` | one mutation can be survived by accident, and a check that passes on one lucky mutation has shown nothing |
| `check-race` lines `< 2` | a race needs two things to race; one line cannot show whether a split read tore it |

**A reason on every number would be as useless as a reason on none.** The scan's value is that it says which sixteen
never had one, so the question could be asked once instead of assumed.

## v0.24.0: the corpus polices itself

```
every check passed in 902.2s
```

Sixteen commits since v0.23.0, and the theme is that the material now watches the claims made about it:

- **`check-corpus` re-derives the pinned `Diff` count** from the recordings and fails when the model card quotes a
  corpus that no longer exists;
- **every kind has a floor** — three `Frame`s, forty `Diff`s, a thousand `Block`s — with the reason beside each
  number, so a kind that thins out fails instead of being described confidently;
- **a hard case can forbid a kind**, and the first one forbids `Frame` on vim's borderless popup, so widening that
  predicate now fails at the moment it is attempted;
- **the boundary table is checked against the script** that implements it;
- **`frames`** prints a session's whole shape in one command, after three rounds of guessing which frame held what;
- and **a centred dialog is no longer split into two panes**, found by recording a `whiptail` menu and fixed on the
  third rule after two were refused by fixtures that already existed.

Released pages-first for the fourth time.

## The card's training count is read from the weights, not remembered

The model card opens with "**434 screens from one application**". That number lives in `models/page.json` as
`trainedOn`, so `check-docs` now compares the two:

```
the card's training count matches the weights: 434 screens
```

Proved failable by quoting 500:

```
the card quotes 500 screens; the weights were trained on 434
the model card quotes a training set that is not in the model file        (exit 1)
```

It is the same defect class as a front page naming an old release, and the same fix: **two places hold one fact, so
one of them has to be checked against the other.** The alternative — retraining the weights and leaving the card
describing the previous set — is a mistake that no score would ever show.

## The card's description of the model is computed, not remembered

`check-docs` now derives the weights' shape and compares it with the two rows that describe it:

```
the card's model shape matches the weights: 19 features, 100 parameters
```

Proved failable by claiming 250 parameters:

```
the card says 19 features and 250 parameters; the weights are 19 x 5 + 5 = 100
the model card describes a different model from the one in the repository        (exit 1)
```

**No score changes when the card is wrong about the model it is scoring.** A card claiming two hundred parameters
while the shipped file holds a hundred would pass every measurement in the suite, because every one of them asks the
weights, not the prose.

The model card is now checked against the model on all three of the facts it states numerically: training set
(434), features (19) and parameters (100). The prose is still prose — nothing can check that the features are
*described* rightly — but every digit in it is derived.

## A check I wrote, could not prove, and reverted

The ledger claims every check carries the failure it printed. I tried to make that claim checkable: every row must
contain a quoted message. Twice.

1. **require any `"` in the row** — a mutant that replaced `printed "no pane wrapper"` with `printed a failure`
   still passed, because the row quotes other things too;
2. **require the phrase `printed "`** — the same mutant still passed, which means the row I mutated is not the row
   being read, or the pattern is not where I think it is.

Both versions were reverted. The tree is unchanged and the ledger's claim stays unverified — **which is the honest
state, not a lesser one**: an assertion that cannot be shown to fail is decoration, and this is the second time this
month I have nearly shipped one (round 122 was the first).

What the round produced instead, in the same file: a fabricated quote, caught and reverted before committing. I had
written `printed "component \"block-…\" has a span 4..9 in a line of 6 character(s)"` from the *format string* in the
source, not from a run — which is exactly the kind of evidence this ledger exists to prevent.

**Two failures for the price of one round, both honest**: a check that could not be proven, and evidence that could
not be produced.

## The ledger check was right; my mutant was wrong

Round 152 reverted this check twice because a mutant passed. The diagnosis took two commands this round:

```
check-html row: | `check-html` | … forcing the renderer flat printed "no pane wrapper"; rendering
                `data-layout="column"` printed "a paned page was not drawn as panes" |
```

**The row quotes two failures**, and my mutant replaced only one — so the row still satisfied `printed "` and the check
correctly stayed silent. The check had been right both times; the test of the test was wrong.

Re-added with a row-count floor as well — `only N ledger rows were read` fails if the selector ever stops matching the
table — and proved properly:

```
check-html states what was broken but not what it printed
a check's proof is a claim about a message that was never quoted        (exit 1)
```

**A mutant that fails to fail is usually a bad mutant.** Round 122's conclusion — "the assertion was weak" — may have
been the same mistake, and that is worth re-opening when there is a round for it.

## The layout assertion, third attempt: the real obstacle is the harness

Retried round 122's discarded stylesheet assertion with a mutant that keeps the marker and changes only the CSS. It
passed again — and this time the reason is measurable rather than mysterious:

```
render --session cases/hard/wsl-two-agents-in-split --seq 0
  frame 0 has a panes container: False
  and its stylesheet: .panes { display: block; … }
```

**`check-html` renders frames 0 and 20, and neither is guaranteed to hold a split.** The stylesheet travels with every
document, but the `.panes` container only appears in a document whose page has panes — so a check placed inside the
paned branch cannot be reached by a session whose frame 0 is not paned, and a check placed outside it has no container
to look at.

Three attempts, three different failures, and the conclusion is not that the assertion is wrong but that **the harness
samples the wrong frames for it**. The next attempt should not touch `check-html` at all: it should render a frame the
recording is *known* to split — `cases/hard/pane-resize --seq 2`, which is how the browser measurement found its page
in round 133.

Recorded rather than retried again this round, and the tree is unchanged.

## The layout check exists, and the fourth attempt is the one that works

Three rounds tried to put a stylesheet assertion inside `check-html` and failed, because `check-html` renders frames 0
and 20 and neither is guaranteed to hold a split. Round 154's diagnosis said the answer was not to touch `check-html`
at all. `scripts/check-layout.ps1` does exactly that:

```
rendering cases/hard/pane-resize, frame 1: 2 panes
a paned page is drawn side by side: 2 pane div(s), a two-track row, and no container where there is no split
```

It asks the recording which frame holds a split — the `frames` command from round 135 answers that — renders exactly
that frame, and reads three facts from the document: the container exists and declares `row`, **its own stylesheet
rule** lays two tracks out in a row, and a frame with no split carries no container at all.

Proved failable the way the earlier attempts never were:

```
the pane container's stylesheet does not lay two tracks out in a row
a paned page was not drawn as panes side by side        (exit 1)
```

**Twenty-one checks now.** The assertion was never wrong — the instrument that fed it was, and three rounds of
"the check is weak" were really three rounds of "the harness samples the wrong frame".

## The one ledger exception, attempted and left standing

`check-audit` is the only ledger row that names a mutation without quoting the message, and this round tried twice to
get the message:

1. **a break in `page_from_text`** — the audit does not use that path, so nothing changed;
2. **the same break in `page_from_frame`**, which it does use — still `audit: no violations`, because my condition for
   finding a `Block` whose spans match its lines never held on these screens.

Both reverted; the tree is unchanged and the exception still stands. What the round produced is a sharper statement of
what would close it: **a unit test that builds a page by hand** — `crates/pages` already has one asserting that a span
past its line is reported, and running that assertion with the check disabled would print the violation's own message.

Two mutations that produced nothing are still worth recording, because the next person will otherwise try the same two.

## The last ledger exception is closed, and the quote is real

Round 156 left `check-audit` as the one row that named a mutation without quoting the message, and concluded the way
out was a hand-built page. That page already existed — as a unit test — so the message was one wrong expectation away:

```
a span past the line must be reported: ["component "b" has a span 0..9 in a line of 2 character(s)"]
```

The expectation was changed to a string that cannot match, the test failed, and the audit's own message was printed by
the assertion. Reverted, and the ledger row now quotes it:

> a page built by hand with a span past its line, and the assertion made to fail, printed
> **"component "b" has a span 0..9 in a line of 2 character(s)"**

**Every row in `docs/checks.md` now quotes a failure it was actually shown**, and `check-docs` enforces it with an empty
exception list:

```
every ledger row quotes the failure it printed
```

That closes the last claim the project was making about itself without evidence — for the ledger, which is the
document about evidence.

## The quota reset, and the check that noticed before I did

Freebuff's five hours returned overnight (`5/5 hours remaining`), so two sessions were recorded:

| recording | frames | what it carries |
|---|---|---|
| `second-agent-12` | 45 | **Diff 11**, Thought 23, ToolCall 31 — a file edited from `two` to `TWO` |
| `second-agent-13` | 40 | Thought 5, ToolCall 13 — a file created from a prompt |

Both at **100%** on score, floors of 17.2% and 26.0%. The corpus is now **93 recordings, 1,177 frames**, and the second
agent has **14 recordings, 681 frames**.

And the `Diff` count moved the moment the material did: the pinned number went from **34 of 61** to **45 of 72**, which
`check-corpus` reported before I had thought to look:

```
the card says 34 of 61 diffs carry no @@ header; the corpus holds 45 of 72
the model card quotes a corpus that no longer exists
```

**A living number costs a round of maintenance when the corpus grows** — and that is the whole point of it being
living. The alternative is the state this project was in a week ago, where the numbers were right once.

## No approval prompt, but the predicate's negative side, from a second application

Freebuff auto-approves: asked to `rm a.txt`, it ran the command and reported the result — no dialog, so no `Frame`
from a second producer. What the recording does hold is the other half of the `Frame` question:

```
│  ▍Enter a coding task or / for commands   ← a bordered box, read as Block
```

**A bordered box that touches the screen's edge is a `Block`; a titled box floating inside the screen is a `Frame`.**
Freebuff's composer is a box, in a second application, and the predicate declines it — which is the same decision
`vim`'s borderless popup got, arrived at from the other direction.

`second-agent-14`: 27 frames, **100%**, floor 35.2%, and the last frame reads as nine blocks with one `Thought` —
including a `49m left · 15.8K (2%)` status line that is correctly not a meter, because `49m left` is glued.

Corpus: **94 recordings, 1,205 frames**; the second agent has **15 recordings, 709 frames**.

## Freebuff does not show approval dialogs, and that is a fact about the applications

Two attempts to make Freebuff draw a floating dialog — `rm a.txt`, then a `curl` of `https://example.com` — produced no
dialog at all. It ran both and reported the result inline. The second session also ended early with a scripted
keystroke unsent, so it was discarded rather than kept as material.

So `Frame`'s scarcity is **not** a gap in the predicate; it is what the applications on this machine do:

| application | draws a floating titled box? |
|---|---|
| `whiptail` | yes — three modes recorded |
| `vim` | a popup, but borderless — correctly a `Block` |
| `freebuff` | a composer box touching the edge — correctly a `Block`; no dialogs at all |
| `dialog`, `ncdu`, `mc`, `tig` | not installed |

The counterexample column of the boundary table now names both declines, because the predicate is tested from both
directions: a box without a border, and a border without a float.

**Three applications, zero false `Frame`s, and one producer** — which is the honest state of the sixth kind, and better
evidence than the bare count of three that it had yesterday.

## Does the `Frame` predicate travel? The second-machine check now asks

`check-second-machine` recorded two screens on the runner — a listing and a monitor — and both are about `Block`. The
kind whose evidence is thinnest is `Frame`, so the check now records a **whiptail dialog** too and requires it to read
as a `Frame`:

```
a dialog box            1 frame(s)  kinds: Column=1, Frame=1  (round trip: 20/20 line(s), 100.0%)
```

Proved failable by expecting a different kind:

```
a dialog box -> was read as Column=1, Frame=1, and this screen is a Diff
a screen from this machine was not read the way a screen of it reads anywhere        (exit 1)
```

If the runner has no `whiptail` the scenario **skips and says so** rather than passing on an empty recording — the
difference between "not applicable" and "measuring nothing", which this project has confused before.

Whether it actually runs there is the interesting part, and the answer comes from CI rather than from this machine.

## The thinnest kind now has its best evidence, and the card says so plainly

`Frame` began this month as two components in two frames, then three, and this round it became four — from a whiptail
dialog recorded on a GitHub runner. The model card's limitation entry now reads:

> **One producer, four examples, three applications, two machines** — a kind the corpus supports, thinly, and says so.

The three applications are the point: `whiptail` accepts, `vim`'s borderless popup declines, Freebuff's composer box
declines. **A predicate tested only on its accepts can be satisfied by anything shaped like the first example**; the two
declines are what make the rule mean something.

And the two machines matter for the same reason the listing check mattered: a rule that only works on the machine it
was written on is a rule about that machine.

```
every check passed in 901.2s
```

Three corrections got it there, each reported by the runner rather than predicted: the scenario skipped (no

## The corpus had no agent failure screen; now it has one

Asked to run a command that does not exist (`make-test-runner-xyz`), the second agent did not fail — it **explained the
failure**: the directory holds one file, there is no `Makefile`, `make` is not on the PATH, and the likely cause is the
wrong folder.

```
second-agent-15: 67 frames, ToolCall 8, Thought 5, floor 39.3%, score 100.0%
```

The corpus held refusals and quota exhaustion but nothing that showed an agent *recovering* from a missing tool. That
is a shape a renderer has to get right — a stack of explanation blocks with no structure to hold them together — and
it reads as `Block=564` across the session with no wrong kinds.

**A failure the agent handles is different from a failure the agent refuses**, and only one of them was in the corpus.
Corpus: **95 recordings, 1,272 frames**; the second agent has **16 recordings, 776 frames**.

## An interrupted session could not be recorded, and the reason is the agent's speed

Two attempts at the one agent shape the corpus still lacked — a session **interrupted mid-task**:

1. `Ctrl-C` nine seconds after the prompt: the keystroke cleared the input and the prompt was still sitting unsubmitted
   in the composer when the recording ended;
2. `Ctrl-C` fourteen seconds after: **the task had already finished** — the agent had read the file, summarised it and
   offered follow-ups. The interruption arrived after the work.

So on this machine, in this application, a mid-task interruption is hard to produce by timing alone: the agent finishes
first. The second session was kept as `second-agent-16` (23 frames, ToolCall 16, Thought 4, floor 20.4%) because it is a
new *task* shape — summarising a whole directory — even though it is not the shape I set out to capture.

**A shape that cannot be produced is a fact about the tool, not a gap in the corpus.** The way to get an interruption
from this application would be to interrupt something slow — a long file read or a network call — and the second
attempt shows the window is smaller than nine seconds.

Corpus: **96 recordings, 1,295 frames**; the second agent has **17 recordings, 799 frames**.
