```
│ 1 Me   2 My employer   3 Both   4 Nobody, I stick to free tools   0 Doesn't apply
│ 1–4 pick · 0 doesn't apply · esc not now (3 days)
```

— and reads it as **`Block`**, which is right: the panel is full width, and the `Frame` predicate is a box that floats
*inside* the screen. Another decline, from a third application, on a shape the corpus did not have.

`second-agent-17`: 57 frames, 100%, floor 20.0%. The Corpus is **97 recordings, 1,353 frames**; the second agent has
**18 recordings, 857 frames**.

**The dialog I have been chasing since round 159 does not exist in this application** — the two menus it draws are a
survey and the composer, both full width. That is now a fact from three attempts across three different prompts,
rather than a guess.

## The handoff had gone three releases stale, and now it is checked

The document a successor reads first said **"Nineteen checks… v0.19.0… 88 recordings, 1,019 frames"** while v0.25.0 was
the newest tag and the corpus held ninety-seven recordings. None of the front-page checks covered it, so nothing
noticed.

Two changes:

- the paragraph now says **twenty-one checks, 15 hard cases, v0.25.0, 97 recordings, 1,353 frames**, and names the
  fourth application (`whiptail`) and the second machine;
- `check-docs` now reads `HANDOFF.md` and applies the same rule the front pages get — the newest release it names may
  be one ahead of the tag and must never be behind:

```
the handoff names v0.19.0; the newest tag is v0.25.0
the handoff names an older release than the newest tag        (exit 1)
```

**A document that is checked for its commands but not its numbers will keep its commands right and its numbers
wrong** — which is exactly the shape this handoff had.

## The corpus counts are checked in every document that states them

Round 167 put the handoff under the *release* check. Its **counts** were still unverified — the corpus-count block read
one page, so the handoff could quote `88 recordings` while the state page was correct, and did.

The block now loops over all three describing documents:

```
HANDOFF.md says 88 recordings, the corpus holds 97
a document counts the corpus wrongly        (exit 1)
```

**A document is checked for what its reader will act on.** For the state page that is the release and the counts; for
the handoff it is every number a successor might quote without re-deriving — and the commands, which `check-readme`
already covered.

Seventh stale document this month, third turned into a check. The pattern has not varied: prose is verified only when
it becomes a claim a script can read.

## An extension of `check-layout` that did not work, reverted

`check-layout` examines one recording. Extending it to walk every recording with a split (`-All`) looked like a
morning's work: the `frames` command already answers which frame is paned, and fifteen recordings have one.

It did not work. The rewritten script reported `no recording holds a frame with two panes` — the same message as a
corpus with none — while the identical function, called from a two-line script against the same path, returned `1`.
Both files were reverted; the check is unchanged and still passes.

**What I did not establish is which of the two scripts was lying**, and that is the honest state: a diagnostic that
takes longer than the change it is diagnosing should be left for a round that can finish it. The lesson from round 122
applies unchanged — an edit whose failure I cannot explain is an edit I should not keep.

## The `-All` extension: three failures, and the diagnosis that should come before a fourth attempt

Round 169 reverted this extension without understanding it. This round established the parts and still could not land
it:

| attempt | symptom |
|---|---|
| the original rewrite | `no recording holds a frame with two panes` — the same message a corpus with none gives |
| a probe in `%TEMP%` | threw `build first` — `$PSScriptRoot` was the temp folder, so it resolved a capture binary that does not exist. **The probe, not the script, was wrong** |
| with the repo pinned | the scaffolding works: **97 sessions, 15 paned**, found with a `[regex]` object |
| rebuilt on that | found the session, then passed `System.Collections.Hashtable` where a path belonged, and after switching to a `PSCustomObject`, the same shape again |

The scaffolding is proven; the loop is not. Both reverted again, and the honest position is that this needs a round
that starts by reading the file it edits rather than by rebuilding it.

**What is established, and worth keeping**: the paned-detection loop that works is four lines with a `[regex]` object,
and it finds fifteen recordings. Anyone attempting this again should start there rather than with a function.

## What is now known about the `-All` extension, and where it stops

Third attempt, this time with the scratch file beside the original so `$PSScriptRoot` was right. Narrows the failure to
one place:

| checked | result |
|---|---|
| `$sessions` | **97**, first is `eval/apps/cargo-help` |
| `$capture` | the right binary inside the repository |
| the same regex over the same sessions, in a standalone probe | **15 paned recordings found** |
| the same regex inside this script's loop | **zero matches**, so the loop's `frames` call returns nothing |

Everything up to the loop is proven and everything the loop needs is proven elsewhere; the failure is in the loop body,
and a debug insertion did not land either. Deleted; `check-layout` is unchanged and passing.

**Three rounds, one conclusion worth acting on**: this is a job for a round that starts by making the loop print
something, not for one that starts by rewriting the script. The next attempt should add a single `Write-Host` inside
the existing loop and nothing else — the previous three all changed the code they were trying to observe.

## The `-All` extension landed, and the bug was a doubled backslash

Four rounds. The cause, found by making the loop print instead of rewriting it:

> the regex that finds a paned frame was written through a **Python string**, which doubled the backslashes — `\s`
> became `\\s`, which matches a literal backslash and never whitespace. Every attempt reported *"no recording holds a
> frame with two panes"*, which was true of the pattern and false of the corpus.

Written literally, the same four lines find **fifteen** recordings, and `check-layout` now walks all of them:

```
every paned page is drawn side by side: 15 recording(s), 15 document(s)
```

Failable as before — stacking the stylesheet prints `pane-resize (frame 1): its stylesheet does not lay two tracks out
in a row`.

**`\s` written through two languages is `\\s`**, and the corpus was blamed for it three times. The method that found it
was the one I prescribed in round 171 and should have used in 169: make the loop print, change nothing else.

## The `\\s` bug was an incident, not a class — and `Meter`'s second producer is still missing

Two checks this round, both negative:

**Every script was scanned for a doubled backslash in a regex** — the bug that cost four rounds. Result: **zero**. It
only ever existed in files I generated through Python and never committed, so the repository was never wrong.

**A non-agent meter was attempted.** `git clone --progress` prints `Receiving objects: 45% (45/100)`, which has the
`N/M` shape the rule wants and comes from a program that is not an agent. The clone stalled at `Cloning into …` —
this machine's network is restricted — so the recording holds one line and no progress bar. Discarded.

`Meter` therefore still has one observed producer, and the model card already says so.

**And the check that landed last round was timed**: `check-layout` covers fifteen recordings in **29.7 seconds**, which
is affordable in a suite that takes fifteen minutes and would not have been affordable at a minute per recording.

**A scan that finds nothing is still a result** — it converts "is this bug elsewhere?" into "it is not", which is the
question I would otherwise carry into the next round.

## v0.26.0: the layout check, and four documents that police each other

```
every check passed in 1151.4s
```

Ten commits since v0.25.0. The one that took the longest to earn is small:

- **`check-layout` walks every paned recording** — fifteen of them, in 29.7 seconds — rendering the frame each one is
  *known* to split at, and failing if the stylesheet stacks them. It cost four rounds, and the cause was a doubled
  backslash written through Python: `\s` became `\\s`, which never matches whitespace, so the check blamed the corpus
  three times.
- **the handoff is checked** for its release and its corpus counts, after being found three releases stale — it is the
  document a successor reads first.
- **corpus counts are checked in every document that states them**, not just the state page.

Released pages-first for the sixth time.

## The live viewer's content check, attempted and reverted

`check-live-page` proves three things about `ui-lm-serve`: the page changes while the program draws, contains the
program's output (`tick`), and holds still when the recording ends. It does **not** prove the served document holds
every word of the recording — the claim `check-html` makes for rendered files.

I tried to add it by reading the recording back through `tui` and requiring each word in the served HTML. Four
attempts, each reporting `only 1 word(s) were available to compare`:

- `tui --all` → nothing at all;
- `tui` (last frame) → one word, because the program had finished;
- `tui --seq 2` → one word;
- a loop over the first nine frames → one word, which is the same result and therefore says the fault is in how I am
  calling `tui` here, not in which frame I choose.

Reverted; `check-live-page` is unchanged and passing. **The undiagnosed part is worth naming**: the same `tui` command
works everywhere else in this repository, so the difference is something about this script's state at that moment —
most likely `$session` no longer being the directory I think it is by the time the control viewer has run.

**A registered check is not a place to experiment.** The addition goes back when I can say why it failed.

## The viewer's content check landed, and the obstacle was my own threshold

Round 175 reverted this after four attempts reported `only 1 word(s) were available`. The probe explains it in one
line:

```
PROBE tui lines=20 first=[tick 22]
```

`tui` was returning twenty lines all along. The program prints `tick 22`, so **`tick` is the only word of four or more
characters on the screen** — my `-ge 4` filter was measuring its own threshold, not the document.

With lines as the unit, `check-live-page` now asserts that the served document holds the recording's own words:

```
the served document holds the recording's own text: 19 word(s) checked across 19 line(s)
```

Failable by demanding a word that is not served:

```
the served document is missing 19 word(s), such as tick        (exit 1)
```

**A filter is a measurement too**, and this one silently reduced a twenty-line screen to a single word — then reported
that as a fact about the viewer.

## `check-layout` now walks both builders, which needed a flag that did not exist

The check rendered the reader's pages only. `check-html` has covered both builders for weeks, and the model's pane path
diverged once before — so the builder whose layout was least checked was the one with the worst history.

It could not be checked: `frames` — the command that says which frame holds a split — had no `--model`, so there was no
way to find a paned frame on the model's side. This round added it, mirroring how `render --model` already worked:

```
frames --session cases/hard/pane-resize --model models/page.json
  frame 1   Block=2     2 panes
```

And `check-layout` loops over both flavours:

```
every paned page is drawn side by side: 30 recording(s), 30 document(s)
```

Failable as before — stacking the stylesheet prints `pane-resize (reader, frame 1): its stylesheet does not lay two
tracks out in a row`.

**A check can only see what the tools can list**, and the tool that lists frames had one builder in it.

## `compare` can compare the model's pages, which is a consistency check nobody could run

Two recordings of one scenario should settle the same screens; `compare` measures that for the reader's pages, and the
flag to measure it for the model's did not exist. It does now:

```
compare --left … --right … --model models/page.json
  compared 5 frame(s): structure 100.0%, text 100.0%
```

That is what makes "the model is deterministic" a measurement rather than a property of Rust code nobody can inspect.

**Four commands still have no `--model`** — `provenance`, `self-check`, `baseline`, `dataset` — and this round says why
that is fine for three of them: `baseline` and `dataset` are about the reader's pages by definition, and `self-check`
compares the label path with the reader. `provenance` is the one that might want it, and the next round that needs it
will add it.

**An audit of which commands take a model is cheap and it found the flag missing where a check needed it** — the same
shape as `frames --model` in round 177.

## A question that had never been asked, and its answer

`provenance` enforces one rule: a component whose text appeared in an earlier frame must be marked `Carried`. It had
only ever been asked of the reader's pages. This round added `--model` and asked the other builder over the corpus:

| builder | components | carried | contradictions |
|---|---|---|---|
| reader | 10,147 | 8,422 | **47** |
| model | 10,147 | **0** | **8,375** |

**The model's pages never mark anything carried**, because `Model::page` sees one frame at a time and cannot know what
came before. That is a property of the API, not of the weights — and it was invisible until a flag existed to ask.

Two things follow, and only one of them is work:

- the model card now states it as a limitation, with both columns;
- the reader's own **47 contradictions** are a separate and smaller question this measurement surfaced by accident.

**A check that can only see one builder will keep reporting zero for the other.** Second time this week — round 177 was
`frames --model` — and the first time the answer was "the other builder is far worse".

## The reader's 47 provenance contradictions live in four paned recordings

`check-all` reports 0 provenance contradictions because it covers the evaluation sets. Asked of the whole corpus, the
reader contradicts itself 47 times — and every one is in a **paned hard case**:

| recording | components | carried | contradictions |
|---|---|---|---|
| `wsl-agent-in-split` | 327 | 270 | **30** |
| `wsl-two-agents-in-split` | 697 | 582 | **13** |
| `pane-conversation` | 84 | 67 | **2** |
| `two-agents-one-screen` | 34 | 19 | **2** |

Four of the fifteen paned recordings, and no others. The rule is simple — a component whose text appeared in an earlier
frame must be marked `Carried` — so a split screen is the one place it can be wrong: content that a pane loses and
regains as the split changes can read as new when the page still calls it carried.

**That is a hypothesis, not a conclusion**: the two frames I inspected held no panes and no repeated text, so the
contradictions happen later in those sessions than I looked. The next round should print the offending components
rather than infer them — `provenance` reports a count and not what it counted, which is the same gap this check was
supposed to close elsewhere.

**A count without a list is a measurement without a subject.** It is enough to know the number is 47 and the shape is
"paned"; it is not enough to fix it.

## `provenance` now names what it counted, and the names say the rule and the builder disagree

Round 180 left the 47 contradictions without a subject. The check now prints the offenders, capped at twelve:

```
block-a44ca591afc62fd7 -> carried, but its text is new: "     ♦ Thought for 0.4s   …"
block-afdea8d3b80e7678 -> carried, but its text is new: "  ╭─────────────────────── …"
```

Every one reads **`carried, but its text is new`** — never the other way round. That is the shape of a **rule
mismatch**, not a page defect:

- the **builder** decides `carried` from row overlap — the same rows are still on screen, so the component is carried;
- the **checker** decides `repeated` from exact text equality — `♦ Thought for 0.4s` became `0.5s`, a border's padding
  changed width, and the two strings are simply not equal.

A duration that ticks and a box whose padding follows its content will always fail an exact-text test while being
carried in every sense a reader cares about. **The checker's definition is the one that is wrong**, and it took printing
the components to see it — the count alone said "47", which sounds like 47 broken pages and is not.

My first hypothesis — blank blocks — was wrong: those blocks hold content past the forty-eighth character, and skipping
whitespace-only text changed the count by zero. Reverted.

`provenance` prints the list now; the rule it applies is unchanged and the 47 stand, with a name attached.

## Two definitions tried, neither explains the 47

Round 181 showed every contradiction reads `carried, but its text is new`. Two candidate causes, both tested:

| hypothesis | test | result |
|---|---|---|
| the checker compares **text** while the builder compares **raw lines** | compare the component **id** instead (the builder hashes raw lines into it) | **47, unchanged** |
| the components are blank padding | skip whitespace-only text | **47, unchanged** |

Reverted both. What that leaves is narrower and more useful: **the builder marks these carried for a reason the checker
cannot reproduce from the page alone** — neither the joined body lines nor the component id equals what the builder
remembered.

The next instrument is therefore not in the checker at all. The builder's `appeared.insert(base)` is where the answer
is, and one `eprintln!` under an environment variable would say which base it matched and from which frame. **Every
attempt so far has instrumented the observer instead of the observed** — the same mistake as the doubled backslash in
rounds 169 to 172, one level up.

## The builder now says why, and the answer is that it carries a different component

An env-gated line in the builder, where the decision is actually made:

```
$ UI_LM_DEBUG_CARRIED=1 ui-lm-capture page --session cases/hard/wsl-agent-in-split --all
carried block-80dc26053a46c423 rows [7, 8]: "     ♦ Thought for 0.4s   …"
```

That is the instrument round 182 concluded was needed, and it settles the shape of the disagreement: **the builder
carries `block-80dc26053a46c423`, while the checker flagged `block-a44ca591afc62fd7`** — two different hashes for what
looks like the same line.

They differ because `block_id` hashes the **raw rows, padding included**, and a pane whose width changes changes every
row's trailing spaces. So the builder carries one hash while the page materialises another, and a checker that
reconstructs text from the body can match neither.

That is not a page defect and not a checker defect: it is **two components that read alike and are not alike**, which is
exactly why `unique_id` exists in the first place.

The flag stays: `UI_LM_DEBUG_CARRIED=1` costs nothing when unset, and it is the first instrument in this repository that
answers a question about the builder from inside the builder.

## The 47 are pinned, not fixed

Four rounds established that the provenance contradictions are two definitions of "carried" meeting — the builder
hashes raw rows including padding, a pane's width rewrites that padding, and the page materialises a hash the checker
cannot reconstruct. Fixing it means exporting the builder's own decision, which is a change to a core function that
deserves its own round.

What this round did instead is the project's pattern for an accepted cost: **pin it, with the reason beside the
number**.

```
provenance contradictions: 47 of a pinned 47 (paned recordings only, explained in round 183)
```

Proved failable by pinning 20:

```
the corpus holds 47 provenance contradiction(s); the pinned ceiling is 20
more pages contradict themselves than this project has accounted for        (exit 1)
```

**A defect with a number and a reason is a decision; a defect with neither is a mystery.** The next corpus addition
that makes this worse now fails a check, and the next round that touches provenance has a target to move rather than a
number to rediscover.

## v0.27.0: the model card's every claim is now checkable

```
every check passed in 1065.7s
```

Ten commits since v0.26.0, and the theme is the second builder:

- **`provenance --model`** answered a question never asked, and the answer was **8,375 contradictions** — the model
  cannot mark anything carried because `Model::page` sees one frame at a time;
- **`compare --model`** made "the model is deterministic" a measurement;
- **`UI_LM_DEBUG_CARRIED=1`** lets the builder say why it carries a component, which is what four rounds of guessing
  needed;
- the reader's own **47** contradictions are **pinned** with their reason, not fixed.

Also: **`check-live-page` now asserts the viewer serves the recording's own words**, after an argument with my own
word filter that had reduced a nineteen-line screen to a single word.

Released pages-first for the seventh time.

## Exporting the builder's memory, attempted and reverted

The fix for the 47 is to let the builder hand its `first_seen` map to whoever judges provenance, so the judgement and
the build cannot disagree. Two attempts:

1. **thread the map through `page_with`** — the `appeared.insert` call lives in a helper one level down, so the new
   parameters were out of scope;
2. **record from the finished envelope** — closer, but splicing a variable into the middle of a large `Envelope {…}`
   construction with a scripted edit left the braces unbalanced.

Both reverted; the tree is unchanged and the 47 stay pinned.

**This is the third splicing failure this month** (rounds 114, 133, and here): scripted edits into the middle of a Rust
expression are exactly the kind of change the compiler cannot help with until it is already broken. The next attempt
should add the method *below* `page_with` — `fn remember(envelope: &Envelope, first_seen, frame_index)` — and call it
at the one site that already returns, rather than rewriting the return.

## The builder's own memory disagrees with its own pages — 48 places, not 47

Round 186 said the fix was to export the builder's `first_seen` map so the judgement could not disagree with the build.
This round built exactly that, in two careful steps (`pages_traced`, then `provenance` reading it), and the compiler
accepted every one.

The answer:

| judgement | contradictions |
|---|---|
| reconstructed from text (the old way) | **47** |
| read from the builder's own memory | **48** |

**One more, not fewer.** So the page's `source` is not simply "had this base been seen before this frame" — the
builder mutates `appeared` from more than one place (the pane branch inserts ids the page then renames), and its own
record does not round-trip.

Both files reverted; the corpus is back to 47 of a pinned 47.

What this buys is a much sharper question than before: **the builder's bookkeeping and the builder's output disagree
about the builder's own rule.** Nothing outside it can resolve that; the next instrument is inside `page_with`, where
`appeared` is written from two branches that do not agree about what an id is.

## The trace explains the 47: one memory, two representations

`UI_LM_TRACE` was already in the builder, and it answers the question in one run:

```
trace: reader 30 line(s), 2 component(s) before the split branch, divider=false
```

over the recording that carries 30 of the 47 contradictions:

| frames | divider | what the page describes |
|---|---|---|
| **19** | true | two panes, each id a hash of its own padded half |
| **57** | false | one merged block, its id a hash of the whole screen's rows |

**The split toggles within a single recording**, and both branches write into the same `appeared` set. So a component
can be judged `carried` against a hash of a shape that is not on screen in that form — pane hashes and block hashes
live in one memory and mean different things.

That is the defect, stated in a sentence a fix can be written against: **either the two representations get separate
memories, or the id must stop depending on how the rows are cut.** The first is smaller; the second is correct.

Nothing changed in the repository this round — the trace already existed, and running it was the whole job. The
provenance ceiling stays pinned at 47 and now says *why*.

**Six rounds on this, and the last one cost nothing but a command.** The instrument existed since round 60; what was
missing was remembering that it did.

## Three surgical hypotheses, none of them moves the number

| hypothesis | change | contradictions |
|---|---|---|
| the checker compares text, the builder ids | judge by id | 47 → **47** |
| the builder's memory can be exported | `pages_traced` | 47 → **48** |
| the plain-pane id hashes padding | hash the trimmed half | 47 → **47** |

All reverted. Three changes to three plausible causes, and the number did not move except once, upward.

**That is a result, though not the one I wanted**: the forty-seven are not in the plain-text pane path, which is the
one whose ids I could see. They are in the **structured-pane path**, which recurses into `page_from_lines` and hands
the same `appeared` set to two recursive builds — left and right — in that order. A left pane and a right pane that
hold the same words will therefore be judged against each other, and one of them will be called carried.

The next attempt should test exactly that: two panes with identical text in one frame. If the right one is marked
carried while its words were never on screen before *in that pane*, that is the forty-seven, and the fix is to give
each pane its own memory rather than sharing one across the recursion.

## Found it: the checker counted a repeat within one frame as "earlier"

The experiment round 189 asked for: **two panes holding the same text in one frame**. The corpus has **63 such
frames**, and their components come out `carried and carried`.

That is the whole disagreement. `provenance` kept one running set of text, so the right pane's words looked like a
repeat of the left's *in the same frame*; the builder means "appeared in an **earlier frame**". Two definitions again,
but this time the checker's is the wrong one and the fix is small: a per-frame set, merged after the frame.

```
before  47 contradiction(s)      one running set
after   48 contradiction(s)      a per-frame set
```

**The number went up by one, and that is progress**: the checker now asks the right question, and forty-eight is how
many times the *builder* answers it wrongly — marking a same-frame duplicate carried when nothing had appeared in an
earlier frame.

The remaining fix is inside the builder: it needs the frame index, so that `carried` means "first seen before this
frame" rather than "seen at all". That is the threading two earlier attempts failed at, and it is now the only thing
left.

## The builder fix landed, built, and changed nothing — reverted

Round 190 named the last change: give the builder a per-frame set, so `carried` means "an **earlier** frame wrote
this" rather than "some frame did". It went in with the compiler's help, four signature edits and one call site at a
time, and it built cleanly.

The measurement:

| state | contradictions |
|---|---|
| checker corrected, builder unchanged (round 190) | 48 |
| checker corrected, builder per-frame | **48** |

**Unchanged.** So the same-frame duplicate is not where the forty-eight live either — or it is, and something else
compensates, which is the same as not knowing.

Reverted. The tree is at `b49975b`'s behaviour: checker corrected, builder unchanged, 48 pinned with their reason.

**Nine rounds on provenance, six reverts, and the honest score is one definition corrected and no defect fixed.** That
is a poor return by the standard this project holds itself to — but the alternative, a patch that moves a number
without explaining it, would have been worse: the pin would have looked healthy for a month and then failed on a
screen nobody had seen.

What the nine rounds did produce: `provenance` prints its subjects, the builder can explain itself under
`UI_LM_DEBUG_CARRIED`, the count is pinned so it cannot grow silently, and five candidate causes have been eliminated
with numbers. The file still says 48.

## A rejection whose numbers nobody else can check

ADR 0007 rejects a `Table` kind on the strength of two counts — "28 of 81" and "20 of 81" recordings matched a
column-alignment rule — and the document never says what the rule is. Re-measured in round 192 over today's corpus
with a careful reconstruction of it (gaps of two or more spaces shared by every non-blank line of a block, over at
least three rows):

```
2+ shared column boundaries: 1 of 97 recordings; worst eval/tui/wsl-top (6 runs)
```

**One, not twenty-eight.** The reconstruction is stricter than whatever was actually run, and without the predicate
written down there is no way to tell which is wrong.

So the ADR now says so in its own text:

> The rejection still stands on the qualitative argument — a survey's answer options align, and so does a `df` row — but
> **the number used to justify it is not checkable by anyone reading this file**. A measurement without its predicate
> is a memory of a measurement.

That is the ninth document this month found to be citing something it cannot show. The first eight were counts that
had drifted; this one is a count that was never reproducible in the first place, which is worse and rarer.

## `Table`, re-opened with the rule stated first — and the rule is inverted

The predicate, written before any measurement:

> three or more consecutive rows, same number of cells, cells separated by two or more spaces, at the same character
> offsets in every row, at least one cell numeric.

| recording | longest run | a table? |
|---|---|---|
| `tui/wsl-vim` | **34 rows** | no — vim's help rows |
| `apps/pip-list` | 31 rows | no — a package listing |
| `tui/linux-top` | 18 rows | no — a process monitor |
| `freebuff/second-agent-17` | 5 rows | no — the survey's options |
| **`tui/df-table`** | **0 rows** | **yes, and the rule misses it** |

43 of 97 recordings match; the largest matches are not tables; **the canonical table does not match at all**, because
`df`'s columns are separated by single spaces — its widths are fixed, not padded.

**The rule is not noisy, it is inverted** with respect to what it was meant to find. That is a stronger rejection than
ADR 0007 had, and it now rests on a predicate a reader can run in an afternoon.

Two conclusions, one of which is about the kind and one about the method:

- `Table` stays out of the catalogue, now for a reason with numbers and a predicate behind it;
- **stating the rule before measuring is what turned a memory into a refutation** — the original ADR had the same
  conclusion and none of the evidence.

## The measurement is now in the repository

Round 193's numbers came from a script in `%TEMP%`, which is the same defect this project keeps finding in its
documents one level down: **a number whose method dies with the session that produced it**. `scripts/measure-tables.py`
now holds the predicate and prints the table:

```
recordings: 97   holding a table run: 43
acceptance cases:
    0 row(s)  eval/tui/df-table
   18 row(s)  eval/tui/linux-top
   18 row(s)  cases/hard/wsl-vim-in-split
    5 row(s)  eval/freebuff/second-agent-17
```

Four lines, and the case for rejecting `Table` is visible in them: the canonical table scores zero, a process monitor
scores eighteen, and vim's help screen scores eighteen.

**A measurement that lives only in a report is a story; in a script it is a finding.** The ADR points at the script now,
so the next reader can disagree with the conclusion and check the arithmetic in the same minute.

## v0.28.0: every claim that was only a memory

```
every check passed in 1056.9s
```

Nine commits since v0.27.0, and all of them are about what a document may assert:

- **ADR 0007 re-opened and closed twice.** The alignment predicate is now written down before it is measured, lives in
  `scripts/measure-tables.py`, and **misses `df` while matching vim's help rows** — an inverted rule, which is a
  stronger rejection than the ADR ever had.
- **The reader's provenance contradictions are pinned at 48** with their reason, after five candidate causes were
  eliminated with numbers.
- **`provenance` prints its subjects**, and the builder explains itself under `UI_LM_DEBUG_CARRIED`.
- **The checker stopped counting a repeat within one frame as "earlier"** — 63 frames in this corpus hold two panes
  with identical text, and that definition was the reason the count never made sense.

Released pages-first for the eighth time.

## A new application, and a table the predicate does match

**PowerShell's own console** is now in the corpus — the first application here that is not a terminal UI at all but a
shell's formatting engine:

```
Name                        Id         WS
----                        --         --
DeepSeek Harness          4232 1346121728
Telegram                 12040 1295192064
```

`eval/apps/pwsh-table`: 2 frames, **100%**, floor 100%. It reads as two blocks, which is right — the output *is* text.

Two things follow.

- **A real table is now in the material**, with the `----` underline row that every `Format-Table` draws. That row is a
  signal the round-193 predicate does not use, and it matches this recording without it — 44 of 98 now.
- **The recursive-alignment question has a positive case at last.** `df` was the only table the predicate missed; this
  one it finds, and the underline is the reason a person would call it a table too.

The recording also cost two attempts for a mundane reason worth recording: `powershell -File script.ps1` refuses to run
under the default execution policy, so the second attempt passed the script with `-EncodedCommand`. That is the recipe
for any future PowerShell capture on this machine.

## A shell's error record, which nothing in the corpus had

PowerShell's `ErrorRecord` is the richest text any application here produces:

```
Get-Item : Cannot find path 'C:\nope\missing.txt' because it does not exist.
At line:1 char:7
+ try { Get-Item C:\nope\missing.txt -ErrorAction Stop } catch { $_ | O ...
+       ~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~~
    + CategoryInfo          : ObjectNotFound: (C:\nope\missing.txt:String) [Get-Item], ItemNotFound
    + FullyQualifiedErrorId : PathNotFound,Microsoft.PowerShell.Commands.GetItemCommand
```

`eval/apps/pwsh-error`: 2 frames, **100%**, floor 100%, two blocks. The reader calls it text — which is right, and is
worth recording precisely because the shape *looks* structured: an underline of `~`, a `+` gutter, key–value lines.
None of it is a kind, and none of it should be.

Two notes on producing it:

- the first attempt exited with `STATUS_CONTROL_C_EXIT` because the shell did not close its own session; adding
  `exit 0` fixed it, and that is the second recipe this application has cost;
- the recorder warned that one scripted keystroke went unsent, so the session is shorter than asked — harmless here,
  and printed rather than hidden.

Corpus: **99 recordings, 1,357 frames**, and `check-docs` caught the two counts that had to move.

## A hundred recordings, and the first REPL

```
Welcome to Node.js v24.21.0.
> 1 + 1
2
> ["a","b"].map(s => s.toUpperCase())
// [ 'A', 'B' ]
```

`eval/apps/node-repl` — nine frames, **100%**, floor 100%, nine blocks.

A REPL is a shape nothing else here has: a prompt (`> `), an echoed expression, a result, and Node's `//` prefix on
inspected values. The reader calls each line a block, which is right — **the prompt is not a kind, and a REPL is not
structural**. That is worth having in the material precisely because it looks like it could be.

**One hundred recordings.** The corpus has doubled its application count this week — PowerShell's tables, its error
records, and now a REPL — and every addition re-derives the numbers that describe it: the diff count, the kind floors,
the provenance ceiling, the evaluation-slice total, the handoff's paragraph.

Of the hundred, four are from another machine and three are from applications that draw structure. The vocabulary has
been asked about a REPL and answered "text", which is the answer a person would give.

## `cmd.exe`'s dialect, and a rule that had to learn the difference

```
 Volume in drive C has no label.
 Directory of C:\Windows\System32\drivers\etc
10/19/2026  01:24 PM               900 hosts
               6 File(s)         24,427 bytes
The system cannot find the path specified.
```

`eval/apps/cmd-dir` — 1 frame, **100%**, floor 100%: `dir`'s classic listing with its date/time/size columns and
`<DIR>` markers, followed by cmd's own error sentence. A real table in a third dialect.

It also broke a check, correctly and then incorrectly. `check-corpus` refuses a recording whose program **failed and
drew almost nothing** — and this one exited 1 with a single frame. But it drew fourteen lines:

```
cmd-dir: the program exited 1 and drew 1 frame(s)     ← the old rule
```

The rule's purpose was to catch a capture of a program that fell over and left a blank screen (round 76: a `less` that
exited 1 and drew nothing). So it now asks about **content**:

```
tmp-blank: the program exited 1 and drew 0 line(s)
a recording exists whose program failed and which holds almost nothing        (exit 1)
```

Proved by planting exactly that capture: a `cmd /c exit 1` with no output still trips, while a failing program that
drew a complete screen is kept as material.

**A rule that counts frames was measuring a proxy.** The thing it wanted was "almost nothing", and now it says so.

## A progress bar from a program that is not an agent

`Meter` has had one observed producer since the vocabulary existed — agents printing a budget. PowerShell's
`Write-Progress` is a second, and a different shape:

```
 Copying files
    2 of 5
    [ooooooooooooooooooooooooooooooooooo                                                  ]
```

`eval/apps/pwsh-progress` — six frames, **100%**, floor 100%, one block each. **The reader calls the bar text**, and
that is what the rule says it should: a bar is a percentage of a job, not a count against a limit, and `as_meter`
declines it. A person looking at that screen would not call the bar a meter either — it is a *progress* bar, which is
what the kind deliberately is not.

So the count stays at 258 meters from one producer, and the rule now has a counterexample from a second application
rather than an argument.

One oddity worth writing down: the recorder reports the process as terminated by a console control event even though
the script ends with `exit 0` and the last frame says `done`. The screen is complete and the exit code is the
recorder's, not the program's — the corpus check passes because it asks about content.

## A tree in a second application, with the same answer

```
Folder PATH listing
C:\USERS\MOREWEB\APPDATA\LOCAL\TEMP\UI-LM-TREEROOT
├───alpha
│   └───nested
├───beta
└───gamma
    └───deep
        └───deeper
```

`eval/apps/cmd-tree` — 1 frame, **100%**, floor 100%, **one block**.

Windows' `tree` draws exactly the glyphs the reader treats as box borders — `├───`, `└───`, `│` — and the reader calls the
whole thing **one block**, which is the same decision `cargo tree` got in round 21 and the same reason: a tree is one
listing, not one block per branch.

That decision was made from a single application. It now has a second, in a different dialect, drawing the same glyphs
in a different arrangement — and it does not move.

Corpus: **103 recordings, 1,374 frames**, ten applications if `tree` and `dir` are counted separately from their shell.

## The full suite failed, and the failure was a check that could not get material

```
1 of 20 check(s) failed in 1066.9s
  race: the recording held too few frames to truncate
```

`check-race` records a program that redraws while a viewer reads it. Its child printed a line every **150 ms** — exactly
the recorder's quiesce window — so the screen never counted as settled, and a suite running on a loaded machine
produced a session with **one frame**. The guard refused it rather than measuring nothing, which is what it is for.

The fix is a wider gap, and the reason is the quiesce window:

```
# A program that redraws every 400 ms … at 150 ms the screen never settled, and on a loaded machine the suite produced
# a session with one frame — which is not a race, it is a recording that never considered itself quiet.
```

It passes now, and the guard is unchanged: a session with one frame is still a failure.

**A check that records live material is a check with a dependency on the machine's mood**, and this is the second one
found this week (the diff check was the first). Both were fixed by giving the source more room, not by relaxing what
counts as enough.

## v0.29.0: the frontier was here all along

```
every check passed in 1065.5s
```

Six commits since v0.28.0, and they add **four applications** to a corpus that had known three:

- **PowerShell's `Format-Table`** — a real aligned table with the `----` underline every formatting engine draws;
- **PowerShell's `ErrorRecord`** — the richest text here, and correctly read as text;
- **Node's REPL** — a prompt, an echoed expression and a result, and the hundredth recording;
- **`cmd`'s `dir` and `tree`** — a third dialect's columns, and the same tree glyphs that `cargo tree` drew, with the
  same answer: one block.

Plus two check refinements that only material could have prompted: the corpus now asks a failing capture about its
**content** rather than its frame count, and the race check's child leaves the screen quiet for longer than the
recorder's quiesce window.

Released pages-first for the ninth time.

## The model card's first evidence row was a number without a subject

It read:

```
| curated corpus | 60 recordings, 343 frames, both builders | 100% agreement, no audit violations |
```

**"Curated corpus" is defined nowhere** — not in the glossary, not in the handoff, not in the state page — and nothing
derived the 60 or the 343. It is the same defect as ADR 0007's unreproducible counts, sitting in the document that asks
to be trusted most.

The row now names something that exists, and `check-docs` verifies it:

```
the card's evidence table names the corpus: 103 recordings, 1374 frames
```

Proved failable by quoting 60 again:

```
the card's evidence table describes a corpus that is not on this machine        (exit 1)
```

**A row that cannot be checked is a row that will be wrong**, and this one had been wrong for the whole month — the
corpus passed 343 frames somewhere around round 60 and the card never noticed.

## The ledger was missing a check, and the count said otherwise

The ledger lists every check with the failure it printed. Audited against the folder:

```
in folder: 22   in ledger: 20
missing from the ledger: check-everything, check-release
```

`check-release` — the check that verifies a published archive — had **never been listed**, and the equality that hid it
was an accident: the count compared every backticked row, so `gate.ps1`'s row stood in for the missing check and the two
numbers matched.

Both halves are fixed:

- `check-release` has a row, with a failure it actually printed — asking for `v0.0.0` gives
  **"the release had no Windows archive"**;
- the count now looks only at rows naming a check, and the folder excludes the runner — so the two numbers mean the
  same thing for the first time.

`check-everything` stays out of the ledger by design and says so in the count: **the runner is not a check**, and
counting it as one is the mistake the comment at that line already recorded from three earlier attempts.

**An accidental equality is worse than a mismatch**: it reads as proof and hides exactly the omission it should catch.

## "Nine applications" was a hand count, and it was wrong

The handoff described the corpus as coming from "nine applications". Nothing derived it, and the recordings have
carried a `command` field since the first one — so the honest sentence is a **derived** one:

```
the handoff's command count matches the recordings: 11
```

Eleven distinct commands, not nine applications. The difference is the word: "application" means whatever the reader
imagines (`sh -c 'top …'` is `top`? `sh`? both?), while "command" is what the file records and what a check can count.

**The number I first wrote was 22**, from parsing programs out of arguments — a more interesting number and a definition
that no file supports. The check refused it, which is the whole point of stating the definition before the count.

Fixed this round (13th document defect): the sentence now says eleven, and `check-docs` derives it from every
`session.json` on the machine.

## "Two machines" was the last unverifiable phrase in that paragraph

The handoff's corpus paragraph said "from 11 distinct commands **and two machines**". The eleven is derived; the two is
not — nothing on this machine can show a recording made somewhere else, and the sentence went on to describe the GitHub
runner in a way that could easily be read as part of the count.

It now says:

> **Every one of those was recorded on this machine.** A GitHub runner records three shapes of its own — a listing, a
> process monitor and a whiptail dialog — so the reader is measured away from this host, but those recordings live in CI
> rather than here, and the numbers above say nothing about them.

**A paragraph that mixes a derived number with an underived one lends the second the authority of the first.** The fix
isn't to delete the CI claim — it's to put it where its evidence is, which is the second-machine job rather than a
corpus total.

## Two headings counted their own lists, and both were wrong

```
model card: "what it cannot do" lists 8 item(s); the review says four
review:     "five defects"      lists 8 numbered item(s)
```

Neither number was ever derived, and both had drifted by four items. The fix is not to correct them to eight — it is to
**stop counting in prose**:

- the review's heading is now "The defects worth remembering";
- its table row says "and what it cannot do".

A count in a heading is a claim with no reader: nobody checks it, and it becomes false the moment a bullet is added.
Where a number is worth stating, this project derives it; where it isn't, the sentence should not carry one.

That is the fourteenth document defect and the first one fixed by **removing** a claim rather than verifying it — which
is the right answer when the claim's only purpose was rhetorical.

## The viewer could serve the model's pages, and nobody had asked it to

`ui-lm-serve` has taken `--model` for weeks. `check-html` draws model pages to a file and compares them; **the live
viewer had only ever been asked for the reader's**. The path existed and was untested — a feature with no evidence, which
this project counts as a liability rather than an asset.

`check-live-page` now starts a third viewer against the recording it already has:

```
the model's page is served live too: 5213 bytes, every word present
```

Failable by demanding a word that page does not hold:

```
the model's served document is missing 19 word(s), such as tick        (exit 1)
```

No new capture was needed — the recording was already finished, so the cost is one more process and three seconds.

**A flag that exists everywhere else is a path nobody notices is missing** — the project wrote that sentence when `render`
grew `--model`, and this is the same defect one layer up: the flag was there, and the check wasn't.

## The one command no check ran

Audited every CLI command against the suite: all but one were exercised. **`disagree` was not** — and it is the command
that produces the numbers the model card quotes.

It now runs inside `check-all`, once per population, because one mixture number hides both:

```
model vs reader, unseen    3.5% of rows differ (ceiling 15%)
model vs reader, freebuff 21.4% of rows differ (ceiling 30%)
```

The ceilings come from the card: 2% to 10% on the training application, 18% to 26% on the second agent. Proved failable
by pinning 1%:

```
disagree (unseen): 3.5% of rows differ, and the pinned ceiling is 1%        (exit 1)
```

**The first version measured the mixture and reported 45.5%** — a number that is true and tells nobody anything, which
is how this check nearly shipped a ceiling covering two populations a factor of six apart.

One self-inflicted bug on the way: my loop reused the variable `$line`, which the row below it prints, so the assembly
line started reporting the disagree rate. The suite caught it in the next run, which is the argument for adding a check
to the thing that prints.

## v0.30.0: the last untested surface

```
every check passed in 1133.6s
```

Seven commits since v0.29.0, and every one closed a gap between a capability and its evidence:

- **the live viewer serves the model's pages**, and `check-live-page` asks it to — the flag had existed for weeks with
  nothing testing it;
- **`disagree` runs in the suite**, once per population, with ceilings from the model card — the last CLI command no
  check exercised;
- **the race check's child** leaves the screen quiet longer than the recorder's quiesce window, after a loaded suite
  produced one frame;
- **the ledger lists `check-release`**, and the count that hid the omission now counts checks rather than rows;
- **the model card's corpus row** is derived, after a month quoting "60 recordings, 343 frames" for a corpus defined
  nowhere;
- **the handoff's command count** is derived from the `command` field instead of remembering "nine applications".

Released pages-first for the tenth time.

## The model is worst where the pages look easiest

Measured `disagree` over every evaluation set — the first time the per-set table has been written down:

| set | non-blank rows that differ |
|---|---|
| `unseen` (Grok, untrained tasks) | **3.5%** |
| `tui` | 10.6% |
| `apps` (PowerShell, cmd, node, git…) | 13.3% |
| `freebuff` (second agent) | 21.4% |
| `workflow` | **45.5%** |
| `structured` | **55.3%** |

The gradient is backwards from intuition. The model is at its best on **unseen Grok tasks** — rows it has never seen but
whose *shape* it was trained on — and at its worst on plain shell output, where half its labels are wrong.

The example says why: on `cargo test` output it calls

```
tests::it_adds --- FAILED
```

a **`Diff`**, because it learned `---` as a diff marker and those rows hold nothing else. There is no diff on that
screen; there is a failing test.

So the two worst sets are the two where a text-only converter already reproduces the page perfectly — **the guards are
the only reason those pages are right**. That is the second agent's conclusion arriving from the opposite direction:
what generalises is the assembly, and where the assembly does no work, the model is visibly wrong.

`apps` at **13.3%** is the number worth watching: it is the set that has doubled this week, and it sits between the
training application and an unfamiliar agent.

## The gradient is guarded, not just measured

Round 211 wrote down six disagreement rates. A rate written in a report is a snapshot; the suite now pins one ceiling
per set:

```
model vs reader, unseen     3.5% (ceiling 15%)
model vs reader, tui       10.6% (ceiling 20%)
model vs reader, apps      13.3% (ceiling 25%)
model vs reader, freebuff  21.4% (ceiling 30%)
model vs reader, workflow  45.5% (ceiling 60%)
model vs reader, structured 55.3% (ceiling 65%)
```

Each ceiling sits a little above its measurement, so drift is caught and ordinary noise is not. Proved failable by pinning
the worst set at 20%:

```
disagree (structured): 55.3% of rows differ, and the pinned ceiling is 20%        (exit 1)
```

**The two high ceilings are the interesting ones.** They say, in the suite's own output, that on plain shell screens the
model is wrong about half the time and the guards are what keep the pages right — a fact that would otherwise live only
in prose, and that will now announce itself if it changes in either direction.

## Two of the six ceilings were formalities, and are gone

Round 212 gave every set a disagreement ceiling. That was right for four of them and wrong for two:

```
model vs reader, unseen     3.5% (ceiling 15%)
model vs reader, tui       10.6% (ceiling 20%)
model vs reader, apps      13.3% (ceiling 25%)
model vs reader, freebuff  21.4% (ceiling 30%)
model vs reader, workflow  45.5% (reported, no ceiling)
model vs reader, structured 55.3% (reported, no ceiling)
```

**A ceiling that permits being wrong about half the time is a formality**, and a formality that fails is worse than a
number that is printed: it would fail on noise and pass on a real regression from 55% to 64%. What actually guards those
two sets is that their **pages** are 100%, which `score` measures on every run and which no model change has ever moved.

So `workflow` and `structured` are reported and uncapped, with the reason in the script beside them. The four that mean
something are still enforced, and still proven failable:

```
disagree (apps): 13.3% of rows differ, and the pinned ceiling is 1%        (exit 1)
```

**A threshold is a promise that crossing it is worth stopping for.** Beyond a certain distance from zero, the promise is
just decoration — and this project has already removed one kind of decoration this week.

## Two hard cases had no ceiling, so only their score was checking them

A ceiling is what says "this screen is a handful of components, not one per row". Measured every case against its
ceiling in round 214: thirteen had one, and **the two newest — `whiptail-menu` and `vim-completion-popup` — had no entry
at all**, so the comparison was skipped for them and only `score` was looking.

Both read as **one component**, and that is the judgement worth protecting: a popup or a menu read as one component per
row would still score 100% and would still be wrong.

```
whiptail-menu          busiest frame holds 1 components, the ceiling is 0        (exit 1)
```

Proved by setting the ceiling below the value. Fifteen cases, fifteen ceilings.

**A case is not complete when it passes; it is complete when the thing it protects has a failure attached.** These two
were added in rounds 141 and 137 with expectations and required kinds, and neither had the number that would notice the
reader losing its grip on them.

## The case that exists for a kind never asserted it

`whiptail-menu` was added in round 137 because a centred dialog is a `Frame`. Its score protects the agreement, its
ceiling protects the count of components — and **nothing protected the kind**, because the `$required` table never listed
it.

It does now, and the failure is the one that matters:

```
whiptail-menu          no Diff component, and this case exists for it        (exit 1)
```

Ten of fifteen cases now name the kind they exist for; the other five exist for shape rather than kind (a split, a blank
pane) and say so by their ceilings alone.

**Three tables, three ways to lose a case silently**: score says the pages agree, ceiling says how many pieces, required
says which kind. A case with only two of them is a case that can rot in the third direction, and each of the last two
rounds found one rotting in a different table.

## Two shell screens now forbid the kinds only agents draw

`$forbidden` had one entry — the borderless popup that must never be a `Frame`. It now also covers the two cases that
are **nothing but shell output**:

```
"pane-resize"        = @("Meter", "Thought", "ToolCall", "Diff", "Frame")
"agent-beside-shell" = @("Diff", "Frame")
```

A reader that invents a meter here has stopped reading the screen — and **its score and ceiling would both survive
that**, because two blocks of made-up kinds agree with themselves perfectly. Proved by forbidding a kind the screen does
hold:

```
pane-resize            holds a Block, and this screen is the counterexample for it        (exit 1)
```

`agent-beside-shell` forbids only `Diff` and `Frame` because its right-hand pane really does carry an agent's meter and
tool calls — the boundary is where the agent is, not where the shell is.

**A forbidden list is a claim about what cannot appear**, and it is the only guard that survives a page being coherently
wrong: score, ceiling and required all pass when the reader invents structure consistently.

## The table screens are text, and moving them would have weakened the set

`structured` holds six screens of `git diff` output and sits at a **55.6% floor** — a converter reproduces half of it,
so half of what the set proves is about kinds. The three new table screens (`pwsh-table`, `cmd-dir`, `cmd-tree`) look
like they belong there by name, so I moved them and measured:

| arrangement | floor |
|---|---|
| `structured` as it was (6 screens) | **55.6%** |
| with the three table screens (9) | **70.4%** |

**Higher, not lower.** The table screens are almost entirely reproducible by a text converter — `dir` and `tree` are
text, and `Format-Table` is text with padding — so adding them makes the set prove *less* about the vocabulary, not
more.

Reverted. The three stay in `apps`, where a 94.6% floor is already the point: that set is about applications being read
correctly, not about kinds being found.

**A better floor is a lower one, and a set's name is not evidence about what it holds.** The measurement took one
command and would have taken a paragraph to argue.
